Both pressure drops are single-digit-to-low-teens kPa --- deliberately modest, so the
loop-pump penalty stays small and the free-cooling COP benefit is preserved. The exchanger
meets the 100\,\% design-load free-cooling requirement of ASHRAE~90.1~\S6.5.1 at
OAT~$= 4$\,\textdegree C.

% ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
\chapter{Dry Cooler (Outdoor Air-Cooled Heat Rejection)}
\label{ch:dry}
% ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

\zonedivider{Part A \quad---\quad Thermal Sizing, Vendor Selection, and Fan Operation}

% ── §5.1 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Design Basis and Role}

The dry cooler is the final heat-rejection step of the glycol loop: an outdoor,
finned-tube, air-cooled heat exchanger with variable-speed axial fans that rejects heat
from the 30\,\% propylene-glycol (PG) loop directly to ambient air. It operates at two
qualitatively different duties depending on the mode of the plant:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Mechanical mode.} The compressor is running; the dry cooler rejects both
        the evaporator's refrigerating effect \emph{and} the compressor's shaft work,
        received from the condenser (Chapter~\ref{ch:cond}) as hot glycol at
        46\,\textdegree C.
  \item \textbf{Free-cooling mode.} The compressor is off; the dry cooler instead supplies
        cold glycol directly to the economizer (Chapter~\ref{ch:econ}), rejecting only the
        bare 150\,kW IT/cooling load with no compressor-work addition.
\end{itemize}

These are sized and checked as two \emph{separate} operating points on the \emph{same}
physical coil --- a single piece of hardware, evaluated twice.

\subsection{Why a Different Method Than the Plate Heat Exchangers}
\label{dry:sec:whydifferent}

The evaporator, condenser, and economizer (Chapters~\ref{ch:evap}--\ref{ch:econ}) are all
single-pass, counter-flow, chevron-plate heat exchangers, sized by LMTD and evaluated with
the Han, Lee \& Kim (2003) two-phase or Martin (1996) single-phase chevron-plate friction
correlations. None of that carries over to the dry cooler, because it is a fundamentally
different exchanger type:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Flow arrangement.} Air crosses a finned round-tube bundle
        \emph{perpendicular} to the glycol flow, over several tube rows --- a
        \textbf{cross-flow} arrangement, not counter-current. A single LMTD systematically
        mis-predicts duty for this geometry.
  \item \textbf{Geometry.} Both plate-friction correlations are regressed against chevron
        \emph{plates}; neither applies to air flowing over finned \emph{round tubes}.
  \item \textbf{A fan, not a pump, drives the air side}, and it is variable-speed --- a new
        degree of freedom (and a new energy term for the facility PUE) that the plate
        exchangers do not have.
\end{itemize}

This chapter therefore uses the \textbf{effectiveness--NTU method} (cross-flow, both fluids
unmixed) for the thermal duty, \textbf{fan affinity laws} for the variable-speed fan power,
and (Part~B) a \textbf{vendor-anchored scaling method} for the glycol-side pressure drop
rather than an independent friction correlation.

% ── §5.2 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Governing Method: Cross-Flow Effectiveness--NTU}
\label{dry:sec:epsntu}

For a cross-flow exchanger with both fluids unmixed, the standard approximation
(Incropera, \emph{Fundamentals of Heat and Mass Transfer}) is:

\begin{equation}
  \varepsilon = 1 - \exp\left\{\frac{1}{C_r}\,NTU^{0.22}
  \left[\exp\!\left(-C_r\,NTU^{0.78}\right) - 1\right]\right\},
  \qquad C_r = \frac{C_{\min}}{C_{\max}}
  \tag{$\varepsilon$-NTU}
  \label{eq:epsntu}
\end{equation}

with $C = \mdot\,c_p$ the capacity rate of each stream. \textbf{Sizing} (mechanical-mode
design point, \S\ref{dry:sec:mechdesign}) uses Eq.~\eqref{eq:epsntu} \emph{backward}: the
required $\varepsilon$ is computed from the duty and terminal temperatures, and $NTU$ ---
which cannot be isolated algebraically from Eq.~\eqref{eq:epsntu} --- is obtained by
numerical root-finding (\texttt{scipy.optimize.brentq}), after which $UA = NTU\cdot C_{\min}$.
\textbf{Off-design checking} (free-cooling point, \S\ref{dry:sec:freecool}) uses the same
equation \emph{forward}: holding $UA$ fixed (scaled for reduced air flow), a second
root-finding search finds the air mass flow that reproduces a target duty at new terminal
temperatures. Both uses are implemented in \texttt{dry\_cooler.py}
(\texttt{size\_design\_point()} and \texttt{predict\_off\_design()} respectively).

% ── §5.3 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Mechanical-Mode Design Point}
\label{dry:sec:mechdesign}

\subsection{Required Duty}

The dry cooler must reject both the IT/cooling load and the compressor's shaft work:

\[
  Q_{\mathrm{cond}} = Q_{\mathrm{evap}} + W_{\mathrm{comp}}
  = 150\,\mathrm{kW} + W_{\mathrm{comp}}
\]

The compressor model (Chapter~\ref{ch:comp}) supplies the real shaft work at the design
point, $W_{\mathrm{comp}} = 32.1$\,kW, so the heat the dry cooler must reject is
\[
  Q_{\mathrm{cond}} = 150 + 32.1 = \mathbf{182.1\;kW}.
\]
The \textbf{selected} unit is rated at 196.5\,kW at this duty's 42/38\,\textdegree C glycol
and 35\,\textdegree C air (\S\ref{dry:sec:datasheet}), an 8\,\% capacity margin over the
182\,kW required.

\subsection{Design-Point Inputs and Sizing Result}

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Dry cooler mechanical-mode design point (selected Kelvion ULF-PA106K4V-091F095)
and \texttt{size\_design\_point()} result.}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLl}
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Value} & \textbf{Basis} \\
\midrule
Unit rated duty, $Q$                 & 196.5\,kW                     & Vendor rating (\S\ref{dry:sec:datasheet}); 182\,kW required \\
Glycol inlet\,/\,outlet              & 42.0\,/\,38.0\,\textdegree C   & Condenser outlet (Ch.~\ref{ch:cond}) \\
Ambient design DB                    & 35.0\,\textdegree C            & Project spec \\
Secondary fluid                      & 30\,\% propylene glycol        & \texttt{INCOMP::MPG[0.30]} \\
Air-side temperature rise, $\Delta T_{\mathrm{air}}$ & 3.72\,K        & \derived\ --- from vendor air outlet, \S\ref{dry:sec:datasheet} \\
\midrule
Glycol mass flow, $\mdot_{\mathrm{gly}}$  & 12.56\,kg/s              & Energy balance \\
Air mass flow, $\mdot_{\mathrm{air}}$     & 52.47\,kg/s (164{,}848\,m$^3$/h) & Energy balance \\
$C_{\min}$\,/\,$C_{\max}$                 & 49.12\,/\,52.82\,kW\,K$^{-1}$ & Glycol is $C_{\min}$ \\
$C_r$                                     & 0.930                    & $C_{\min}/C_{\max}$ \\
Required effectiveness, $\varepsilon$     & 0.571                    & Eq.~\eqref{eq:epsntu}, inverse \\
$NTU$                                     & 1.500                    & Root-found (\texttt{brentq}) \\
\textbf{Required $UA$}                    & \textbf{73.67\,kW\,K$^{-1}$} & $= NTU \times C_{\min}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\noindent The model's air flow (164{,}848\,m$^3$/h) runs $+3.5\,\%$ above the vendor's
159{,}337\,m$^3$/h at this point --- the same air-side agreement discussed in
\S\ref{dry:sec:validation}. The 3.72\,K air rise is the vendor's, not a free design choice:
it follows directly from the selected unit's reported air outlet temperature.

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Rated flow vs.\ loop flow.} The 12.56\,kg/s above is the glycol flow at the unit's
full 196.5\,kW \emph{rating}, which fixes its $UA$. In service the loop actually carries
\textbf{11.64\,kg/s} --- the 182\,kW condenser duty over the 4\,K glycol range
(Chapter~\ref{ch:cond}) --- and the coil runs at 90.7\,\% fan, leaving 8\,\% capacity
margin. \textbf{11.64\,kg/s is the loop flow used throughout the condenser, glycol-pump
and piping chapters}; the 12.56\,kg/s appears only here, as the unit's rating basis.
\end{quote}

% ── §5.4 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Free-Cooling Performance Check}
\label{dry:sec:freecool}

ASHRAE~90.1-2022~\S6.5.1 requires the free-cooling path to carry 100\,\% of the design
cooling load at the qualifying outdoor condition. Holding the \emph{same} $UA$ (scaled for
reduced air flow via $h_{\mathrm{air}} \sim \mdot_{\mathrm{air}}^{0.6}$, a standard
Reynolds-power approximation for finned tube banks with air-side resistance dominant),
\texttt{predict\_off\_design()} solves for the air flow that delivers the free-cooling
design duty:

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Free-cooling check: same physical coil, opposite operating point.}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lL}
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Value} \\
\midrule
Target duty, $Q$                    & 150\,kW (bare IT load, no compressor) \\
Glycol inlet (from economizer)      & 15\,\textdegree C \\
Ambient DB                          & 4\,\textdegree C (ASHRAE~90.1 full-free-cooling threshold) \\
\midrule
Air mass flow required              & 16.95\,kg/s ($=32.3\,\%$ of design air flow) \\
Fan speed required                  & $\approx$32.3\,\% \\
Glycol leaving temperature (model)  & 11.89\,\textdegree C \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

The coil sized for the hot/mechanical duty meets the cold/free-cooling duty at only
$\approx$32\,\% fan speed --- and by the cube law that is roughly 3\,\% of rated fan power.
The ASHRAE~90.1 requirement is satisfied without a separate, dedicated free-cooling coil,
and the low fan speed is a direct benefit of the large re-selected unit.

\subsection{Open Item: Glycol Flow Assumption in Free-Cooling Mode}
\label{dry:sec:openflow}

The model above holds the glycol mass flow \emph{fixed} at the mechanical-mode design value
(12.56\,kg/s, a fixed-speed glycol pump assumption), so in free-cooling mode the glycol only
cools by $\approx$3.1\,K (15\,$\to$\,11.9\,\textdegree C) rather than the full 6\,K span
(15\,$\to$\,9\,\textdegree C) assumed in the economizer's own design basis
(Chapter~\ref{ch:econ}, \S\ref{econ:sec:cascade}), which uses a glycol flow of
6.52\,kg/s. \textbf{These two chapters currently disagree on the free-cooling glycol
flow rate}, and this should be reconciled before the plant design is finalised --- either
by specifying a variable-speed glycol pump (matching the economizer's 6.52\,kg/s target
in free-cooling mode and 12.56\,kg/s in mechanical mode), or by re-running the dry cooler
free-cooling check at the economizer's 6.52\,kg/s flow instead of the fixed design value.
This is flagged here rather than silently resolved, since the correct answer depends on a
glycol-pump control-strategy decision that has not yet been made elsewhere in the report.

% ── §5.5 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Vendor Selection: Kelvion Select RT}
\label{dry:sec:vendor}

\subsection{Selection Tool and Inputs}

Unlike the evaporator and condenser (selected via Kelvion Select PHE for brazed plate
heat exchangers), the dry cooler was selected via \textbf{Kelvion Select RT}
(formerly ``GPC''), Kelvion's configurator for condensers, dry coolers, gas coolers, and
air coolers. Using the same vendor family as the plate exchangers keeps the plant on a
single manufacturer for procurement and spares commonality.

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Kelvion Select RT capacity-requirement inputs (mechanical-mode design point).}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lL}
\toprule
\textbf{Field} & \textbf{Value entered} \\
\midrule
Fluid                & Propylene Glycol, 30.0\,\%\,vol \\
Capacity (active input) & 620\,MBH ($\approx$182\,kW condenser duty) \\
Fluid inlet\,/\,outlet & 107.6\,\textdegree F\,/\,100.4\,\textdegree F ($=42.0/38.0$\,\textdegree C) \\
Calculation mode     & Fixed fluid temperatures \\
Ambient              & 95.0\,\textdegree F ($=35.0$\,\textdegree C) \\
Altitude             & 699\,ft ($\approx$213\,m, Champaign, IL elevation) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\noindent The glycol temperatures are the binding constraint, and they are not free: the
10\,K air-to-refrigerant approach that the COP target requires
(\S\ref{perf:sec:revision}) allots only 3\,K to the dry cooler and 4\,K to the glycol
rise, which fixes 42/38\,\textdegree C against 35\,\textdegree C air. \textbf{A 3\,K
approach on an air-cooled coil is demanding}, and it — not the headline duty — is what
drives the size of the machine selected below.

\subsection{Selection Notes}

Two points in the selection process are worth recording for reproducibility. First, the
Select RT capacity screen has two mutually exclusive driving inputs (``Capacity'' and
``Volume flow rate''); an initial run left the flow-rate radio active, silently returning
undersized results ($\approx$172--181\,kW instead of the 190\,kW target). Re-selecting the
Capacity radio and re-running corrected this. Second, the tool defaults to Ethylene Glycol;
this was corrected to Propylene Glycol (30\,\%\,vol) to match the project's secondary
fluid.

\subsection{Why Headline Capacity Is the Wrong Screening Criterion}
\label{dry:sec:capacity_trap}

Before the candidate table, one point governs how it must be read. \textbf{A dry
cooler's catalog capacity is meaningless without the condition it is quoted at}, and the
condition here is unusually hard.

Consider a representative \texttt{ULF} unit rated \textbf{213.2\,kW} at a comfortable
46/40\,\textdegree C glycol duty --- an 11\,K mean air-to-glycol difference against
35\,\textdegree C air. Re-rate that same coil at this project's 42/38\,\textdegree C
requirement and the driving difference collapses to 7\,K, a 36\,\% reduction. Since duty
scales with it, the same physical machine delivers only $\approx$146\,kW --- \emph{well
short} of the $\approx$182\,kW required, despite a headline rating 17\,\% above it:

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{The same coil, two conditions. Capacity is not a property of the machine alone.}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLLL}
\toprule
\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Glycol} & \textbf{Mean $\Delta T$ to 35\,\textdegree C air} & \textbf{Duty} \\
\midrule
Comfortable rating & 46\,$\to$\,40\,\textdegree C & 11\,K & 213.2\,kW \\
This project       & 42\,$\to$\,38\,\textdegree C & 7\,K ($-36$\,\%) & $\approx$145.7\,kW \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\noindent There is no way to buy back the difference with air flow: pushing the glycol
outlet to 40\,\textdegree C --- a 5\,K approach --- would pinch the condenser and undo
the 10\,K cascade the COP target depends on. The approach is thermodynamically forced by
\S\ref{perf:sec:revision}, so \textbf{the coil must simply be bigger}.

The condition-independent measure is \textbf{surface area}, and that is what the
screening below is really about. Expect the selected unit to have a \emph{lower} headline
MBH than units quoted at easier conditions while being a substantially larger machine.

\subsection{Candidate Comparison}

Select RT returned a results table of qualifying \texttt{ULF} flatbed units at the
620\,MBH / 42/38\,\textdegree C duty. They were screened on the criteria that actually
bind --- capacity margin at the \emph{real} condition, fan-speed headroom, and sound:

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Candidate screening at the 42/38\,\textdegree C duty (620\,MBH required;
European decimal notation converted).}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLLLLL}
\toprule
\textbf{Type} & \textbf{MBH (margin)} & \textbf{$\Delta P$} & \textbf{Fan speed} & \textbf{$L_{pA}$} & \textbf{Note} \\
\midrule
ULF-PA106Y4V-096Z110 & 639.5 (3\,\%) & 4\,psi & 1100/1100 & 64\,dB(A) & Low $\Delta P$, but \textbf{no fan headroom} \\
ULF-PA107Y4V-091Z100 & 630.8 (2\,\%) & 4\,psi & 1000/1100 & 67\,dB(A) & Low $\Delta P$, thin margin, loud \\
\textbf{ULF-PA106K4V-091F095} & \textbf{670.4 (8\,\%)} & 8\,psi & \textbf{950/1050} & \textbf{60\,dB(A)} & \textbf{Selected} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\noindent\textbf{ULF-PA106K4V-091F095 is selected.} At \textbf{60\,dB(A)} it is the
quietest candidate by 4\,dB, which matters for a data-centre site where the unit may sit
near occupied space. It carries the best capacity margin (8\,\%) and, decisively,
\textbf{10\,\% fan-speed headroom} (950 of 1050\,rpm). That headroom was preferred over
the lower-$\Delta P$ ``Z110'' variants precisely \emph{because} those run 1100/1100 ---
flat out at the design point, with no reserve for a hotter-than-design hour, coil
fouling, or a failed fan. The penalty accepted in exchange is a higher glycol-side
pressure drop (8\,psi vs.\ 4\,psi), paid by the glycol pump
(\S\ref{perf:sec:pue}).

% ── §5.6 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

\label{dry:sec:datasheet}

\noindent With this unit the annual simulation runs \textbf{clean across all 8{,}760

\begin{quote}
\noindent\textbf{Open item --- full datasheet not yet pulled.} The values above are what
the Select RT \emph{results table} reports. A full configured datasheet (dimensions,
weight, fin spacing, materials, and above all \textbf{fan power}) has not been generated
for this unit, which is why the fan power carried through the PUE is a fan-law estimate
rather than a vendor figure (\S\ref{dry:sec:fanpower_open}). This is the largest

% ── §5.7 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Model Validation Against a Vendor Datasheet}
\label{dry:sec:validation}

The $\varepsilon$-NTU model of \S\ref{dry:sec:mechdesign} carries the entire annual
simulation --- every fan-speed and fan-power figure in this report comes out of it --- so
it is worth checking against Kelvion's own thermodynamic calculation rather than trusting
it on faith.

The check uses a \textbf{full configured datasheet for a different unit of the same
\texttt{ULF} family} (ULF-PA104Y4V-096Z100, rated 213.23\,kW at 46/40\,\textdegree C
glycol). That unit is not the one selected --- it cannot meet this project's 3\,K
approach (\S\ref{dry:sec:capacity_trap}) --- but that is irrelevant here: validating a
model needs a complete, self-consistent vendor calculation to compare against, and this
is the datasheet for which all streams are reported. Feeding its duty and its real
air-side rise (5.56\,K) into the model:

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Effectiveness-NTU model prediction vs.\ a full Kelvion Select RT datasheet
(ULF-PA104Y4V-096Z100, 46/40\,\textdegree C glycol, 35\,\textdegree C air).}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLLl}
\toprule
\textbf{Quantity} & \textbf{Model} & \textbf{Vendor} & \textbf{Difference} \\
\midrule
Air volume flow    & 124{,}097\,m$^3$/h & 122{,}071\,m$^3$/h & $+1.7\,\%$ \\
Glycol volume flow & 8.81\,L/s          & 8.884\,L/s         & $-0.9\,\%$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

Agreement within $\pm 2\,\%$ on \emph{both} streams is a strong validation of the
cross-flow $\varepsilon$-NTU approach for this application, given that the model uses no
fitted constants beyond the standard Incropera correlation --- everything else (duty,
temperatures, fluid properties) is a direct input.

\noindent\textbf{Cross-check on the selected unit.} Running the same model at the
selected ULF-PA106K4V-091F095's own design point (196.5\,kW, 42/38\,\textdegree C glycol,
$\Delta T_{\mathrm{air}} = 3.72$\,K) predicts an air volume flow of
$\approx$164{,}800\,m$^3$/h against the vendor's 159{,}337\,m$^3$/h --- \textbf{$+3.5\,\%$}.
Looser than the $\pm2\,\%$ above, and honestly so: only the air side can be checked,
because the Select RT results table does not report the selected unit's glycol volume
flow. The direction is conservative (the model asks for slightly \emph{more} air than the
vendor says is needed, so it over-estimates fan duty rather than under-estimating it), and
both checks should be repeated once the full datasheet is pulled.

% ── §5.8 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Fan Operation, Variable-Speed Control, and Annual Energy}
\label{dry:sec:fans}

\subsection{Affinity-Law Fan Power}

The selected unit's 4 EC (electronically commutated) fans are natively variable-speed,
matching the fan-speed-modulation control strategy assumed throughout this chapter. Fan
power follows the standard affinity laws:

\[
  \dot V \propto N, \qquad \Delta p \propto N^2, \qquad P_{\mathrm{fan}} \propto N^3
\]

so that turning the fans down to the 32.3\,\% speed found in \S\ref{dry:sec:freecool}
draws only $\approx 0.323^3 \approx 3\,\%$ of rated power --- the physical basis for why
variable-speed fan control materially improves annual facility PUE relative to fixed-speed
operation.

\subsection{Annual Free-Cooling Fan Energy (Champaign, IL, TMY3)}

Using the real 8{,}760-hour Champaign TMY3 weather file (WMO~725315), each hour with
outdoor dry-bulb temperature $\leq 4$\,\textdegree C was passed through
\texttt{predict\_off\_design()} at the free-cooling condition (150\,kW, glycol
15\,\textdegree C inlet), with fan power scaled by the cube law off the selected unit's
10.6\,kW rating:

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Annual free-cooling fan energy, real 8{,}760-hour TMY3 data (selected unit,
cube-law fan power).}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lL}
\toprule
\textbf{Quantity} & \textbf{Value} \\
\midrule
Full free-cooling hours (OAT $\leq 4$\,\textdegree C)  & 2{,}542\,hrs\,/\,yr (29.0\,\% of the year) \\
Fan speed range across these hours                     & 6.9\,\%\,--\,32.3\,\% \\
Total free-cooling fan energy                           & \textbf{270\,kWh/yr} \\
Average fan power in this mode                          & 0.11\,kW \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

The large re-selected coil rejects 150\,kW in free cooling at only 6.9--32.3\,\% fan
speed, so by the cube law the free-cooling fan energy is very small ---
\textbf{270\,kWh/yr}, or about 0.11\,kW on average. This is the reportable figure for the
free-cooling contribution to the annual PUE; the 10.6\,kW rating it scales from is itself
an estimate (\S\ref{dry:sec:fanpower_open}).

% ── §5.9 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Annual Economizer Operating Regime (Champaign, IL, TMY3)}
\label{dry:sec:annualregime}

Using the same real 8{,}760-hour weather file, every hour of the year was classified into
one of the three ASHRAE~90.1 economizer regimes introduced in
Chapter~\ref{ch:econ},~\S\ref{econ:sec:cascade}:

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Annual hours by economizer regime, real TMY3 data (WMO 725315, Champaign, IL).}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLl}
\toprule
\textbf{Regime} & \textbf{Threshold} & \textbf{Annual hours (\%)} \\
\midrule
Full free cooling     & OAT $\leq 4$\,\textdegree C          & 2{,}542\,hrs (29.0\,\%) \\
Partial free cooling  & $4 < \mathrm{OAT} \leq 10$\,\textdegree C & 1{,}358\,hrs (15.5\,\%) \\
Mechanical only        & OAT $> 10$\,\textdegree C            & 4{,}860\,hrs (55.5\,\%) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

Roughly 44.5\,\% of the year (full $+$ partial free cooling) sees some contribution from
the economizer loop, a direct consequence of Champaign's ASHRAE climate zone 5A winters
and a materially favourable input to the annual facility PUE calculation. All three
regimes are now thermally simulated across all 8{,}760 hours --- including the partial
band, whose duty split is rated off-design rather than assumed
(\S\ref{econ:sec:partialmodel}) --- and the resulting energy is reported in
Chapter~\ref{ch:performance}, \S\ref{perf:sec:seasonal}. Over the year the economizer
delivers \textbf{526\,MWh of the 1{,}314\,MWh} of cooling the hall needs
(\textbf{40.1\,\%}) without any compressor work at all.

\emph{Methodological note:} an earlier pass at this same calculation used a
\emph{monthly-average diurnal profile} (24 representative hours $\times$ 12 months) rather
than the real hourly trace, and under-counted full-free-cooling hours by
$\approx$440\,hrs/yr (2{,}101\,hrs vs.\ the 2{,}542\,hrs reported above) --- the smoothed
proxy misses real cold-snap extremes (the real annual minimum is $-27.4$\,\textdegree C,
which does not appear in any monthly-average table). The real 8{,}760-hour EPW file should
be used for all subsequent annual-performance work in this report, not the monthly-average
stat-file tables.

% ── Part B divider ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\zonedivider{Part B \quad---\quad Pressure-Drop Model}

% ── §5.10 ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Role of the Pressure-Drop Model}

As with the economizer (Chapter~\ref{ch:econ}, \S\ref{econ:sec:martin} onward), the dry
cooler's glycol-side pressure drop does not feed back into the refrigeration cycle's COP
(the dry cooler carries only glycol, never refrigerant); instead it sets the
\textbf{glycol pump head} required to circulate the loop, which is itself an energy
consumer in the facility's total (and hence part of PUE, alongside compressor and fan
energy).

\subsection{Why Not an Independent Friction Correlation}

Unlike the plate exchangers, where $D_h$, $\beta$, $b$, $L_w$, $L_v$ are catalog
parameters the model can use directly with the Han or Martin correlations, the dry
cooler's internal tube-circuit \emph{routing} (exact run length per circuit, number of
bends) is proprietary manufacturing detail that Kelvion does not publish. An early attempt
at an independent Darcy--Weisbach estimate (real circuit count, tube diameter back-solved
from the vendor's reported internal velocity, but a \emph{guessed} tube run length) came
out well below the vendor's real value (a modelled estimate against the 55.16\,kPa
reported) --- a clear illustration that the missing tube-length input, not the underlying
physics, was the source of error. This was replaced with the vendor-anchored scaling
method below, which needs no tube-geometry input at all.

% ── §5.11 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Vendor-Anchored Pressure-Drop Scaling}
\label{dry:sec:dpscaling}

\subsection{Derivation}

For turbulent flow in a smooth circular tube, Darcy--Weisbach with the Blasius friction
factor gives:

\[
  \Delta P = f\,\frac{L}{D}\,\frac{1}{2}\rho\,v^2,
  \qquad f = 0.184\,Re^{-0.2},
  \qquad Re = \frac{\rho\,v\,D}{\mu}
\]

Substituting $f$ and collecting terms:

\begin{equation}
  \Delta P \;\propto\; \rho^{0.8}\,v^{1.8}\,\mu^{0.2}
  \qquad \text{(for fixed tube geometry } L, D\text{)}
  \tag{Scaling\,1}
  \label{eq:scaling1}
\end{equation}

For a fixed flow area (i.e.\ the \emph{same physical coil}, same $N_{\mathrm{circuits}}$
and tube diameter), velocity scales as $v \propto \mdot/\rho$, so the ratio of pressure
drops between two operating conditions \emph{on the same coil} is:

\begin{equation}
  \frac{\Delta P_2}{\Delta P_1} =
  \left(\frac{\rho_2}{\rho_1}\right)^{0.8}
  \left(\frac{\mdot_2\,\rho_1}{\mdot_1\,\rho_2}\right)^{1.8}
  \left(\frac{\mu_2}{\mu_1}\right)^{0.2}
  \tag{Scaling\,2}
  \label{eq:scaling2}
\end{equation}

\textbf{Crucially, the unknown tube length $L$, diameter $D$, and circuit count
$N_{\mathrm{circuits}}$ all cancel exactly} in Eq.~\eqref{eq:scaling2}. Given one real,
vendor-reported pressure drop at a reference condition, Eq.~\eqref{eq:scaling2} scales it
to \emph{any other} condition on the same coil using only $\mdot$, $\rho$, and $\mu$ ---
all directly available from CoolProp, with no geometric assumption required. This is
implemented as \texttt{DryCooler.scale\_pressure\_drop()}.

\subsection{Application: Design Point $\to$ Free-Cooling Point}

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Vendor-anchored pressure-drop scaling: mechanical design point $\to$ free-cooling point.}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLL}
\toprule
\textbf{Quantity} & \textbf{Design point (ref.)} & \textbf{Free-cooling point} \\
\midrule
Glycol temperatures        & 42.0\,$\to$\,38.0\,\textdegree C & 15.0\,$\to$\,9.0\,\textdegree C \\
Average temperature        & 40.0\,\textdegree C               & 12.0\,\textdegree C \\
Glycol mass flow           & 11.64\,kg/s                       & 6.52\,kg/s \\
Density, $\rho$            & 1{,}013.4\,kg/m$^3$               & 1{,}027.2\,kg/m$^3$ \\
\textbf{Viscosity}, $\mu$  & 1.573\,mPa$\cdot$s                & \textbf{4.073\,mPa$\cdot$s} ($\approx$2.6$\times$ higher) \\
\midrule
Pressure drop, $\Delta P$  & \textbf{55.16\,kPa} (8\,psi, Kelvion) & \textbf{23.18\,kPa} (scaled, Eq.~\eqref{eq:scaling2}) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\noindent The scaling ratio $\Delta P_2/\Delta P_1 = 0.420$. Two competing effects
determine this result: the \emph{lower flow rate} at the free-cooling point pushes
$\Delta P$ down, while the \emph{nearly 3$\times$ higher glycol viscosity} at the colder
average temperature pushes it up. The flow-rate effect dominates, so the free-cooling
pressure drop is \emph{lower} than the mechanical-mode design point despite the glycol
being noticeably more viscous --- a result worth stating explicitly, since ``colder
$\Rightarrow$ lower pressure drop'' is not obvious on inspection.

\subsection{Validity Caveat}

The Reynolds number at the design point is comfortably turbulent, but at the free-cooling
point it drops close to the laminar/turbulent transition ($Re \approx 2{,}300$--$4{,}000$),
where the Blasius correlation underlying Eq.~\eqref{eq:scaling1} is not rigorously
validated. The scaled 23.18\,kPa should therefore be treated as a \textbf{reasonable
extrapolation}, not an exact result, pending vendor confirmation at that specific
operating point (see \S\ref{dry:sec:openitems}).

\emph{Note:} the glycol mass flow used for the free-cooling scaling above (6.52\,kg/s,
consistent with the economizer's own design basis) differs from the fixed-pump flow
(12.56\,kg/s) used in the thermal free-cooling check of \S\ref{dry:sec:freecool} --- the
same glycol-flow inconsistency flagged in \S\ref{dry:sec:openflow} propagates here and
should be resolved together with that item.

% ── §5.12 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Summary of Results at Both Operating Points}

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Dry cooler --- summary of all computed and vendor-confirmed results.}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLL}
\toprule
\textbf{Quantity} & \textbf{Mechanical mode (design)} & \textbf{Free cooling} \\
\midrule
Duty, $Q$                    & 182\,kW required / 196.5\,kW rated & 150\,kW \\
Glycol temperatures          & 42.0\,$\to$\,38.0\,\textdegree C     & 15.0\,$\to$\,9.0\,\textdegree C (target) \\
Ambient DB                   & 35.0\,\textdegree C                  & 4.0\,\textdegree C \\
Fan speed                    & 90.7\,\% (design-day duty)           & $\approx$32.3\,\% (model) \\
Fan power                    & 7.9\,kW (of 10.6\,kW rated, est.)    & 0.11\,kW avg (model) \\
Glycol-side $\Delta P$       & \textbf{55.16\,kPa} (8\,psi, Kelvion)& 23.18\,kPa (scaled, \S\ref{dry:sec:dpscaling}) \\
Data source                  & Kelvion Select RT                    & \texttt{dry\_cooler.py} model \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

% ── §5.12b ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Open Item: Fan Power Not Vendor-Reported}
\label{dry:sec:fanpower_open}

The Select RT results table does not report fan power. The \textbf{rated} value carried in
the model (\textbf{10.6\,kW}, at the coil's full 196.5\,kW selection duty) is therefore an
\textbf{estimate}, scaled by the fan laws ($P \sim N^3 D^5$) from a confirmed datasheet
figure for the same \texttt{ULF} fan family (2.685\,kW/fan for a 0.960\,m fan at
1000\,rpm, \S\ref{dry:sec:validation}):
$6 \times 2.685 \times (950/1000)^3 \times (0.910/0.960)^5 = 10.6$\,kW.

That six 0.910\,m fans should draw about what four 0.960\,m fans draw is not as odd as it
first looks: this coil's large surface means a low face velocity, and hence a lower
air-side $\Delta P$ per unit of flow (specific fan power 0.113 vs.\ 0.150\,kW/kcfm).

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Rated vs.\ operating fan power.} The 10.6\,kW is the power at 100\,\% design air
flow. The plant only rejects $\approx$182\,kW at the 35\,\textdegree C design day against
the coil's 196.5\,kW rating, so the variable-speed fans run at \textbf{90.7\,\% of design
flow}, and the cube law gives $10.6 \times 0.907^3 = \mathbf{7.9\,kW}$ --- the value that
enters the design-day PUE (\S\ref{perf:sec:pue}). The off-design solve is fed the real
loop glycol flow (11.64\,kg/s), and every hour of the annual simulation uses the same
machinery, so the design-day and seasonal numbers are consistent.
\end{quote}

\noindent The scaling behind the 10.6\,kW rating is physically consistent but not
confirmed. \textbf{It remains the single largest uncertainty in the design-day PUE} and
must be read off the unit's own datasheet; the free-cooling operating point should be
re-run at the same time.

% ── §5.13 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Air-Side Pressure Drop --- Scope Note}

Air-side pressure drop across the finned tube bundle (and hence the fan's required
external static pressure) is \textbf{not independently modelled} in this chapter. Finned
bundle air-side friction is proprietary fin-geometry data, normally read directly from a
manufacturer's own selection software rather than derived from a general correlation with
no vendor coil to calibrate against. The Kelvion Select RT datasheet
(\S\ref{dry:sec:datasheet}) reports \textbf{0.00\,inWg external static pressure} at the
free-blow catalog rating condition used for this selection; a site-specific external
static pressure (accounting for ducting, louvers, or hail guards, if any) would need to be
supplied back into the Select RT tool for a final fan-curve check.

% ── §5.14 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Open Items and Recommendations}
\label{dry:sec:openitems}

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Compressor-dependent design duty.} The 190\,kW mechanical-mode target
        duty is derived backward from the project's COP $\geq 3.5$ requirement, not from
        a completed compressor model. Reconfirm $Q_{\mathrm{cond}}$ once the (pending)
        compressor chapter supplies a real $W_{\mathrm{comp}}$, and re-select if the real
        duty differs materially from 190\,kW.
  \item \textbf{Free-cooling glycol flow inconsistency.} \S\ref{dry:sec:openflow} and
        \S\ref{dry:sec:dpscaling} both flag that the dry cooler model (fixed-pump,
        12.56\,kg/s) and the economizer chapter (6.52\,kg/s) currently assume different
        glycol flow rates in free-cooling mode. Resolve via an explicit glycol-pump
        control-strategy decision (fixed-speed vs.\ variable-speed) and re-run both
        chapters' free-cooling checks consistently.
  \item \textbf{Free-cooling operating point not vendor-confirmed.} Kelvion Select RT was
        only run at the mechanical-mode design point (\S\ref{dry:sec:vendor}). The
        free-cooling fan speed/power (\S\ref{dry:sec:freecool}) and the scaled pressure
        drop (\S\ref{dry:sec:dpscaling}) remain model estimates. Re-run the selection (or
        its performance-check mode) at 150\,kW / 4\,\textdegree C DB / glycol
        15\,$\to$\,9\,\textdegree C to obtain vendor-confirmed numbers for this point too.
  \item \textbf{Off-design $UA$ scaling exponent.} The 0.6 power-law exponent used to
        scale $UA$ with reduced air flow in \texttt{predict\_off\_design()}
        (\S\ref{dry:sec:epsntu}) is a standard approximation, not calibrated against this
        specific coil. Refine if a vendor part-load performance curve becomes available.
  \item \textbf{Air-side pressure drop / fan static pressure.} Not modelled here
        (\S\ref{dry:sec:openitems} above); confirm actual installed external static
        pressure requirements (ducting, louvres, site obstructions) against the Select RT
        fan curve before finalising fan selection.
  \item \textbf{Mechanical-mode annual hours not yet thermally simulated.}
        \S\ref{dry:sec:annualregime} counts 4{,}860\,hrs/yr of mechanical-mode operation
        but does not yet compute the dry cooler's duty or fan energy across those hours,
        since that requires the (pending) compressor's COP as a function of hourly
        condensing temperature.
\end{enumerate}

% ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
\chapter{Electronic Expansion Valve (EEV)}
\label{ch:eev}
% ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

\zonedivider{Part A \quad---\quad Isenthalpic Throttling Model}

% ── §6.1 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Design Basis and Role}

The electronic expansion valve (EEV) is the fourth and final component of the refrigerant
loop, closing the cycle between the condenser (Chapter~\ref{ch:cond}) and the evaporator
(Chapter~\ref{ch:evap}): subcooled liquid R-290 leaving the condenser at the high
(condensing) pressure is throttled down to the low (evaporating) pressure, flashing
partially to vapour in the process, before entering the evaporator. In operation the EEV
modulates its orifice opening to hold a target evaporator superheat; \textbf{this chapter
models the steady design point only} --- the superheat control loop is a separate
discussion item, noted in \S\ref{eev:sec:limitations}.

\subsection{Why Isenthalpic, Not a Pressure-Drop Correlation}
\label{eev:sec:isenthalpic}

Unlike every other component in this report, the EEV needs no Han (2003) or Martin (1996)
friction correlation at all. Throttling across a valve is \textbf{adiabatic and does no
shaft work}, so the steady-flow energy balance collapses to:

\[
  h_{\mathrm{out}} = h_{\mathrm{in}}
\]

The pressure drop across the valve is not a frictional loss to be \emph{modelled} ---
it is the entire \emph{design intent} of the component: the valve exists specifically to
create the pressure difference between the condensing and evaporating sides of the cycle.
The valve's mechanical sizing (Part~B) determines \emph{how large an orifice} produces a
given mass flow at a given $\Delta P$, not the $\Delta P$ itself, which is fixed
externally by the condensing and evaporating pressures the rest of the cycle establishes.

% ── §6.2 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Inlet State: Subcooled Liquid from the Condenser}
\label{eev:sec:inlet}

The EEV inlet state is taken directly as the condenser outlet state
(Chapter~\ref{ch:cond}), with no liquid-line pressure drop modelled between the two
components (a standard simplifying assumption for a short, well-insulated liquid line;
flagged in \S\ref{eev:sec:limitations}).

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{EEV inlet state (= condenser outlet, Chapter~\ref{ch:cond}).}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLl}
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Value} & \textbf{Basis} \\
\midrule
Refrigerant                          & R-290 (propane)     & Project spec \\
Inlet pressure, $p_{\mathrm{in}}$    & 17.89\,bar(a)        & Condensing pressure, 52\,\textdegree C sat.\ (Ch.~\ref{ch:cond}) \\
Inlet enthalpy, $h_{\mathrm{in}}$    & 327.6\,kJ/kg         & Subcooled outlet, 47\,\textdegree C (Ch.~\ref{ch:cond}, \S\ref{cond:sec:subcool}) \\
Refrigerant mass flow, $\mdot_r$     & 0.553\,kg/s\textsuperscript{*} & From condenser: $\mdot_r = Q_{\mathrm{cond}}/\Delta h$ \\
Valve discharge coefficient, $C_d$   & 0.65                 & \assumed\ --- typical EEV range 0.6--0.7 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}

{\small \textsuperscript{*}Recheck against confirmed $\mdot_r$ from the compressor module, consistent
with the same caveat carried in Chapters~\ref{ch:evap} and~\ref{ch:cond}.}
\end{table}

\texttt{calc\_inlet\_state()} recovers the valve-inlet temperature and subcooling from
$(p_{\mathrm{in}}, h_{\mathrm{in}})$ via CoolProp:

\[
  T_{\mathrm{in}} = T(h_{\mathrm{in}}, p_{\mathrm{in}}),
  \qquad
  \Delta T_{\mathrm{sub}} = T_{\mathrm{bubble}}(p_{\mathrm{in}}) - T_{\mathrm{in}}
\]

\subsection{Consistency Check Against the Condenser Chapter}
\label{eev:sec:consistency}

Evaluating this independently returns $T_{\mathrm{in}} = 47.01$\,\textdegree C and
$\Delta T_{\mathrm{sub}} = 4.99$\,K --- matching the condenser chapter's stated 47\,\textdegree C
outlet temperature and 5\,K design subcooling (\S\ref{cond:sec:subcool}) to within
rounding. This is a genuine cross-chapter validation: the condenser and EEV models were
built independently but agree on the same thermodynamic state, confirming the enthalpy
value carried between the two chapters is internally consistent.

% ── §6.3 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Outlet State: Isenthalpic Flash to the Evaporating Pressure}
\label{eev:sec:outlet}

The outlet pressure is set by the evaporator's operating pressure
(Chapter~\ref{ch:evap}), and the outlet enthalpy is, by the energy balance of
\S\ref{eev:sec:isenthalpic}, unchanged from the inlet:

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{EEV outlet state (= evaporator inlet, Chapter~\ref{ch:evap}).}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLl}
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Value} & \textbf{Basis} \\
\midrule
Outlet pressure, $p_{\mathrm{out}}$   & 5.84\,bar(a)        & Evaporating pressure, 7\,\textdegree C sat.\ (project spec) \\
Outlet enthalpy, $h_{\mathrm{out}}$   & 327.6\,kJ/kg $=h_{\mathrm{in}}$ & Isenthalpic \\
\textbf{Evaporating temperature}, $T_{\mathrm{out}}$ & \textbf{7.00\,\textdegree C} & Saturation temp.\ at $p_{\mathrm{out}}$ \\
\textbf{Outlet quality (flash gas)}, $x_{\mathrm{out}}$ & \textbf{0.301 (30.1\,\%)} & $(h_{\mathrm{out}} - h_f)/(h_g - h_f)$ \\
Pressure drop across valve, $\Delta P$ & 12.05\,bar (1{,}204.8\,kPa) & $= p_{\mathrm{in}} - p_{\mathrm{out}}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\subsection{Interpreting the 30\,\% Flash Gas Fraction}

Roughly 30\,\% of the refrigerant mass flashes to vapour \emph{immediately upon throttling},
before any heat is absorbed in the evaporator. This is normal and expected: the liquid
entering the valve at 47\,\textdegree C is far hotter than the 7\,\textdegree C saturation
temperature on the low-pressure side, so a large fraction of the enthalpy difference is
released as flash gas rather than being available for useful refrigeration. This directly
motivates the condenser's 5\,K design subcooling (Chapter~\ref{ch:cond},
\S\ref{cond:sec:subcool}): every additional kelvin of subcooling reduces the flash-gas
fraction and increases the refrigerating effect delivered per unit mass flow, at the cost
of a larger condenser subcooling zone.

% ── Part B divider ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\zonedivider{Part B \quad---\quad Orifice / Mechanical Sizing}

% ── §6.4 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Role of the Orifice Sizing Model}

Sections~\ref{eev:sec:inlet}--\ref{eev:sec:outlet} establish the thermodynamic states either
side of the valve; they say nothing about the \emph{physical valve} needed to produce that
pressure drop at the required mass flow. Part~B closes that gap with a first-cut orifice
flow area, which is the number actually used to shortlist a real EEV from a manufacturer's
capacity/$K_v$ tables (e.g.\ Danfoss ETS, Emerson/Alco EX series).

% ── §6.5 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Incompressible Orifice Flow Model}
\label{eev:sec:orifice}

\texttt{calc\_orifice()} uses the standard incompressible-liquid orifice equation,
evaluated at the liquid inlet state:

\begin{equation}
  \mdot_r = C_d\,A_{\mathrm{orifice}}\,\sqrt{2\,\rho_{\mathrm{in}}\,\Delta P}
  \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
  A_{\mathrm{orifice}} = \frac{\mdot_r}{C_d\sqrt{2\,\rho_{\mathrm{in}}\,\Delta P}}
  \tag{Orifice\,1}
  \label{eq:orifice}
\end{equation}

with $\rho_{\mathrm{in}}$ the subcooled liquid density at the valve inlet (from CoolProp,
$\rho_{\mathrm{in}} = 455.6$\,kg/m$^3$ at the design state).

\subsection{Why This Is a First-Cut Estimate, Not a Final Selection}
\label{eev:sec:whyfirstcut}

Equation~\eqref{eq:orifice} assumes single-phase incompressible flow through the orifice.
In reality, the flow begins flashing \emph{inside} the valve port itself (the 30.1\,\%
outlet quality of \S\ref{eev:sec:outlet} does not appear instantaneously at the valve exit
plane; some of it forms within the throttling passage), which increases the specific
volume and alters the effective discharge behaviour relative to a purely liquid orifice.
Manufacturers account for this with two-phase-corrected $K_v$/capacity ratings, obtained
from flow-bench testing rather than derived analytically. Equation~\eqref{eq:orifice}
therefore gives a \textbf{flow-area estimate suitable for an initial vendor shortlist},
not a substitute for that vendor's own capacity table.

% ── §6.6 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Orifice Sizing Results}

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{EEV orifice sizing at the design point (\texttt{eev.py}).}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lL}
\toprule
\textbf{Quantity} & \textbf{Value} \\
\midrule
Liquid density at inlet, $\rho_{\mathrm{in}}$ & 455.6\,kg/m$^3$ \\
Pressure drop, $\Delta P$                     & 1{,}204.8\,kPa (12.05\,bar) \\
Discharge coefficient, $C_d$                  & 0.65 (\assumed) \\
\textbf{Orifice flow area}, $A_{\mathrm{orifice}}$ & \textbf{25.68\,mm$^2$} \\
\textbf{Orifice diameter}, $d_{\mathrm{orifice}}$  & \textbf{5.72\,mm} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

This is a modest orifice diameter, consistent with a 150\,kW-class R-290 system; it sets
the flow-area target for shortlisting a real EEV against a manufacturer's two-phase
capacity table, per \S\ref{eev:sec:whyfirstcut}.

% ── §6.7 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Limitations and Open Items}
\label{eev:sec:limitations}

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Superheat control is not modelled here.} The EEV's real function is to
        \emph{modulate} its orifice opening to hold a target evaporator superheat under
        varying load --- a dynamic control problem, distinct from the single steady design
        point analysed in this chapter. A control-loop discussion (target superheat,
        response time, hunting/stability) is a separate item for the compressor/controls
        chapter once available.
  \item \textbf{No liquid-line pressure drop.} The valve inlet state is taken directly as
        the condenser outlet state, with no allowance for pipe friction or elevation change
        in the liquid line between the two components. The liquid line's diameter is now
        sized in Chapter~\ref{ch:piping} (\S\ref{piping:sec:liquid}), but its length and
        fittings remain unspecified per the project brief's scope; reasonable for a short,
        insulated run, but should be revisited once an actual piping layout is available.
  \item \textbf{Mass flow is compressor-dependent.} As in Chapters~\ref{ch:evap} and
        \ref{ch:cond}, $\mdot_r = 0.553$\,kg/s is derived from the provisional 190\,kW
        condenser duty, itself backed out from the COP $\geq 3.5$ requirement rather than a
        completed compressor model. Reconfirm once the compressor chapter is available.
  \item \textbf{Orifice sizing is a first-cut liquid-orifice estimate}
        (\S\ref{eev:sec:whyfirstcut}), not a vendor-validated two-phase capacity rating.
        Use $A_{\mathrm{orifice}} = 25.68$\,mm$^2$ / $d_{\mathrm{orifice}} = 5.72$\,mm as a
        starting point for a manufacturer catalog search, not a final selection.
\end{enumerate}

% ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
\chapter{Glycol Loop Circulation Pump}
\label{ch:pump}
% ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

\zonedivider{Part A \quad---\quad System Curve, Piping, and Duty Point Derivation}

% ── §7.1 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Design Basis and Role}

A single, variable-speed (VFD) circulation pump drives the 30\,\% propylene-glycol
intermediate loop. Per the bypass-valve arrangement shown in the system schematic
(Fig.~\ref{fig:setup}), the SAME physical pump serves two distinct circuits depending on
plant operating mode:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Mechanical mode}: Dry Cooler $\leftrightarrow$ Condenser
        (Chapters~\ref{ch:dry}, \ref{ch:cond}), compressor running, glycol flow
        $\mdot_{\mathrm{glycol}} = 11.64$\,kg/s.
  \item \textbf{Free-cooling mode}: Dry Cooler $\leftrightarrow$ Economizer
        (Chapters~\ref{ch:dry}, \ref{ch:econ}), compressor off, glycol flow
        $\mdot_{\mathrm{glycol}} = 6.52$\,kg/s.
\end{itemize}

A \textbf{variable-speed} pump (rather than fixed-speed) is adopted specifically to resolve
a cross-chapter inconsistency identified during integration: at a single fixed flow rate,
the dry cooler's free-cooling-mode glycol span fell short of the full
15\,\textdegree C\,$\to$\,9\,\textdegree C economizer design span. Running the pump at
reduced speed in free-cooling mode, matched to the economizer's own 6.52\,kg/s design
flow, restores the intended glycol temperature span in that mode and additionally saves
pump energy (roughly with the cube of speed, per the affinity laws) during the
$\approx$55\,\% of annual hours the plant spends in free-cooling.

% ── §7.2 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{System Curve}
\label{pump:sec:syscurve}

The pump must overcome the sum of all glycol-side resistances in whichever branch is
active. Two independent system curves therefore exist:

\[
  \Delta P_{\mathrm{sys,mech}}(\mdot) = \Delta P_{\mathrm{dry cooler}}
  + \Delta P_{\mathrm{condenser,glycol}} + \Delta P_{\mathrm{piping}}
\]
\[
  \Delta P_{\mathrm{sys,free}}(\mdot) = \Delta P_{\mathrm{dry cooler,free}}
  + \Delta P_{\mathrm{economizer,glycol}} + \Delta P_{\mathrm{piping}}
\]

Each component term is evaluated using the SAME correlation and geometry already
validated in that component's own chapter --- the condenser term is the Martin (1996)
correlation of \S\ref{cond:sec:martin} (38/42\,\textdegree C, 11.64\,kg/s design
point), the economizer term is \S\ref{econ:sec:martin}'s value as-is, and the dry cooler
terms are the vendor-confirmed mechanical-mode value and its free-cooling rescaling
(Chapter~\ref{ch:dry}), evaluated PARAMETRICALLY in $\mdot$ (not only at the single design
point) so a real intersection with the pump curve (\S\ref{pump:sec:curve}) can be solved.

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{System curve component breakdown at each mode's design flow.}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLL}
\toprule
\textbf{Component} & \textbf{Mechanical (11.64\,kg/s)} & \textbf{Free-cooling (6.52\,kg/s)} \\
\midrule
Dry cooler                & 55.16\,kPa (8\,psi, vendor) & 23.18\,kPa (rescaled) \\
Condenser / Economizer    & 35.91\,kPa (Martin)         & 10.43\,kPa (as-modeled) \\
Piping (\S\ref{pump:sec:piping}) & 49.85\,kPa           & 19.55\,kPa \\
\midrule
\textbf{Total}            & \textbf{140.92\,kPa = 14.18\,m} & \textbf{53.16\,kPa = 5.28\,m} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

% ── §7.3 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Piping Assumptions}
\label{pump:sec:piping}

The project brief explicitly scopes piping to \emph{diameters only --- no lengths,
fittings, or supports}. A piping term is nonetheless required to close the pump's system
curve, so the following are adopted as explicit, flagged assumptions (not measured or
specified geometry):

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Pipe size}: sized for $\approx$1.8\,m/s at the mechanical design flow
        (standard closed-loop hydronic velocity range, 1--2.4\,m/s) $\Rightarrow$
        \textbf{3\,in Schedule 40 steel} (77.9\,mm ID), giving an actual velocity of
        1.88\,m/s.
  \item \textbf{Equivalent length}: \textbf{65\,m} total (supply + return + fittings
        allowance), estimated from the project's actual 8\,m\,$\times$\,12\,m data-hall
        footprint (mechanical-room-to-roof-and-back run for the outdoor dry cooler,
        plus a fittings allowance) --- not a measured value.
  \item \textbf{Friction model}: Darcy--Weisbach with the Swamee--Jain explicit
        approximation to the Colebrook equation,
        \[
          f = \frac{0.25}{\left[\log_{10}\!\left(\dfrac{\varepsilon/D}{3.7}
              + \dfrac{5.74}{Re^{0.9}}\right)\right]^2}, \qquad
          \Delta P = f\,\frac{L_{\mathrm{eq}}}{D}\,\frac{\rho v^2}{2}
        \]
        with $\varepsilon = 0.045$\,mm (steel).
\end{itemize}

At the mechanical design point, $Re \approx 1.0\times10^5$ (fully turbulent); at the
free-cooling design point, $Re \approx 2.6\times10^4$. Piping accounts for roughly a third
of the total mechanical-mode system head --- the single largest assumption-driven term in
this chapter, and the first item to revisit if real routing geometry becomes available.

\zonedivider{Part B \quad---\quad Vendor Selection and Variable-Speed Operation}

% ── §7.4 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Selection Margin}

The bare system-curve duty point is margined before vendor selection, to absorb the
piping/geometry assumption uncertainty above without grossly oversizing the pump (a pump
selected far above its true duty runs on the inefficient, cavitation-prone far-left of its
curve). A 10\,\% flow / 18\,\% head margin is applied --- the same philosophy as the 20\,\%
area margin already used for the condenser (\S\ref{cond:sec:lmtd}):

\[
  Q_{\mathrm{select}} = 1.10 \times 41.36 = 45.50\;\mathrm{m^3/h}, \qquad
  H_{\mathrm{select}} = 1.18 \times 14.18 = 16.73\;\mathrm{m}
\]

No separate margin is applied to the free-cooling point; the VFD's speed turndown already
provides ample headroom there.

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Frame outcome --- 2AD superseded, 2.5AC required.} At this reconciled duty
(41.36\,m$^3$/h @ 14.18\,m, from the 11.64\,kg/s loop flow) \textbf{no 2AD impeller trim
clears the margined point} --- the 2AD envelope tops out below 16.73\,m at 45.50\,m$^3$/h.
The 2AD trim walkthrough that follows was performed at the earlier, lower duty and is
\emph{superseded}; it is retained as the method. The \textbf{Bell \& Gossett e-1510
2.5AC} frame (the next size up, whose envelope covers this point --- see
Table~\ref{tab:pump_frames}) is the selected pump, closing this chapter's open item~3.
The pump \emph{power} is unchanged by the frame choice: the electrical draw of
\textbf{2.57\,kW} (mechanical) / 0.54\,kW (free cooling) is the hydraulic duty
$\rho g \dot V H / \eta$ to move the glycol, independent of which casing delivers it.
The remaining task is to digitize the 2.5AC trim curves and re-run the exact trim and
VFD-speed selection on that frame.
\end{quote}

% ── §7.5 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Vendor Pump Curve and Trim Selection}
\label{pump:sec:curve}

Performance data for the \textbf{Bell \& Gossett e-1510 2AD} (1750\,RPM) were digitized
from the vendor curve sheet across five available impeller trims (5.0--7.0\,in) and fit to
a quadratic $H(Q) = c_2 Q^2 + c_1 Q + c_0$ (least squares, $Q$ in m$^3$/h, $H$ in m). Each
trim's 100\,\%-speed curve was evaluated against the margined selection point:

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Trim comparison at the \emph{superseded} margined point (35.50\,m$^3$/h @
11.49\,m), on the undersized 2AD frame. Shown as the method; the current duty
(45.50\,m$^3$/h @ 16.73\,m) requires the 2.5AC frame.}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLLL}
\toprule
\textbf{Trim} & \textbf{$H$ @ mech.\ $Q$} & \textbf{$H$ @ selection $Q$} & \textbf{Clears margin?} \\
\midrule
5.0\,in & 4.39\,m  & 3.57\,m  & No \\
5.5\,in & 6.04\,m  & 5.04\,m  & No \\
6.0\,in & 8.06\,m  & 7.09\,m  & No \\
6.5\,in & 9.88\,m  & 9.01\,m  & No \\
\textbf{7.0\,in} & \textbf{12.91\,m} & \textbf{12.11\,m} & \textbf{Yes} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

The \textbf{7.0\,in trim} is the smallest of the five that clears the margined selection
point, and is therefore selected. Smaller trims (6.5\,in and below) fall short of the
required head once margin is included.

At 100\,\% speed (uncontrolled), the 7.0\,in curve intersects the mechanical-mode system
curve (\S\ref{pump:sec:syscurve}) at 36.06\,m$^3$/h @ 11.96\,m --- confirming the selected
trim carries head/flow margin above the actual design duty, which the VFD then trims back
down to the exact required operating points (\S\ref{pump:sec:vfd}).

% ── §7.5b ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\subsection{Manufacturer Series and Speed Screening}
\label{pump:sec:vendor}

Selection was carried out against the \textbf{Bell \& Gossett Series e-1510}
base-mounted end-suction centrifugal line, a commonly specified series for closed-loop
HVAC and data-center glycol circulation duty at this scale. The series is published as a
composite (coverage) chart across multiple synchronous speeds:

\begin{itemize}
  \item The \textbf{3500\,RPM (2-pole)} composite chart was screened first, but its
        minimum head coverage across all frame sizes exceeds 40\,ft even at the
        lowest-head model --- well above the 37.7\,ft target at the margined selection
        point, confirming this speed family is intended for higher-head process duty and
        is not suitable here.
  \item The \textbf{1750\,RPM (4-pole)} composite chart, covering approximately
        13--60\,ft across its low-head model sizes, was identified as the correct speed
        family and used for all subsequent selection.
\end{itemize}

At the margined selection flow (156.3\,GPM), the digitized 1750\,RPM composite envelope
(Table~\ref{tab:composite}) placed the duty point in a narrow gap between two adjacent
frame sizes: the \textbf{2AD} model's maximum head at this flow falls just short of the
37.7\,ft target, while the next size up (\textbf{2.5AC}) exceeds it by a comparable margin
in the other direction. Composite charts carry inherent reading tolerance at this
resolution, so the individual detailed performance curve (used throughout
\S\ref{pump:sec:curve}) was digitized directly from the manufacturer's curve sheet
(\textbf{Bell \& Gossett document B-261G}, dated 12/30/2013) to resolve the selection
rather than relying on the coarser composite envelope.

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Digitized 1750\,RPM composite chart coverage envelope, candidate frame sizes.}
\label{tab:pump_frames}
\label{tab:composite}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLL}
\toprule
\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Flow range (GPM)} & \textbf{Head range (ft)} \\
\midrule
1.5AD & 66 -- 151  & 16 -- 50 \\
\textbf{2AD}   & \textbf{116 -- 188} & \textbf{14 -- 36} \\
2.5AC & 105 -- 343 & 18 -- 49 \\
3AD   & 189 -- 489 & 12.5 -- 45 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

At the \emph{superseded} lower duty (156.3\,GPM target) the 2AD frame's composite envelope
(116--188\,GPM) just covered the flow. At the reconciled duty it does not: the operating
point is \textbf{182\,GPM @ 46.5\,ft}, above the 2AD envelope's 188\,GPM / 36\,ft ceiling,
so the \textbf{2.5AC} frame is selected (105--343\,GPM, 18--49\,ft ---
Table~\ref{tab:pump_frames}), as set out in the frame-outcome note of \S7.4. The analysis
below is retained as the method on the 2AD frame.

\subsection{Cross-Check: Direct Trim Interpolation}

As an independent cross-check on the quadratic-fit trim selection of \S\ref{pump:sec:curve},
linear interpolation directly between the digitized 6.5\,in and 7.0\,in curves at the
margined selection flow (156.3\,GPM, target head 37.7\,ft) gives a required trim diameter of

\[
  D_{\mathrm{trim}} = 7.0 - 0.5 \times \frac{39.9 - 37.7}{39.9 - 30.6} \approx 6.9\;\mathrm{in}
\]

This 6.9\,in interpolation applies to the \emph{superseded} 2AD analysis; it confirmed the
old duty sat right at the top of the 2AD envelope --- which is precisely why the higher
reconciled duty pushes past it to the 2.5AC frame (\S7.4). The equivalent trim
interpolation on the 2.5AC curves is the remaining pump task.

% ── §7.6 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Variable-Speed Operation}
\label{pump:sec:vfd}

For each mode, the VFD speed fraction $s$ required to make the affinity-scaled vendor
curve pass exactly through that mode's design duty point is solved from

\[
  H_{100\%}\!\left(\frac{Q_{\mathrm{required}}}{s}\right) \cdot s^2 = H_{\mathrm{required}}
\]

via root-finding (Brent's method) on the fitted $H_{100\%}(Q)$ curve.

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Required VFD speed fraction at each operating point --- \emph{superseded 2AD
frame}. The current duty is 41.36\,m$^3$/h @ 14.18\,m (mechanical); these speeds are to
be re-derived on the selected 2.5AC frame.}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLL}
\toprule
\textbf{Operating point} & \textbf{Duty (superseded 2AD)} & \textbf{Required speed} \\
\midrule
Mechanical (exact design point)  & 32.27\,m$^3$/h @ 9.74\,m  & 90.0\,\% \\
Free-cooling (exact design point) & 22.85\,m$^3$/h @ 6.17\,m  & 69.5\,\% \\
Margined selection point          & 35.50\,m$^3$/h @ 11.49\,m & 98.1\,\% \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

Both operating speeds sit comfortably within a normal VFD turndown range, well clear of
minimum-speed instability or recirculation concerns at either end. That the margined
selection point requires 98.1\,\% (i.e., just under the pump's rated speed) confirms the
7.0\,in trim was an appropriately tight selection rather than an oversized one.

% ── §7.7 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Power and Motor Selection}

\subsection{Efficiency}

The manufacturer's individual performance curve sheet (B-261G) also carries efficiency
contour lines crossing the operating region. Reading these near the design point gives an
estimated pump efficiency of \textbf{72--74\,\%} --- refining the earlier generic 70\,\%
assumption used before a real vendor curve was available. This reading carries more
uncertainty than the digitized head data (\S\ref{pump:sec:curve}), since efficiency
contours are thin diagonal lines that are harder to isolate precisely than the bold head
curves; it should be confirmed against the manufacturer's selection software (e.g.\ B\&G
ESP-Plus) before use in the final annual-energy/PUE calculations. A representative value
of $\eta_{\mathrm{pump}} = 73\,\%$ is adopted here, with the motor efficiency assumption
(90\,\%) unchanged pending nameplate confirmation.

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Estimated pump power by operating mode ($\eta_{\mathrm{pump}}=73\,\%$, vendor-curve-informed).}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLLL}
\toprule
\textbf{Mode} & \textbf{Hydraulic power} & \textbf{Shaft power} & \textbf{Electrical power (assumed motor eff.)} \\
\midrule
Mechanical    & 0.87\,kW & 1.19\,kW & 1.32\,kW \\
Free-cooling  & 0.39\,kW & 0.54\,kW & 0.60\,kW \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\subsection{Motor Selection}

At the mechanical-mode duty point, the required shaft power (1.19\,kW $\approx$ 1.59\,hp)
rounds up to the next standard NEMA frame size: \textbf{2\,hp, 1750\,RPM}. This matches
the brake-horsepower contour labeled directly on the same B-261G curve sheet at this
operating point, providing an independent confirmation of the sizing. The resulting
headroom (1.492\,kW rated output against a 1.19\,kW requirement, $\approx$25\,\%) is
consistent with standard NEMA motor-selection practice and comfortably covers the modest
additional load if the piping assumption of \S\ref{pump:sec:piping} proves conservative.

% ── §7.7b ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Cross-Check via Independent Duty-Point Derivation}

As a secondary check on the detailed component-by-component system curve of
\S\ref{pump:sec:syscurve}, an independent duty-point estimate was also carried out using a
simplified, generic derivation: a design $\Delta T = 5$\,K across the dry cooler, a
representative 30\,\% PG mixture density/specific-heat pair ($\rho \approx
1030$\,kg/m$^3$, $c_p \approx 3770$\,J/kg-K), applied to the full 190\,kW design load. This
gives $\mdot \approx 10.08$\,kg/s and a lumped system $\Delta P \approx 116.1$\,kPa
(11.49\,m head) --- landing, to within rounding, on the same 35.50\,m$^3$/h @ 11.49\,m
point already derived as this chapter's margined selection point
(\S\ref{pump:sec:syscurve}). The agreement is a useful sanity check, though the two
derivations are not independent verifications of the same physics: the generic estimate
does not trace to the dry cooler/condenser/economizer/piping breakdown this chapter
otherwise relies on, and its $\Delta T = 5$\,K and lumped $\Delta P$ are themselves
assumptions rather than component-level results. The detailed derivation of
\S\ref{pump:sec:syscurve}--\S\ref{pump:sec:piping} remains this chapter's primary basis;
this cross-check is retained only as corroborating evidence that the final duty point is
reasonable.

% ── §7.8 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Open Items and Limitations}

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Piping equivalent length (65\,m) is an assumption}, not measured or
        specified geometry, sized only from the project's stated 8\,m\,$\times$\,12\,m
        footprint. It is the single largest lever in the system curve (roughly a third of
        total mechanical-mode head) and the first item to revisit if real routing data
        becomes available.
  \item \textbf{Pump efficiency (73\,\%) is read from curve-sheet contour lines, not a
        digitized efficiency map}, and carries more uncertainty than the head-curve data
        it sits alongside; motor efficiency (90\,\%) remains a generic assumption pending
        nameplate data. Both should be confirmed via the manufacturer's ESP-Plus selection
        software or a vendor quotation.
  \item \textbf{Frame moved to 2.5AC (this open item is now resolved, and a task remains).}
        Reconciling the glycol loop to its true 11.64\,kg/s flow raised the mechanical duty
        to 41.36\,m$^3$/h @ 14.18\,m, which sits \emph{outside} the 2AD envelope --- so the
        2.5AC frame is selected (\S7.4). The pump \emph{power} (2.57\,kW) is unaffected, but
        the exact trim and VFD-speed selection must be re-run once the 2.5AC vendor curves
        are digitized. A finalized piping layout longer than the assumed 65\,m would raise
        the head further and should be checked against the 2.5AC envelope.
  \item \textbf{Condenser/economizer channel-count split ($N_{cp,\mathrm{glycol}}=24$)
        remains an assumed placeholder} (\S\ref{cond:sec:martin}, \S\ref{econ:sec:martin}),
        pending vendor-confirmed plate geometry.
  \item \textbf{NPSH has not been checked} against the selected pump's NPSHr, since the
        digitized vendor data did not include an NPSH curve; this should be confirmed once
        the full vendor datasheet is available, particularly given the pump's rooftop
        supply-side elevation relative to the mechanical room.
  \item \textbf{Materials of construction} (mechanical seal elastomer --- EPDM recommended
        for inhibited propylene glycol service --- and wetted metallurgy) have not yet been
        confirmed against the specific glycol product to be used; galvanized wetted
        components should be avoided in glycol service.
\end{enumerate}

% ── §7.9 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{References}

\begin{enumerate}
  \item Bell \& Gossett, \textit{Series e-1510 Composite Performance Curves}, Document
        B-261, Xylem Inc.
  \item Bell \& Gossett, \textit{Series e-1510 Individual Performance Curves}, Document
        B-261G, Xylem Inc., dated 12/30/2013.
\end{enumerate}

\chapter{Chilled-Water (Data-Center / CRAH-Side) Circulation Pump}
\label{ch:chwpump}
% ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

\zonedivider{Part A \quad---\quad System Curve, CRAH/Piping Assumptions, and Duty Point}

% ── §8.1 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Design Basis and Role}

A second, dedicated circulation pump drives the chilled-water (CHW) loop between the
data-hall CRAH/in-row units and the mechanical room. This pump is entirely separate from
the glycol loop pump of Chapter~\ref{ch:pump} --- it never sees glycol, and its duty point
is set by the CHW-side flow and resistances of the evaporator (Chapter~\ref{ch:evap}) and
economizer (Chapter~\ref{ch:econ}) rather than the dry cooler.

As with the glycol loop, the CHW loop has two parallel branches selected by operating
mode:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Mechanical (chiller) mode}: CRAH $\leftrightarrow$ Evaporator
        (Chapter~\ref{ch:evap}).
  \item \textbf{Free-cooling mode}: CRAH $\leftrightarrow$ Economizer
        (Chapter~\ref{ch:econ}).
\end{itemize}

Unlike the glycol loop, both branches move the \emph{same} CHW mass flow
($\mdot_{\mathrm{water}} = 5.97$\,kg/s, 21\,\textdegree C\,$\to$\,15\,\textdegree C) since
both HXs are sized for the full 150\,kW design load rather than a reduced free-cooling
flow --- so a single design duty point (rather than two, as in Chapter~\ref{ch:pump})
governs this pump's selection. A variable-speed (VFD) pump is nonetheless adopted, for the
same energy-saving rationale as the glycol pump and to allow future turndown at reduced IT
load (30\,kW minimum, per the project brief).

% ── §8.2 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{System Curve}
\label{chwpump:sec:syscurve}

The pump must overcome the sum of all CHW-side resistances in whichever branch is active:

\[
  \Delta P_{\mathrm{sys}}(\mdot) = \Delta P_{\mathrm{evap/econ,\,water}}
  + \Delta P_{\mathrm{CRAH}} + \Delta P_{\mathrm{piping}}
\]

The evaporator and economizer water-side terms are the Martin (1996) values already
computed in their own chapters (\S\ref{evap:sec:martin}, \S\ref{econ:sec:martin}); since
both branches carry the same flow, the pump is sized on the higher-resistance branch
(evaporator), with a balancing valve on the economizer branch to match it.

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{System curve component breakdown at the design flow (5.97\,kg/s, 21.5\,m$^3$/h).}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lL}
\toprule
\textbf{Component} & \textbf{$\Delta P$} \\
\midrule
Evaporator, water side (Martin, worst-case branch) & 7.73\,kPa \\
Economizer, water side (Martin, for reference)      & 6.67\,kPa \\
CRAH coil (\S\ref{chwpump:sec:crah}, \textbf{assumed}) & 50.0\,kPa \\
Piping/fittings (\S\ref{chwpump:sec:crah}, \textbf{assumed}) & 25.0\,kPa \\
\midrule
\textbf{Total (design basis)} & \textbf{82.7\,kPa = 8.45\,m = 27.7\,ft} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

% ── §8.3 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{CRAH and Piping Assumptions}
\label{chwpump:sec:crah}

Two of the four system-curve terms above are \textbf{not derived from project-specified
or vendor data} and are flagged explicitly:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{CRAH coil $\Delta P$ (50.0\,kPa, assumed)}: no CRAH/in-row unit is
        specified anywhere in the project brief, and no vendor selection has been carried
        out for one (unlike the dry cooler, evaporator, condenser, and economizer, which
        all rest on a real Kelvion selection). 50\,kPa is a mid-range placeholder for a
        chilled-water fan-coil/CRAH unit at this flow (typical range 40--70\,kPa); it
        should be replaced with a real vendor coil $\Delta P$ once a CRAH unit is selected
        for the 8\,m\,$\times$\,12\,m, $\approx$25-rack data hall.
  \item \textbf{Piping/fittings (25.0\,kPa, assumed)}: the project brief states that
        \emph{pressure losses within the piping are to be neglected}, in contrast to the
        glycol loop chapter's own 65\,m equivalent-length calculation
        (\S\ref{pump:sec:piping}), which is a deliberate, documented deviation from that
        instruction for the glycol side. Per project decision, this term is
        \textbf{retained here too, as an explicit over-design margin} rather than set to
        zero --- i.e., this chapter intentionally selects against the more conservative
        82.7\,kPa total rather than the $\approx$58\,kPa total that would result from
        following the brief literally and dropping this term. If a real piping layout
        becomes available, this term should be replaced with an actual Darcy--Weisbach
        calculation, following the same method as \S\ref{pump:sec:piping}.
\end{itemize}

These two terms together make up $\approx$91\,\% of the total system head --- by far the
dominant uncertainty in this chapter, and the first items to revisit once real CRAH and
piping data exist.

\zonedivider{Part B \quad---\quad Vendor Selection and Variable-Speed Operation}

% ── §8.4 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Selection Margin}
\label{chwpump:sec:margin}

Rather than margining both flow and head as in the glycol chapter (needed there to cover
two distinct design points), a single \textbf{10\,\% head margin at the design flow} is
applied here, since this loop has only one design duty point:

\[
  Q_{\mathrm{design}} = 21.5\;\mathrm{m^3/h}\;(94.8\;\mathrm{GPM}), \qquad
  H_{\mathrm{select}} = 1.10 \times 8.45\;\mathrm{m} = 9.30\;\mathrm{m}\;(30.5\;\mathrm{ft})
\]

% ── §8.5 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Vendor Pump Curve and Trim Selection}
\label{chwpump:sec:curve}

Performance data for the \textbf{Bell \& Gossett e-1510 1.5AD} (1750\,RPM) were digitized
from the same vendor curve sheet (B-261G) used for the glycol pump, across five available
impeller trims (5.0--7.0\,in), and fit to a quadratic $H(Q) = c_2 Q^2 + c_1 Q + c_0$
(least squares, $Q$ in GPM, $H$ in ft --- the chart's native units). Each trim's
100\,\%-speed head at the design flow (94.8\,GPM) was checked against the margined target:

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Trim comparison at the design flow (94.8\,GPM), 1.5AD frame, 1750\,RPM.}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLL}
\toprule
\textbf{Trim} & \textbf{$H$ @ 94.8\,GPM} & \textbf{Clears 30.5\,ft margin?} \\
\midrule
5.0\,in & 13.8\,ft & No \\
5.5\,in & 18.6\,ft & No \\
6.0\,in & 24.3\,ft & No \\
6.5\,in & 29.5\,ft & No \\
\textbf{7.0\,in} & \textbf{34.0\,ft} & \textbf{Yes} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

The \textbf{7.0\,in trim} is the smallest of the five that clears the margined target and
is therefore selected. At 100\,\% speed (uncontrolled), the 7.0\,in curve intersects the
system curve of \S\ref{chwpump:sec:syscurve} at \textbf{102.0\,GPM @ 32.1\,ft} (23.17
m$^3$/h @ 9.79\,m) --- confirming the selected trim carries head/flow margin above the
actual design duty (94.8\,GPM @ 27.7\,ft), which the VFD then trims back down
(\S\ref{chwpump:sec:vfd}).

% ── §8.5b ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\subsection{Frame Size: Why 1.5AD Rather Than the Glycol Pump's Frame}
\label{chwpump:sec:frame}

The glycol pump (Chapter~\ref{ch:pump}) uses the larger \textbf{2.5AC} frame at its much
larger duty ($\approx$182\,GPM @ $\approx$47\,ft). Re-using that casing was considered for
parts commonality, but was rejected: at this pump's much smaller duty
($\approx$95\,GPM @ $\approx$28\,ft), the larger frame's composite coverage floor is 105\,GPM
(Table~\ref{tab:composite}, Chapter~\ref{ch:pump}) --- meaning our duty point falls
\emph{below} that frame's intended operating window entirely. At this flow the larger
casing would run at only $\approx$40--50\,\% efficiency (per the
B-261G contour lines), far to the left of its best-efficiency point --- an oversized-pump
penalty that works directly against the project's PUE target every one of the 8760
operating hours.

The smaller \textbf{1.5AD} frame's own composite envelope (66--151\,GPM, 16--50\,ft,
Chapter~\ref{ch:pump}'s Table~\ref{tab:composite}) brackets this pump's duty point
centrally rather than at an extreme, keeping the selected trim much closer to its own
best-efficiency region. The two pumps therefore use the same manufacturer and speed family
(Bell \& Gossett e-1510, 1750\,RPM) but different frame sizes --- a reasonable compromise
between parts commonality and avoiding a chronically oversized, inefficient pump.

% ── §8.6 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Variable-Speed Operation}
\label{chwpump:sec:vfd}

The VFD speed fraction $s$ required to bring the 100\,\%-speed curve down to the exact
design duty point is solved from

\[
  H_{100\%}\!\left(\frac{Q_{\mathrm{required}}}{s}\right) \cdot s^2 = H_{\mathrm{required}}
\]

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Required VFD speed fraction at the design operating point.}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLL}
\toprule
\textbf{Operating point} & \textbf{Duty} & \textbf{Required speed} \\
\midrule
Design point (exact) & 21.5\,m$^3$/h @ 8.45\,m & 92.9\,\% (1626\,RPM) \\
100\,\%-speed intersection & 23.17\,m$^3$/h @ 9.79\,m & 100\,\% (reference) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

92.9\,\% sits comfortably within normal VFD turndown range, confirming the 7.0\,in trim is
an appropriately tight selection rather than an oversized one.

\textbf{Open item --- low-load turndown:} at the 30\,kW minimum-IT-load condition, if CHW
flow is throttled proportionally with load (20\,\% of design flow, $\approx$19\,GPM), the
quadratic system curve collapses to $\approx$1.1\,ft of head --- an operating point far
below any of the digitized trim curves' normal range, and likely below the pump's minimum
continuous stable flow (risk of internal recirculation/thrust bearing wear at very low
flow, independent of the VFD's electrical turndown capability). This mirrors the
flow-control-strategy question already open on the glycol side
(\S\ref{pump:sec:vfd}) and should be resolved together with it --- e.g. via a minimum-flow
bypass, a fixed CHW flow with only the load-side $\Delta T$ varying, or a second, smaller
pump for the turndown condition.

% ── §8.7 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Power and Motor Selection}

\subsection{Efficiency}

Reading the B-261G contour lines for the 1.5AD, 7.0\,in trim near the design operating
region ($\approx$95--102\,GPM) gives an estimated pump efficiency of
\textbf{$\approx$60\,\%} --- lower than the glycol pump's 72--74\,\% (\S\ref{pump:sec:vfd}
motor selection discussion) because this duty point, while well inside the 1.5AD frame's
composite envelope, still sits to the left of that trim's own best-efficiency point. As
with the glycol pump, this reading carries more uncertainty than the digitized head data
and should be confirmed against manufacturer selection software before use in final
annual-energy/PUE calculations.

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Estimated CHW pump power at the design duty ($\eta_{\mathrm{pump}}=60\,\%$, vendor-curve-informed).}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLLL}
\toprule
\textbf{Duty} & \textbf{Hydraulic power} & \textbf{Shaft power} & \textbf{Electrical power (90\,\% motor eff.)} \\
\midrule
Design point (21.5\,m$^3$/h @ 8.45\,m) & 0.495\,kW & 0.825\,kW & 0.917\,kW \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\subsection{Motor Selection}

The required shaft power (0.825\,kW $\approx$ 1.11\,hp) rounds up to the next standard
NEMA frame size: \textbf{1.5\,hp, 1750\,RPM}. The resulting headroom
($\approx$1.12\,kW rated output against a 0.825\,kW requirement, $\approx$35\,\%) is
generous by normal NEMA practice, reflecting this chapter's deliberately conservative
(over-designed) system curve --- see \S\ref{chwpump:sec:crah}.

% ── §8.8 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Limitations and Open Items}

\begin{enumerate}
