  \item \textbf{No CRAH unit has been vendor-selected.} The 50\,kPa coil $\Delta P$ used
        throughout this chapter is a placeholder, not a datasheet value --- unlike every
        other major heat-transfer component in this report (dry cooler, evaporator,
        condenser, economizer), all of which rest on a real Kelvion selection. This is the
        single largest open item in this chapter and should be resolved first.
  \item \textbf{Piping/fittings (25\,kPa) is a project-decision override, not a
        calculation}, retained deliberately as an over-design margin against the project
        brief's instruction to neglect piping losses (\S\ref{chwpump:sec:crah}). If a real
        piping layout becomes available, replace with an actual Darcy--Weisbach
        calculation (same method as \S\ref{pump:sec:piping}) rather than this placeholder.
  \item \textbf{Pump efficiency ($\approx$60\,\%) is read from curve-sheet contour
        lines}, not a manufacturer-certified selection, and carries the same
        digitization uncertainty flagged for the glycol pump (\S\ref{pump:sec:vfd}).
  \item \textbf{Low-load (30\,kW turndown) flow/head combination has not been resolved}
        against either the CRAH's minimum flow requirement or this pump's minimum
        continuous stable flow (\S\ref{chwpump:sec:vfd}) --- this is the same open
        flow-control-strategy question already flagged on the glycol loop side and should
        be closed out together with it.
  \item \textbf{NPSH has not been checked} against the selected pump's NPSHr; the
        digitized vendor curve sheet's NPSHr trace was not extracted for this chapter.
  \item \textbf{Materials of construction} (mechanical seal elastomer, wetted metallurgy)
        have not been confirmed; since this loop carries plain water (not glycol), standard
        EPDM/bronze-fitted construction should suffice, but this has not been checked
        against the specific CHW water treatment program.
\end{enumerate}

% ── §8.9 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{References}

\begin{enumerate}
  \item Bell \& Gossett, \textit{Series e-1510 Composite Performance Curves}, Document
        B-261, Xylem Inc.
  \item Bell \& Gossett, \textit{Series e-1510 Individual Performance Curves}, Document
        B-261G, Xylem Inc., dated 12/30/2013.
\end{enumerate}


\chapter{Compressor Selection and Performance Modeling}
\label{ch:comp}
% ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

\zonedivider{Part A \quad---\quad Vendor Selection and Performance Map}

% ── §9.1 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Design Basis and Staging Strategy}

The 150\,kW cooling duty is carried by \textbf{two identical 75\,kW compressors in
parallel on a common suction and discharge manifold}, feeding a \textbf{single shared
refrigerant circuit} --- one condenser, one evaporator, one expansion valve. This
chapter covers the compressor selection and performance modeling; the two units are
assumed identical.

\begin{quote}
\noindent\textbf{Why one circuit, not two.} Two compressors could in principle each
serve their own 75\,kW circuit with its own exchangers and valve. They do not here, for
two reasons. First, the staging analysis below (\S\ref{comp:sec:staging}) depends on both
compressors sharing one $t_o$/$t_c$ --- which is only possible on a common manifold.
Second, and decisively, splitting the flow halves the duty each expansion valve sees and
drops it to 12.2\,\% open at minimum load, below the range in which it can hold superheat
(Chapter~\ref{ch:controls}, \S\ref{ctrl:sec:authority}). The single shared circuit keeps
the valve controllable across the full 5:1 turndown. Its cost --- that the exchangers and
the valve are unduplicated --- is stated plainly in \S\ref{imp:sec:architecture}.
\end{quote}

The staging rationale (developed alongside this selection):
\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Compressor redundancy}: losing one compressor leaves 50\,\% capacity
        available, versus 25\,\% for an unequal split --- a materially better
        partial-failure state for a data-center cooling system. Note this is
        \emph{compressor} redundancy only: with a single shared circuit, a fault in
        the condenser, evaporator or expansion valve stops the plant regardless
        (\S\ref{pc:sec:cons}).
  \item \textbf{Coarse capacity steps}: staging one or two units gives two capacity
        settings without any drive involvement. \textbf{They do not, on their own, cover
        the project's 5:1 turndown} --- at the design point one unit delivers 67\,\% of
        the load and two deliver 134\,\%, so staging alone cannot reach the 30\,kW
        minimum. That is what the VFDs are for, and the two mechanisms are complementary
        rather than alternative (\S\ref{comp:sec:staging}).
  \item \textbf{Parts commonality}: one compressor model, one spare-parts inventory,
        one service procedure.
\end{itemize}

% ── §9.2 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Vendor Selection}

\textbf{Bitzer 4FEP-35Z}, a semi-hermetic reciprocating compressor rated for R-290
(propane, ASHRAE safety class A3), was selected via Bitzer's own selection software.
Semi-hermetic construction was chosen specifically because it has no external shaft
seal --- a meaningful leak-path reduction for an A3-classified refrigerant, versus an
open-drive compressor.

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{The two conditions that matter for this unit. The left column is where it was
\emph{shortlisted} in Bitzer's software; the right is where it actually \emph{runs}.}
\label{tab:comp_points}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLL}
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Vendor selection point} & \textbf{Plant design point} \\
\midrule
Evaporating dew point, $t_o$ & 7.0\,\textdegree C & \textbf{10.0\,\textdegree C} \\
Condensing dew point, $t_c$ & 50.0\,\textdegree C & \textbf{45.0\,\textdegree C} \\
Useful superheat & 8\,K (15\,\textdegree C return gas) & 8\,K \\
Liquid subcooling & 0\,K (vendor default) & \textbf{5\,K} (\S\ref{cond:sec:subcool}) \\
Cooling capacity, per unit & 80.65\,kW & \textbf{100.39\,kW} \\
Power input, per unit & 22.74\,kW & \textbf{21.50\,kW} \\
Mass flow, per unit & 1{,}079.7\,kg/h & \textbf{1{,}232.1\,kg/h} \\
COP & 3.55 & \textbf{4.67} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\noindent \textbf{The two columns differ, and that is neither an error nor an
inconsistency --- it is the point of using a map.} The unit was shortlisted at a
conservative catalogue condition; the plant then runs it at an easier one (3\,K warmer
evaporating, 5\,K cooler condensing), where the same machine gives 24\,\% more capacity
at 5\,\% less power. Nothing in this report uses the left-hand column as a performance
figure: \emph{all} reported capacity, power and mass flow come from the ten-coefficient
map of \S\ref{comp:sec:map}, evaluated at the actual $t_o$/$t_c$, with the superheat and
subcooling corrections of \S\ref{comp:sec:correction}.

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Map validation.} Evaluated back at the vendor's own selection condition
($t_o=7$, $t_c=50$\,\textdegree C, EN12900 basis), the map returns \textbf{80.65\,kW,
22.74\,kW, COP 3.55} --- reproducing Bitzer's published figures exactly, to the last
digit. The map is therefore not an approximation of the vendor's data; it \emph{is} the
vendor's data, and the design-point column above is a genuine interpolation within the
same validated surface.
\end{quote}

% ── §9.3 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{AHRI Standard 540 / EN12900 Ten-Coefficient Performance Map}
\label{comp:sec:map}

Rather than a fixed isentropic/volumetric efficiency pair, compressor performance is
modeled directly from the manufacturer's published polynomial map, in the standard
AHRI-540/EN12900 form:

\[
  y = c_1 + c_2\,t_o + c_3\,t_c + c_4\,t_o^2 + c_5\,t_o t_c + c_6\,t_c^2
      + c_7\,t_o^3 + c_8\,t_c t_o^2 + c_9\,t_o t_c^2 + c_{10}\,t_c^3
\]

with $t_o$, $t_c$ the evaporating and condensing \emph{dew points} in \textdegree C, and
$y$ evaluated separately for cooling capacity $Q$ [W], power input $P$ [W], and mass
flow $\mdot$ [kg/h] using three independent coefficient sets (Table~\ref{tab:ahricoeff}).
This directly resolves the constant-efficiency limitation of the earlier compressor
model --- efficiency now varies correctly with pressure ratio (i.e. with $t_o$ and
$t_c$) since it is built into the polynomial's own curvature, rather than assumed
constant.

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{AHRI-540 coefficients, Bitzer 4FEP-35Z, R-290.}
\label{tab:ahricoeff}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLLL}
\toprule
& \textbf{$Q$ [W]} & \textbf{$P$ [W]} & \textbf{$\mdot$ [kg/h]} \\
\midrule
$c_1$  & 114372.76 & 7147.24  & 1009.99 \\
$c_2$  & 3903.60   & $-$239.01 & 36.10 \\
$c_3$  & $-$1121.94 & 255.72  & $-$4.34 \\
$c_4$  & 45.37     & $-$7.87  & 0.494 \\
$c_5$  & $-$33.97  & 8.65     & $-$0.0997 \\
$c_6$  & 3.40      & 1.62     & 0.0378 \\
$c_7$  & 0.168     & $-$0.0412 & 0.00287 \\
$c_8$  & $-$0.338  & 0.0698   & $-$0.00109 \\
$c_9$  & 0.00990   & 0.01026  & $-$0.0000239 \\
$c_{10}$ & $-$0.0256 & $-$0.0204 & $-$0.000382 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\textbf{Verification:} evaluating the map at the \emph{vendor's own rating point}
($t_o=7$, $t_c=50$\,\textdegree C) reproduces Bitzer's stated performance exactly
(80.65\,kW / 22.74\,kW / 1079.7\,kg/h, COP 3.55) --- confirming the transcribed
coefficients are correctly implemented. The plant's design point is elsewhere
($t_o=10$, $t_c=45$\,\textdegree C, Table~\ref{tab:comp_points}); this map is what
carries performance between the two.

% ── §9.4 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Rating-Basis Correction: EN12900 vs.\ Actual Application}
\label{comp:sec:correction}

The map coefficients reproduce performance at the \textbf{EN12900 standard rating
basis}: a suction (return) gas temperature fixed at 20\,\textdegree C \emph{absolute}
(not a fixed superheat --- the implied superheat therefore changes with $t_o$), and 0\,K
liquid subcooling. This is a testing/rating convention, not the actual application
operating point (8\,K useful superheat, 15\,\textdegree C actual return gas). A
density-ratio correction is applied:

\[
  \mathrm{DR} = \frac{\rho_{\mathrm{actual}}(T_{\mathrm{suction,actual}}, p_o)}
                     {\rho_{\mathrm{rated}}(20\,\text{\textdegree C}, p_o)}
\]

\begin{itemize}
  \item Mass flow scales directly with DR (same physical displacement and volumetric
        efficiency --- a denser suction gas simply packs more mass per stroke).
  \item Capacity is recomputed directly from the corrected mass flow and the
        \emph{actual} refrigeration effect ($h_{\mathrm{suction,actual}} -
        h_{\mathrm{liquid,actual}}$), which also captures the (smaller) enthalpy shift
        from the different suction temperature --- more rigorous than scaling capacity
        by DR alone.
  \item Power input is scaled by the same DR (standard simplified industry practice;
        specific compression work is only weakly sensitive to suction superheat over
        this range).
\end{itemize}

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Design-point performance: catalog (EN12900) vs.\ corrected (actual application).}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLL}
\toprule
& \textbf{Catalog (EN12900)} & \textbf{Corrected (8\,K superheat)} \\
\midrule
Cooling capacity & 80.65\,kW & 80.00\,kW \\
Power input & 22.74\,kW & 23.32\,kW \\
Mass flow & 1079.7\,kg/h & 1107.1\,kg/h \\
COP & 3.55 & 3.43 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

The correction is small at the design point ($\mathrm{DR}=1.025$) but is not
negligible --- COP drops from 3.55 to 3.43, which is worth carrying through rather than
using the uncorrected catalog numbers directly, especially since the correction grows
at operating points further from the rating condition (e.g.\ colder $t_o$, where the
20\,\textdegree C fixed return-gas rating implies a much larger rated superheat than
the actual 8\,K).

\zonedivider{Part B \quad---\quad Two-Compressor Bank: Staging and Evaporating-Temperature Solve}

% ── §9.5 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Load-Following Logic}
\label{comp:sec:staging}

Both compressors sit on a common suction/discharge manifold and therefore always share
the same $t_o$/$t_c$. Capacity is controlled by two mechanisms working together:
\textbf{staging} (how many units run) for coarse steps, and \textbf{drive speed} for
everything in between.

\subsection{Why Staging Alone Is Not Enough}
\label{comp:sec:staging_insufficient}

It is tempting to assume two equal compressors deliver the project's 5:1 turndown by
themselves. \textbf{They do not.} At the design point one unit produces 100.4\,kW and two
produce 200.8\,kW --- that is 67\,\% and 134\,\% of the 150\,kW load. Staging is a
two-position switch, not a dial, and neither position is 30\,kW.

A fixed-speed bank has exactly one further lever: let $t_o$ float down. Colder suction
vapour is less dense, so the machine swallows less mass per stroke and capacity falls.
That lever is real but it runs out, and it is expensive:

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{What a \emph{fixed-speed} bank can reach at the design condition
($t_c = 45$\,\textdegree C) by floating $t_o$ to its $-10$\,\textdegree C map limit.}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLLL}
\toprule
\textbf{Configuration} & \textbf{$t_o$} & \textbf{Capacity} & \textbf{\% of 150\,kW load} \\
\midrule
2 units, full speed & 10\,\textdegree C & 200.8\,kW & 134\,\% \\
1 unit, full speed & 10\,\textdegree C & 100.4\,kW & 67\,\% \\
1 unit, $t_o$ floated to the floor & $-10$\,\textdegree C & 48.2\,kW & 32\,\% \emph{(COP 2.43)} \\
\midrule
\textbf{Project minimum load} & --- & \textbf{30\,kW} & \textbf{20\,\%} --- unreachable \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\noindent Two things to read off this. First, \textbf{32\,\% is the floor} --- a
fixed-speed selection physically cannot reach the 30\,kW minimum, whatever the control
logic does. Second, even getting to 32\,\% costs a \textbf{COP of 2.43}, because boiling
refrigerant at $-10$\,\textdegree C to make 15\,\textdegree C chilled water is
thermodynamically absurd: the lift has been inflated to 55\,K to do \emph{less} work.
Below that floor the only fixed-speed options are on/off cycling or hot-gas bypass, which
throws capacity away as recirculated discharge gas at a similar efficiency penalty.

\subsection{The VFD Closes the Gap --- and Improves Efficiency Doing It}

A variable-speed drive attacks capacity from the opposite direction: instead of making the
refrigerant colder to choke the compressor, it simply turns the compressor \emph{slower}
and leaves $t_o$ where the evaporator wants it. The bank solve therefore holds
$t_o = 10$\,\textdegree C as a setpoint, picks the smallest $n_{\mathrm{active}}$ that can
cover the duty, and trims speed to land exactly on it
(\texttt{compressor.solve\_with\_vfd()}):

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Load-following with VFDs at the design condition ($t_c = 45$\,\textdegree C, i.e.\
a 35\,\textdegree C ambient with the IT load varying). Compare the bottom two rows against
the fixed-speed floor above.}
\label{tab:staging}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLLLLL}
\toprule
\textbf{$Q_{\mathrm{required}}$} & \textbf{$n_{\mathrm{active}}$} & \textbf{Drive speed} & \textbf{$Q_{\mathrm{achieved}}$} & \textbf{$P_{\mathrm{total}}$} & \textbf{COP} \\
\midrule
150.0\,kW (100\,\%) & 2 & 74.7\,\% & 150.0\,kW & 32.12\,kW & 4.67 \\
112.5\,kW (75\,\%)  & 2 & 56.0\,\% & 112.5\,kW & 24.09\,kW & 4.67 \\
75.0\,kW (50\,\%)   & 1 & 74.7\,\% & 75.0\,kW & 16.06\,kW & 4.67 \\
37.5\,kW (25\,\%)   & 1 & 37.4\,\% & 37.5\,kW & 8.03\,kW & 4.67 \\
\textbf{30.0\,kW (min.\ IT load)} & \textbf{1} & \textbf{29.9\,\%} & \textbf{30.0\,kW} & \textbf{6.42\,kW} & \textbf{4.67} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\noindent The 30\,kW minimum is reached at 29.9\,\% speed on a single unit --- and at
\textbf{COP 4.67 rather than 2.43}, because $t_o$ never leaves 10\,\textdegree C. The
whole 5:1 turndown is covered with no hot-gas bypass and no cycling penalty. Note also
the 100\,\% row: the bank is 34\,\% oversized at this condition, so even at full load the
drive is trimming to 75\,\% rather than letting $t_o$ sink.

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Caveat, stated:} COP is held speed-invariant here (capacity and power both scale
$\approx$linearly with speed for a reciprocating machine, so their ratio holds). Real
part-load COP at moderate turndown is typically equal or slightly better, so this is
neutral-to-conservative. Note too that the 30\,kW point sits at 29.9\,\% speed, right at
the $\approx$30\,\% floor a drive can hold continuously --- at or below that the machine
\textbf{cycles} rather than modulates. Ideal cycling gives the same average power, but
cycling losses are not modelled (\S\ref{econ:sec:partialmodel} makes the same point for
the partial free-cooling band).
\end{quote}

% ── §9.6 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{The Turndown Floor, and Why the Design Carries Drives}
\label{comp:sec:floor}

\S\ref{comp:sec:staging_insufficient} establishes the finding that drove this chapter's
most consequential decision: \textbf{a fixed-speed selection of this compressor cannot
meet the project's 30\,kW minimum load.} The floor sits at $\approx$32\,\% of design
duty, above the 20\,\% required, and it is a genuine physical limit rather than a solver
artifact --- a fixed-speed, non-unloading reciprocating compressor has a bounded capacity
range as a function of $t_o$ alone.

Five strategies were available:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{VFDs on the compressors} --- \textbf{selected.} Capacity follows speed, so
        $t_o$ stays at its setpoint and COP holds at 4.67 all the way to 30\,kW
        (Table~\ref{tab:staging}). Standard, well-understood hardware, and the same drives
        also trim the bank's 34\,\% oversize at full load and lift the 25\,\% IPLV point
        (\S\ref{perf:sec:iplv}). It is the reason both the full-load and IPLV targets are
        met.
  \item \textbf{Cylinder unloading} on the 4FEP-35Z --- would step capacity down discretely
        at fixed $t_o$, but only in coarse steps and only if offered for this model.
        Rejected in favour of continuous control.
  \item \textbf{Hot-gas bypass} --- reaches any load, at a severe efficiency penalty:
        capacity is thrown away as recirculated discharge gas while the compressor still
        draws near-full power. It would collapse the 25\,\% IPLV point to COP 2.55
        (against 4.35 with the VFD, \S\ref{perf:sec:iplv}). Rejected.
  \item \textbf{A smaller trim/pony compressor} for the 20--30\,\% range --- a third
        machine, a third set of spares, more charge, more failure modes. Rejected as
        disproportionate.
  \item \textbf{Leaning on the economizer} to cover low-load hours --- \textbf{rejected as
        unsafe reasoning}, and worth stating why: it assumes low IT load coincides with
        cold ambient, and \textbf{nothing guarantees that}. A quiet compute night in July
        is exactly the 30\,kW / 35\,\textdegree C case the economizer cannot help with.
        The plant must reach 30\,kW on mechanical cooling alone, which is precisely what
        Table~\ref{tab:staging} demonstrates.
\end{itemize}

\noindent \textbf{This open item is closed.} The drives appear in the BOM
(Ch.~\ref{ch:bom}), the VFD operating model is used throughout
(\texttt{compressor.solve\_with\_vfd()}), and the annual simulation runs cleanly through
the minimum-load hours.

% ── §9.7 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Limitations and Open Items}
\label{comp:sec:limitations}

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{The selection point is not the design point}, by construction
        (Table~\ref{tab:comp_points}). The unit was shortlisted at
        $t_c=50\,$\textdegree C / 0\,K subcooling and runs at 45\,\textdegree C / 5\,K.
        This is \emph{not} an inconsistency --- every performance figure in this report
        comes from the map evaluated at the actual condition, and the map reproduces
        Bitzer's published numbers exactly at their own rating point. What remains worth
        doing is a confirmation re-run in Bitzer's software \emph{at}
        $t_o=10$/$t_c=45$\,\textdegree C with 5\,K subcooling, to check the map is being
        interpolated inside the unit's real application envelope rather than merely
        inside its polynomial's numeric range.
  \item \textbf{Useful superheat (8\,K)} matches the vendor's own selection basis and is
        used consistently throughout, but it has not been confirmed against a tuned EEV
        superheat control loop; Chapter~\ref{ch:controls} argues the setpoint from
        compressor protection and loop stability rather than from the map, and
        \S\ref{ctrl:sec:superheat} explains why the map's own superheat trend is
        misleading.
  \item \textbf{The $t_o \in [-10, 15]\,$\textdegree C} solve bound is an assumption
        made for this chapter, not a validated compressor operating envelope limit;
        the actual minimum/maximum $t_o$ the 4FEP-35Z can run at (oil return, motor
        cooling at low suction density, etc.) should be confirmed against Bitzer's
        application manual.
  \item \textbf{Power-input correction (density-ratio scaling)} is a simplified
        industry approximation, not a first-principles recomputation of compression
        work at the actual suction state; acceptable for this stage but worth
        revisiting once a full annual-energy number is needed.
\end{enumerate}

% ── §9.8 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{References}

\begin{enumerate}
  \item Bitzer, \textit{Semi-Hermetic Reciprocating Compressors for R-290, Series
        ECOLINE}, selection software output, model 4FEP-35Z.
  \item AHRI Standard 540, \textit{Performance Rating of Positive Displacement
        Refrigerant Compressors and Compressor Units}, Air-Conditioning, Heating, and
        Refrigeration Institute.
  \item EN 12900, \textit{Refrigerant compressors -- Rating conditions, tolerances and
        presentation of manufacturer's performance data}, European Committee for
        Standardization.
\end{enumerate}

% ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
\chapter{Oil Separator Selection}
\label{ch:oilsep}
% ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

\zonedivider{Part A \quad---\quad Design Basis and Vendor Selection}

% ── §9b.1 ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Purpose and Design Basis}
\label{oilsep:sec:basis}

The compressor (Chapter~\ref{ch:comp}) circulates lubricating oil with the refrigerant;
some fraction of that oil leaves the compressor entrained in the discharge vapour. An
oil separator is installed immediately downstream of the discharge manifold to recover
this oil and return it to the compressor(s), preventing oil fouling of the condenser and
evaporator heat-transfer surfaces and protecting the compressor from running oil-lean.

Both compressors (Chapter~\ref{ch:comp}, \S\ref{comp:sec:staging}) share a common
discharge manifold, so a single separator sized for the combined discharge flow is used
rather than one unit per compressor.

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Oil separator design basis (combined, both compressors).}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lL}
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Value} \\
\midrule
Refrigerant & Propane (R-290), HC, ASHRAE safety class A3 \\
Compressor arrangement & 2\,$\times$ Bitzer 4FEP-35Z, common discharge manifold \\
Displacement, per unit / total & 101.8 / 203.6\,m\textsuperscript{3}/h (50\,Hz, catalog) \\
Mass flow, per unit (corrected, \S\ref{comp:sec:correction}) & 1107.1\,kg/h \\
\textbf{Mass flow, total (both units)} & \textbf{2214.2\,kg/h} \\
Condensing dew point, $t_c$ (compressor selection basis) & 50.0\,\textdegree C \\
Discharge pressure, $p_c$ (compressor selection basis) & 17.3\,bar(a)\textsuperscript{*} \\
Standby (off-cycle) pressure basis, 55\,\textdegree C ambient & $\approx$19.9\,bar(a) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}

{\small \textsuperscript{*}Taken at the compressor's $t_c=50\,$\textdegree C selection
basis (Table~\ref{tab:comp_points}). The plant condenses at 45\,\textdegree C
$=$ 15.3\,bar(a), so this figure is \textbf{conservative} --- the separator is specified
against a higher discharge pressure than it will ever see in service.}
\end{table}

\textbf{Total mass flow} is taken as the sum of both compressors' corrected
(actual-application) rated flow from \S\ref{comp:sec:correction}, not the raw EN12900
catalog figure --- consistent with how mass flow is used elsewhere in this report
(Chapter~\ref{ch:eev}, \S\ref{eev:sec:inlet}). \textbf{Displacement} (a fixed geometric
property, unaffected by the EN12900-vs.-actual correction) is used separately below as
the basis for the vendor's quick-selection chart.

% ── §9b.2 ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Vendor Selection: Bitzer OA Series}
\label{oilsep:sec:vendor}

Staying with \textbf{Bitzer} for the oil separator keeps the plant on a single
compressor/accessory vendor for procurement, spares, and application-data consistency
--- the same parts-commonality rationale already applied to the compressor selection
(Chapter~\ref{ch:comp}, \S9.1) and to the dry cooler and pump vendor choices.

Bitzer's \textbf{OA series} (models OA1954 through OA25112) are primary oil separators
explicitly approved for A1, A2, A2L, and A3 refrigerants, rated to a maximum allowable
pressure of 28\,bar(a) and a temperature range of $-10$ to $120\,$\textdegree C
(Table~\ref{tab:oatable}). The 28\,bar rating carries substantial margin over the
$\approx$19.9\,bar(a) R-290 standby pressure basis (\S\ref{oilsep:sec:basis}), which
governs vessel design pressure more than the running condensing pressure does.

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Bitzer OA-series primary oil separators, permitted fluids and pressure ratings
(manufacturer data, per EU Pressure Equipment Directive 2014/68/EU).}
\label{tab:oatable}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLLLL}
\toprule
\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Safety class} & \textbf{PS (max.\ pressure)} & \textbf{TS (temp.\ range)} \\
\midrule
OA1954  & A1, A2, A2L, A3 & 28\,bar & $-$10 / 120\,\textdegree C \\
OA4188  & A1, A2, A2L, A3 & 28\,bar & $-$10 / 120\,\textdegree C \\
OA9111  & A1, A2, A2L, A3 & 28\,bar & $-$10 / 120\,\textdegree C \\
OA14111 & A1, A2, A2L, A3 & 28\,bar & $-$10 / 120\,\textdegree C \\
OA25112 & A1, A2, A2L, A3 & 28\,bar & $-$10 / 120\,\textdegree C \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\subsection{Quick-Selection Cross-Check on Displacement}
\label{oilsep:sec:quickselect}

Bitzer's published quick-selection chart sizes each OA model by maximum suction volume
flow (theoretical displacement), tabulated for HFC refrigerants (R22, R134a, R404A,
R507A) rather than R-290 directly:

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{OA-series quick-selection envelope (HFC basis) vs.\ this project's displacement.}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lL}
\toprule
\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Max.\ suction volume flow (HFC basis)} \\
\midrule
\textbf{OA1954} & \textbf{250 -- 300\,m\textsuperscript{3}/h} \\
OA4188  & 580 -- 660\,m\textsuperscript{3}/h \\
OA9111  & 1160 -- 1320\,m\textsuperscript{3}/h \\
OA14111 & 1320\,m\textsuperscript{3}/h \\
OA25112 & 2050 -- 2500\,m\textsuperscript{3}/h \\
\midrule
\textbf{This project (2\,$\times$\,4FEP-35Z, combined)} & \textbf{203.6\,m\textsuperscript{3}/h} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

At 203.6\,m\textsuperscript{3}/h combined displacement, the project's duty falls well
inside the smallest model's envelope (OA1954, rated to 250--300\,m\textsuperscript{3}/h),
with the next size up (OA4188) rated nearly 3$\times$ the required flow --- confirming
OA1954 as the appropriate size class rather than a marginal selection.

\zonedivider{Part B \quad---\quad Safety, Oil Return, and Open Items}

% ── §9b.3 ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Primary-Only: No Secondary Separator Required}
\label{oilsep:sec:primaryonly}

Bitzer's secondary/fine-filter oil separators (OAS series) and combined units (OAC
series) exist primarily to serve \textbf{NH\textsubscript{3} (R-717)} systems: ammonia is
effectively immiscible with the mineral/synthetic oils used in ammonia compressors, so a
single primary separator cannot reliably strip the last traces of oil mist, and a
secondary fine-filter stage is standard practice.

R-290 is the opposite case: propane is \textbf{highly miscible} with the polyolester oil
used in the 4FEP-35Z (Chapter~\ref{ch:comp}), which is itself a key reason hydrocarbon
refrigerant/oil systems achieve good primary separation efficiency ($\geq$99\,\% typical)
without a secondary stage. Accordingly, only the primary OA1954 unit is carried in the
BOM; no OAS/OAC secondary or combined separator is included.

% ── §9b.4 ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Oil Return and A3 Ignition-Source Avoidance}
\label{oilsep:sec:oilreturn}

The oil return line from the separator back to the compressor crankcase should use a
\textbf{float-actuated mechanical return} rather than an electrically energized solenoid
valve. For an A3-classified refrigerant, this avoids introducing an electrical
ignition source directly in the refrigerant-wetted oil-return path; if an electric
solenoid oil-return valve is used elsewhere in the design, it must carry ATEX (or
equivalent) explosion-protection certification. This is carried forward as a design
constraint for the P\&ID and control-interaction discussion (still outstanding per the
project's overall open-items list).

The separator's oil heater (1\,$\times$\,140\,W, \S\ref{oilsep:sec:selected}) must remain
energized whenever the compressors are at standstill, consistent with Bitzer's general
guidance for hydrocarbon refrigerants: insufficient oil temperature during shut-off
allows excess refrigerant to dissolve into the oil, reducing viscosity at the next
start and increasing oil carry-over.

% ── §9b.5 ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Limitations and Open Items}
\label{oilsep:sec:limitations}

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Selection basis is the compressor's $t_c=50\,$\textdegree C rating point,
        not the plant's 45\,\textdegree C design point} (Table~\ref{tab:comp_points}).
        This errs the safe way: the real discharge pressure is 15.3\,bar(a) against the
        17.3\,bar(a) specified, and the real swept volume is lower. Combined with the
        OA1954's roughly 3$\times$ margin over the required displacement
        (\S\ref{oilsep:sec:quickselect}), re-running at the design point cannot change
        the model selected --- it would only widen an already-large margin.
  \item \textbf{Quick-selection chart is HFC-basis, not R-290-specific}: the displacement
        comparison in \S\ref{oilsep:sec:quickselect} uses Bitzer's published HFC
        (R22/R134a/R404A/R507A) quick-selection envelope as a conservative cross-check,
        since a public R-290-specific capacity table was not available. Given the large
        margin found, this is expected to hold, but the \textbf{final model should be
        confirmed by re-running the selection in Bitzer's selection software with R-290
        entered directly} --- the same software already used for the compressor
        selection (Chapter~\ref{ch:comp}, Reference 1) --- before the model is locked
        into the BOM.
  \item \textbf{Common discharge manifold is assumed}, consistent with
        Chapter~\ref{ch:comp}, \S\ref{comp:sec:staging}; if the two refrigerant circuits
        are ultimately kept fully independent instead, this would require two smaller
        separators (one per compressor) rather than one shared unit, and the sizing in
        \S\ref{oilsep:sec:quickselect} would need to be re-split accordingly.
  \item \textbf{Discharge-line connection size (DN\,50 at the separator) has not yet been
        cross-checked} against the refrigerant discharge-line sizing in
        Chapter~\ref{ch:piping}; this should be reconciled so the separator's
        connections and the selected discharge pipe size are consistent.
  \item \textbf{Oil-return solenoid vs.\ float-valve decision} (\S\ref{oilsep:sec:oilreturn})
        is a design intent, not yet reflected in a finalized P\&ID or BOM line item.
\end{enumerate}

% ── §9b.6 ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{References}

\begin{enumerate}
  \item Bitzer, \textit{Pressure vessels: Liquid receivers and oil separators}, Operating
        Instructions DB-300-9, Order no.\ 80491202, 01.08.2018 --- permitted fluids,
        pressure/temperature ratings, and connection dimensions for the OA series.
  \item Bitzer, \textit{Oil Separators} (Primary and secondary oil separators),
        Brochure DP-500-2, 09.2010 --- HFC-basis quick-selection chart
        (\S\ref{oilsep:sec:quickselect}).
  \item Bitzer, \textit{Oils for hydrocarbon refrigerants (R290, R600a, etc.)}, Technical
        Information KT-500 --- oil miscibility and oil-heater guidance for R-290 systems.
  \item EN 378, \textit{Refrigerating systems and heat pumps --- Safety and environmental
        requirements} --- basis for A3 safety-class handling referenced throughout this
        chapter.
\end{enumerate}


% ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
\chapter{System Piping --- Refrigerant, Glycol, and Chilled-Water Line Sizing}
\label{ch:piping}
% ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

\zonedivider{Part A \quad---\quad Refrigerant Charge Lines (Table 1 Method)}

% ── §10.1 ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Scope and Method}
\label{piping:sec:scope}

The project brief scopes piping to \textbf{diameters only --- no lengths, fittings, or
supports} (subtask 1d), and separately instructs that pressure losses within the piping
are to be neglected. This chapter closes that deliverable for all three fluid circuits in
the system:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item The \textbf{R-290 refrigerant circuit} --- suction, discharge (hot-gas), and
        liquid lines connecting the compressor, condenser, and evaporator
        (Chapters~\ref{ch:comp}, \ref{ch:cond}, \ref{ch:evap}).
  \item The \textbf{glycol loop} --- already partially sized in
        Chapter~\ref{ch:pump} (\S\ref{pump:sec:piping}); restated and cross-checked here.
  \item The \textbf{chilled-water loop} --- sized here for the first time.
\end{enumerate}

For the refrigerant circuit, sizing follows the project's \textbf{Table 1} streaming
velocities, reproduced below, which vary by refrigerant \emph{state} rather than by loop:

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Streaming velocities within charge lines (project brief, Table 1).}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lL}
\toprule
\textbf{State} & \textbf{Velocity, $v$} \\
\midrule
Gaseous --- low pressure  & 4.5--20\,m/s \\
Gaseous --- high pressure & 10--18\,m/s \\
Liquid                    & $<1.5$\,m/s \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\noindent No equivalent table exists for the glycol or chilled-water loops, so those two
circuits instead follow the conventional closed-loop hydronic velocity guideline
(1--2.4\,m/s) already adopted in Chapters~\ref{ch:pump} and \ref{ch:chwpump}, for
consistency with those chapters' pump selections.

\textbf{Common method for all six lines below:}

\begin{equation}
  D_{\mathrm{req}} = \sqrt{\frac{4\,\mdot}{\pi\,\rho\,v_{\mathrm{design}}}}
  \tag{Eq.\,P.1}
\end{equation}

\noindent A design velocity $v_{\mathrm{design}}$ is chosen inside the applicable band,
$D_{\mathrm{req}}$ is computed from the design mass flow and fluid density at that state,
and the result is \textbf{rounded up} to the nearest standard tube/pipe size (ACR copper
for refrigerant lines, Schedule 40 steel for the glycol/CHW loops, matching
Chapter~\ref{ch:pump}'s choice of material). The actual velocity at the selected standard
size is then rechecked against the band.

% ── §10.2 ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Refrigerant State Points and Properties}
\label{piping:sec:states}

Refrigerant mass flow is the confirmed design-point value from the compressor chapter,
$\mdot_r = 0.511$\,kg/s (150\,kW $\div$ the 293.3\,kJ/kg refrigerating effect,
Chapter~\ref{ch:comp}), superseding the earlier provisional 0.553\,kg/s. The state points
are the revised cycle's ($t_o = 10$, $t_c = 45$\,\textdegree C). Three state points bound
the circuit:

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Refrigerant state points used for line sizing (CoolProp, R-290).}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLLL}
\toprule
\textbf{Line} & \textbf{State} & \textbf{$T$} & \textbf{$p$} \\
\midrule
Suction     & 10\,\textdegree C sat.\ + 8\,K superheat & 18.0\,\textdegree C & 6.366\,bar(a) \\
Discharge   & compressor outlet (desuperheated approach, Ch.~\ref{ch:cond}) & 64.8\,\textdegree C & 15.343\,bar(a) \\
Liquid      & condenser outlet, 5\,K subcooled        & 40.0\,\textdegree C & 15.343\,bar(a) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Fluid properties at each state point (CoolProp, R-290).}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLL}
\toprule
\textbf{Line} & \textbf{Density, $\rho$} & \textbf{Dynamic viscosity, $\mu$} \\
\midrule
Suction     & 13.20\,kg/m$^3$  & $7.98\times10^{-6}$\,Pa\,s \\
Discharge   & 29.81\,kg/m$^3$  & $9.60\times10^{-6}$\,Pa\,s \\
Liquid      & 468.18\,kg/m$^3$  & $8.32\times10^{-5}$\,Pa\,s \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\noindent The suction-line density is notably low --- a direct consequence of propane's
low molar mass and the modest 6.37\,bar(a) evaporating pressure. This is a known
characteristic of R-290 systems relative to higher-pressure synthetic refrigerants and is
the reason the suction line below sizes out so much larger than the discharge or liquid
lines despite carrying the same mass flow.

% ── §10.3 ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Suction Line}
\label{piping:sec:suction}

The suction line falls in Table~1's \textbf{gaseous, low-pressure} band (4.5--20\,m/s). A
design velocity toward the upper-middle of the band is chosen to keep the line from
sizing excessively large, while retaining margin below the 20\,m/s ceiling (noise,
erosion) and comfortably above the floor (oil entrainment back to the compressor):

\[
  v_{\mathrm{design}} = 15\;\mathrm{m/s} \quad \Rightarrow \quad
  D_{\mathrm{req}} = \sqrt{\frac{4 \times 0.511}{\pi \times 13.20 \times 15}}
  = 57.3\;\mathrm{mm}
\]

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Suction line selection.}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lL}
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Value} \\
\midrule
Required ID (design basis)     & 57.3\,mm \\
\textbf{Selected tube}          & \textbf{2-5/8\,in ACR copper, Type L} \\
Actual ID                       & 62.87\,mm \\
Actual velocity                & 12.48\,m/s \\
Reynolds number                & $\approx 1.30\times10^{6}$ (fully turbulent) \\
\textbf{In Table 1 band?}       & \textbf{Yes} (4.5--20\,m/s) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

% ── §10.4 ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Discharge (Hot-Gas) Line}
\label{piping:sec:discharge}

The discharge line falls in Table~1's \textbf{gaseous, high-pressure} band (10--18\,m/s).
A design velocity of 14\,m/s is chosen specifically because it is the value that lands
the required diameter just inside the next standard tube size without excess margin
(\S\ref{piping:sec:tuning}):

\[
  v_{\mathrm{design}} = 14\;\mathrm{m/s} \quad \Rightarrow \quad
  D_{\mathrm{req}} = \sqrt{\frac{4 \times 0.511}{\pi \times 29.81 \times 14}}
  = 39.5\;\mathrm{mm}
\]

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Discharge line selection.}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lL}
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Value} \\
\midrule
Required ID (design basis)     & 39.5\,mm \\
\textbf{Selected tube}          & \textbf{1-5/8\,in ACR copper, Type L} \\
Actual ID                       & 38.23\,mm \\
Actual velocity                & 14.95\,m/s \\
Reynolds number                & $\approx 1.75\times10^{6}$ (fully turbulent) \\
\textbf{In Table 1 band?}       & \textbf{Yes} (10--18\,m/s) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

% ── §10.5 ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Liquid Line}
\label{piping:sec:liquid}

The liquid line falls in Table~1's \textbf{liquid} band ($<1.5$\,m/s, no stated floor).

\[
  v_{\mathrm{design}} = 1.2\;\mathrm{m/s} \quad \Rightarrow \quad
  D_{\mathrm{req}} = \sqrt{\frac{4 \times 0.511}{\pi \times 468.18 \times 1.2}}
  = 34.0\;\mathrm{mm}
\]

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Liquid line: candidate tube sizes at the confirmed 0.511\,kg/s flow.}
\label{tab:liquid_candidates}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLLL}
\toprule
\textbf{Candidate} & \textbf{ID} & \textbf{Velocity} & \textbf{Margin to 1.5\,m/s} \\
\midrule
\textbf{1-3/8\,in ACR Cu (selected)} & \textbf{33.88\,mm} & \textbf{1.21\,m/s} & \textbf{$\approx$19\,\%} \\
1-5/8\,in ACR Cu & 38.23\,mm & 0.95\,m/s & $\approx$37\,\% (over-sized) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\textbf{1-3/8\,in is selected.} At the confirmed design flow of 0.511\,kg/s (from the
completed compressor chapter, lower than the earlier provisional 0.553\,kg/s), 1-3/8\,in
gives 1.21\,m/s with a comfortable 19\,\% margin to the 1.5\,m/s ceiling. The next size up
(1-5/8\,in) would run at only 0.95\,m/s --- unnecessarily over-sized, and it holds more
of the dense liquid that dominates the A3 charge inventory (Chapter~\ref{ch:safety}), so
the smaller line is preferred on both counts.

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Liquid line selection (as adopted).}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lL}
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Value} \\
\midrule
Required ID (design basis)     & 34.0\,mm \\
\textbf{Selected tube}          & \textbf{1-3/8\,in ACR copper, Type L} \\
Actual ID                       & 33.88\,mm \\
Actual velocity                & 1.21\,m/s \\
Reynolds number                & $\approx 2.6\times10^{5}$ (fully turbulent) \\
\textbf{In Table 1 band?}       & \textbf{Yes} ($<1.5$\,m/s) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

% ── §10.6 ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Refrigerant Circuit --- Summary}
\label{piping:sec:tuning}

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Refrigerant line sizing summary, $\mdot_r = 0.511$\,kg/s throughout.}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLLLL}
\toprule
\textbf{Line} & \textbf{Table 1 band} & \textbf{Selected tube} & \textbf{Actual ID} & \textbf{Actual $v$} \\
\midrule
Suction              & 4.5--20\,m/s & 2-5/8\,in ACR Cu & 62.87\,mm & 12.48\,m/s \\
Discharge (hot gas)   & 10--18\,m/s  & 1-5/8\,in ACR Cu & 38.23\,mm & 14.95\,m/s \\
Liquid                & $<1.5$\,m/s  & 1-3/8\,in ACR Cu & 33.88\,mm & 1.21\,m/s \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

Note the discharge (1-5/8\,in) and liquid (1-3/8\,in) lines come out only one nominal
size apart despite carrying the same mass flow at wildly different densities --- the dense
subcooled liquid needs far less area than the hot discharge gas, but the low target
velocity for liquid ($<1.5$\,m/s vs.\ 14\,m/s) nearly cancels that, leaving the two lines
close in size.

\zonedivider{Part B \quad---\quad Secondary-Fluid Loop Piping}

% ── §10.7 ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Glycol Loop Piping}
\label{piping:sec:glycol}

The glycol loop diameter was established in Chapter~\ref{ch:pump}
(\S\ref{pump:sec:piping}), sized against the higher of its two design flows (mechanical
mode, 11.64\,kg/s):

\[
  v_{\mathrm{design}} \approx 2.4\;\mathrm{m/s} \quad \Rightarrow \quad
  \textbf{3\,in Schedule 40 steel (77.9\,mm ID)}
\]

This chapter re-checks that same pipe against \emph{both} glycol-loop operating modes:

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Glycol loop pipe check, 3\,in Schedule 40 steel (77.9\,mm ID), both modes.}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLLLL}
\toprule
\textbf{Mode} & \textbf{Flow} & \textbf{Density} & \textbf{Velocity} & \textbf{Reynolds} \\
\midrule
Mechanical (dry cooler $\leftrightarrow$ condenser) & 11.64\,kg/s & 1013.4\,kg/m$^3$ & 2.41\,m/s & $\approx1.21\times10^{5}$ \\
Free-cooling (dry cooler $\leftrightarrow$ economizer) & 6.52\,kg/s & 1027.2\,kg/m$^3$ & 1.33\,m/s & $\approx2.62\times10^{4}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

Both modes fall inside the 1--2.4\,m/s hydronic guideline through the \emph{same} fixed
pipe. Mechanical mode now sits \textbf{right at the 2.4\,m/s ceiling} (2.41\,m/s), a
direct consequence of the 4\,K glycol range demanding a high flow; 3\,in is retained as
acceptable, but a 4\,in line would be the conservative choice if pumping loss or noise
became a concern. Free-cooling mode, at the lower economizer flow, has ample margin. This
is consistent with, but independent of, the still-open glycol-flow-rate reconciliation
flagged in
Chapter~\ref{ch:dry} (\S\ref{dry:sec:openflow}): whichever flow the eventual VFD control
strategy settles on, both candidate values check out against this pipe size.

% ── §10.8 ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Chilled-Water Loop Piping}
\label{piping:sec:chw}

The chilled-water loop carries a single design flow
($\mdot_{\mathrm{water}} = 5.97$\,kg/s, common to both the evaporator and economizer
branches per Chapter~\ref{ch:chwpump}), so only one diameter is required:

\[
  v_{\mathrm{design}} \approx 2.0\;\mathrm{m/s}, \qquad \rho_{\mathrm{18^\circ C}} = 998.6\;\mathrm{kg/m^3}
  \quad \Rightarrow \quad
  D_{\mathrm{req}} = \sqrt{\frac{4 \times 5.97}{\pi \times 998.6 \times 2.0}} = 61.7\;\mathrm{mm}
\]

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Chilled-water loop pipe selection.}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lL}
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Value} \\
\midrule
Required ID (design basis)     & 61.7\,mm \\
\textbf{Selected pipe}          & \textbf{2-1/2\,in Schedule 40 steel} \\
Actual ID                       & 62.71\,mm \\
Actual velocity                & 1.94\,m/s \\
Reynolds number                & $\approx1.15\times10^{5}$ (fully turbulent) \\
\textbf{In hydronic guideline?} & \textbf{Yes} (1--2.4\,m/s), centrally placed \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

This diameter is independent of the 25\,kPa piping/fittings \emph{pressure-drop} allowance
already carried as a placeholder in Chapter~\ref{ch:chwpump} (\S\ref{chwpump:sec:crah});
that allowance is a lumped margin standing in for real geometry, whereas this diameter is
the actual project-brief deliverable (subtask 1d). If a real piping layout is developed,
the 2-1/2\,in ID above --- together with an equivalent length, following
Chapter~\ref{ch:pump}'s Darcy--Weisbach method (\S\ref{pump:sec:piping}) --- would let that
placeholder be replaced with a calculated value.
