% ── §10.10 ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Limitations and Open Items}
\label{piping:sec:limitations}

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Refrigerant mass flow is the confirmed 0.511\,kg/s} from the completed
        compressor chapter (150\,kW $\div$ 293.3\,kJ/kg refrigerating effect), and all
        three lines are sized at it, at the revised cycle's state points ($t_o=10$,
        $t_c=45$\,\textdegree C). \emph{Note: the condenser and EEV chapters still quote the
        earlier provisional 0.553\,kg/s for their channel-count and $K_v$ sizing; that
        geometry retains ample margin at the lower confirmed flow, but those two chapters
        are the ones still to be brought onto the confirmed value.}
  \item \textbf{Liquid line is 1-3/8\,in} (1.21\,m/s, 19\,\% margin to the 1.5\,m/s
        ceiling at the confirmed flow), matching the bill of materials
        (Ch.~\ref{ch:bom}) and the charge inventory (Ch.~\ref{ch:safety}). The larger
        1-5/8\,in would be over-sized and would hold more A3 charge, so 1-3/8\,in is
        preferred.
  \item \textbf{No lengths, fittings, elevation, or supports are sized here}, per the
        project brief's explicit scope (``diameters only''). The glycol loop's 65\,m
        equivalent-length pressure-drop calculation (Chapter~\ref{ch:pump},
        \S\ref{pump:sec:piping}) and the chilled-water loop's 25\,kPa placeholder
        (Chapter~\ref{ch:chwpump}, \S\ref{chwpump:sec:crah}) are deliberate, documented
        deviations from that scope for pump-selection purposes only, and should not be
        read as this chapter's deliverable.
  \item \textbf{Refrigerant tube material/rating (copper vs.\ steel, pressure class) has
        not been checked against R-290's A3 safety classification} or the discharge-line
        pressure (17.89\,bar(a)); ACR copper is assumed adequate at this scale but should
        be confirmed against applicable refrigeration piping codes before procurement.
  \item \textbf{Glycol and chilled-water pipe sizing uses generic hydronic velocity
        practice (1--2.4\,m/s)}, since the project brief's Table 1 does not cover
        secondary fluids; this is a reasonable industry default but is not itself a
        project-specified requirement, unlike the refrigerant-line velocities.
\end{enumerate}

% ── §10.11 ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{References}

\begin{enumerate}
  \item Project \#3 brief, IRCC 2026: Table 1, ``Streaming velocities within charge
        lines,'' and subtask 1d, ``Piping (diameters only --- no lengths, fittings, or
        supports).''
  \item ASHRAE Handbook --- Refrigeration (2006): basis for Table 1's velocity ranges
        (cost, efficiency, noise, and oil circulation rate trade-offs).
  \item ASTM B88, \textit{Standard Specification for Seamless Copper Water Tube}: Type L
        dimensional data used for ACR tube ID values.
  \item ASME B36.10M, \textit{Welded and Seamless Wrought Steel Pipe}: Schedule 40
        dimensional data used for the glycol and chilled-water loop pipe ID values.
\end{enumerate}


% ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
\chapter{Liquid Receiver: Evaluated and Omitted}
\label{ch:receiver}
% ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

\section{The Brief Makes This Conditional}
\label{receiver:sec:conditional}

The project brief calls for an ``oil separator and liquid receiver, \textbf{if
required by the chosen architecture}'' (subtask~1d). That conditional is the whole
content of this chapter. The oil separator \emph{is} required and is selected in
Chapter~\ref{ch:oilsep}. \textbf{A liquid receiver is not}, and this chapter
documents why --- an omission reached on evidence, and one that improves the design
on two independent axes.

A receiver is the default choice on many systems, and a plausible one here would have been
the Bitzer F252HP (25\,L). Two findings, in order of severity, rule it out.

\section{Finding 1: A Receiver Would Break AHRI Compliance}
\label{receiver:sec:subcooling}

A conventional \textbf{through-flow} receiver sits between the condenser outlet and
the EEV, and every kilogram of refrigerant passes through it. Inside, liquid and
vapour sit in equilibrium, so \textbf{its outlet is saturated liquid by
construction} --- the vessel cannot deliver subcooled liquid, because any subcooling
entering it is destroyed as vapour condenses onto the cooler liquid surface.

This matters because the entire cycle model assumes the condenser's \textbf{5\,K
subcooling reaches the valve} (Chapter~\ref{ch:eev}, \S\ref{eev:sec:inlet} takes the
condenser outlet state directly). A through-flow receiver placed between them would
silently invalidate that assumption:

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Effect of a through-flow receiver on the EEV inlet state and the resulting
performance.}
\label{tab:receiver_cop}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLLLL}
\toprule
\textbf{EEV inlet state} & \textbf{$h_3$} & \textbf{Flash gas $x_4$} & \textbf{Design COP} & \textbf{AHRI full-load COP} \\
\midrule
5\,K subcooled (condenser outlet, no receiver) & 307.1\,kJ/kg & 0.227 & 4.67 & \textbf{3.53} --- meets $\geq3.5$ \\
Saturated liquid (through-flow receiver outlet) & 321.8\,kJ/kg & 0.268 & 4.44 & \textbf{3.35} --- \textbf{FAILS} $\geq3.5$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\noindent The receiver costs \textbf{5\,\% of the COP}, and the AHRI full-load figure
carries only $\approx$1\,\% margin over its target (\S\ref{perf:sec:fullload}).
\textbf{A through-flow receiver therefore takes the design from compliant to
non-compliant} --- the single largest consequence of any component decision in this
report. The mechanism is visible on the p-h chart (Figure~\ref{fig:phchart}): losing
subcooling moves state~3 right, dragging state~4 with it along the isenthalpic
expansion, so more of the refrigerant flashes to vapour before it can absorb any
useful heat.

\section{Finding 2: A Receiver Would Be a Third to a Half of the Charge, in an A3 System}
\label{receiver:sec:charge_cost}

An F252HP receiver would hold \textbf{2.97--6.16\,kg} of refrigerant. Added to the
charge-minimized 5.5--8.7\,kg inventory of Chapter~\ref{ch:safety}, that is
\textbf{35--42\,\% of the whole system charge}, sitting in a vessel whose only purpose is
to hold charge. For an A3 refrigerant this is close to circular: the receiver holds
charge, the charge drives the EN~378 obligations (Chapter~\ref{ch:safety}), and a larger
charge demands a larger receiver. The tighter the rest of the plant is made, the worse
that ratio gets --- which is exactly the direction this design has taken the line lengths.

Omitting it, both columns at the same charge-minimized line lengths:

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Effect of omitting the receiver on the system charge and its EN~378 consequences
(both columns at the adopted 4\,m liquid line).}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLL}
\toprule
& \textbf{With F252HP receiver} & \textbf{No receiver (adopted)} \\
\midrule
System charge & 8.4--14.8\,kg & \textbf{5.5--8.7\,kg} \\
Emergency ventilation (EN 378-3) & 303\,m$^3$/h & \textbf{213\,m$^3$/h} \\
Room volume to reach LFL on full release & 221\,m$^3$ & \textbf{143\,m$^3$} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\noindent Note the third row: the F252HP was \emph{undersized anyway} --- it could not
hold the charge it was partly responsible for creating. Resolving that by buying a
larger vessel would have added yet more charge. Omission resolves the problem instead
of escalating it.

\section{Is the Receiver's Function Actually Needed Here?}
\label{receiver:sec:functions}

A receiver is omitted only if the jobs it does are either unnecessary or done
elsewhere. Each of its conventional functions is assessed against this specific
architecture:

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Conventional receiver functions, assessed against this design.}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLL}
\toprule
\textbf{Function} & \textbf{Needed here?} & \textbf{Reasoning} \\
\midrule
Guarantee liquid (no flash gas) at the EEV & \textbf{No --- it does the opposite} &
The condenser's 5\,K subcooling already guarantees it; a receiver \emph{degrades}
this to saturated liquid (\S\ref{receiver:sec:subcooling}) \\
Buffer charge redistribution across ambient & \textbf{No} & Mechanical mode spans only
$t_c \approx 23$--45\,\textdegree C (below that the plant is in free cooling,
Ch.~\ref{ch:dry}). Falling ambient frees condenser area, which \emph{increases}
subcooling --- self-correcting in the favourable direction \\
Absorb off-design operating variation & \textbf{No} & This is the EEV's function: it
modulates continuously to hold evaporator superheat (Ch.~\ref{ch:eev}), and does so
across the load and ambient range \\
Buffer the mechanical\,/\,free-cooling mode switch & \textbf{No} & In free cooling the
compressor is off and the refrigerant circuit is simply idle --- there is no
redistribution to buffer, only a static inventory \\
Storage for service pump-down & \textbf{Partly} & The one genuine loss. Recovery into
service cylinders is standard practice for hydrocarbon plant and is adopted instead
(\S\ref{receiver:sec:consequences}) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\noindent Only the pump-down function is genuinely given up, and it has a routine
alternative. This conclusion is also consistent with commercial practice: production
R-290 chillers in this capacity class are typically \textbf{critically charged with no
receiver}, precisely because charge minimisation dominates the design of an A3 system.

\section{Consequences of the Omission}
\label{receiver:sec:consequences}

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{The system is critically charged.} There is no buffer vessel, so the
        charge must be correct rather than merely sufficient. It is established by
        \textbf{weighing at commissioning} against the inventory of
        Chapter~\ref{ch:safety}, and confirmed by observing the design subcooling at
        the condenser outlet --- \textbf{5\,K subcooling is now a commissioning
        acceptance criterion}, not merely a design assumption.
  \item \textbf{Subcooling is delivered by the condenser alone}, which is what the
        cycle model and the EEV chapter already assume. The architectural
        inconsistency between the former receiver chapter and
        Chapter~\ref{ch:eev} is resolved by removing the receiver, not by weakening
        the assumption.
  \item \textbf{Service recovery uses cylinders}, not pump-down to a vessel ---
        standard for hydrocarbon plant, and consistent with the charge-minimisation
        objective (\S\ref{safe:sec:minimisation}).
  \item \textbf{Fewer components, fewer leak paths.} For an A3 refrigerant every
        joint is a potential release; deleting a vessel with two connections and a
        sight glass is a safety gain in its own right, not only a cost saving.
\end{enumerate}

\section{If a Receiver Were Nonetheless Required}
\label{receiver:sec:ifrequired}

Should a later stage of the design reintroduce a need for one --- for example if a
real layout produced a much longer liquid line, or if commissioning found the
critical charge unacceptably sensitive --- it \textbf{must not} be a through-flow
vessel. The correct arrangement is a \textbf{surge (bypass) receiver}, teed off the
liquid line so that only surplus charge enters it while the main liquid flow passes
by and retains its subcooling. This preserves the COP at the cost of a more complex
arrangement and some added charge, and would need to be re-evaluated against
Chapter~\ref{ch:safety}'s inventory.

\section{Limitations and Open Items}
\label{receiver:sec:limitations}

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Critical-charge tolerance has not been quantified.} How sensitive the
        plant's subcooling and superheat are to a $\pm$5\,\% charge error across the
        ambient range has not been modelled, and a receiver-less system is by
        definition less forgiving. This should be checked before commissioning
        procedures are finalised (subtask~2).
  \item \textbf{Condenser liquid holdup at low ambient is not modelled.} At the
        coldest mechanical-mode condition ($t_c \approx 23$\,\textdegree C) more
        liquid sits in the condenser. The argument above is that this is
        self-correcting (it raises subcooling), but the flooded fraction and its
        effect on active condensing area have not been computed.
  \item \textbf{No head-pressure control strategy is specified.} With no receiver,
        low-ambient head-pressure control (if needed) must be achieved by dry-cooler
        fan-speed control rather than by flooding a receiver --- consistent with the
        variable-speed fans already selected (Ch.~\ref{ch:dry}), but not yet designed
        as a control loop.
\end{enumerate}

\section{References}

\begin{enumerate}
  \item Project \#3 brief, IRCC 2026: subtask~1d, ``Oil separator and liquid
        receiver, \emph{if required by the chosen architecture}.''
  \item EN 378, \textit{Refrigerating systems and heat pumps --- Safety and
        environmental requirements} --- A3 charge-minimisation basis.
  \item Bitzer, \textit{Liquid Receivers --- P Series, for Hydrocarbon
        Applications}, Product Data Sheet DP-341-1 --- the withdrawn F252HP
        selection's source data.
\end{enumerate}

% ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
\chapter{System Performance: Full-Load COP, IPLV, and PUE}
\label{ch:performance}
% ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

\zonedivider{Part A \quad---\quad Standardized Rating-Point Metrics}

% ── §12.1 ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Scope and Method}
\label{perf:sec:scope}

This chapter reports the three standardized efficiency metrics the project brief
requires (subtask~1f): the \textbf{full-load COP}, the \textbf{Integrated Part Load
Value (IPLV.IP)}, and the \textbf{design-day facility PUE} --- followed by the
weather-driven \textbf{seasonal performance} across the real Champaign TMY3 year.

\subsection{The Design Basis Behind These Numbers}
\label{perf:sec:revision}

Three choices set every metric in this chapter, and each was made \emph{because} of the
efficiency targets rather than independently of them. They are stated here up front
because the reported COP and IPLV are meaningless without them:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{A 10\,K air-to-refrigerant condensing approach}, giving
        $t_c = 45$\,\textdegree C at the 35\,\textdegree C design ambient. This is
        \emph{not} one approach but three stacked in series --- 3\,K across the dry
        cooler, a 4\,K glycol rise, 3\,K across the condenser --- an unavoidable
        consequence of the intermediate glycol loop
        (Chapter~\ref{ch:cyclerep}, Fig.~\ref{fig:cascade}). Each kelvin is bought with
        heat-transfer area, and $t_c$ is the single most powerful lever on the full-load
        COP (\S\ref{perf:sec:sensitivity}). It is why the dry cooler is as large as it is.
  \item \textbf{A 5\,K evaporator approach}, giving $t_o = 10$\,\textdegree C at the
        15\,\textdegree C design LCHW. Bought with evaporator plates.
  \item \textbf{VFDs on both compressors.} At full load the two-unit bank over-delivers
        at the evaporator-dictated $t_o$ ($\approx$201\,kW available at
        $t_o=10$/$t_c=45$\,\textdegree C), so the drive trims speed to $\approx$75\,\% and
        delivers exactly 150\,kW \emph{there}, instead of letting $t_o$ float down --- and
        colder $t_o$ costs COP. Below the fixed-speed turndown floor the drive modulates
        capacity rather than hot-gas bypassing, which is what makes the 25\,\% IPLV point
        and the 30\,kW minimum load affordable (\S\ref{perf:sec:iplv}). COP is taken as
        speed-invariant (capacity and power both scale $\approx$linearly with speed for a
        reciprocating compressor), a neutral-to-conservative standard simplification.
\end{itemize}

\noindent The hardware these choices demand --- the dry cooler frame, the plate counts,
the glycol flow --- is quantified in \S\ref{perf:sec:hardware}. All compressor
performance is drawn from the same Bitzer 4FEP-35Z AHRI-540 map used throughout the
report (Chapter~\ref{ch:comp}); the VFD operating model is
\texttt{compressor.solve\_with\_vfd()}, and the metrics are implemented in
\texttt{performance.py}.

% ── §12.2 ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Full-Load COP}
\label{perf:sec:fullload}

The project brief requires the full-load COP at the strict AHRI 550/590-2023 rating
condition (6.7\,\textdegree C leaving chilled water, 35\,\textdegree C condenser
entering air) \emph{and}, for this study, at the project's own design point
(15\,\textdegree C leaving chilled water, per the ASHRAE~TC~9.9 W17 data-center
envelope). Both share the same 35\,\textdegree C entering air --- hence the same
$t_c = 45$\,\textdegree C via the 10\,K approach --- and differ only in
chilled-water temperature, which sets $t_o$ via the 5\,K evaporator approach.

\input{tables/performance/full_load_cop.tex}

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.72\linewidth]{figures/performance/design_point/full_load_cop.pdf}
\caption{Full-load COP at the AHRI rating point and the project design point, against
the 3.5 target.}
\end{figure}

\textbf{Result: both points meet the 3.5 target.} At the AHRI rating condition the
full-load COP is \textbf{3.53} ($\geq3.5$, met with a $\approx$1\,\% margin);
at the warmer project design point it is \textbf{4.67}. The two-unit bank's full-speed
capacity at the AHRI condition is 150.9\,kW, so the 150\,kW duty is also still met
there. The margin at the strict AHRI point is thin and rests on the design approaches
being achieved in the final vendor selections (\S\ref{perf:sec:hardware}); the project
design point carries a comfortable margin.

\subsection{Condensing-Temperature Sensitivity: How Much the 10\,K Approach Is Worth}
\label{perf:sec:sensitivity}

The condensing temperature dominates the full-load result, and Figure~\ref{fig:cop_sens}
is the justification for every dollar spent on the dry cooler. Sweeping $t_c$ at the AHRI
chilled-water condition, with everything else at its design value, the COP falls at
\textbf{$\approx$0.09 per kelvin} of condensing temperature. Relaxing the approach from
10\,K to a conventional air-cooled 17\,K ($t_c = 52$\,\textdegree C) would cost
\textbf{21.7\,\%} of the full-load COP and drop the design to 2.90 --- far below target.

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Read the margin off this curve.} The design sits at $t_c = 45$\,\textdegree C and
COP 3.529 against a 3.5 target. At 0.09\,COP/K, that is \textbf{about 0.3\,K of
headroom}: a condensing temperature of $\approx$45.3\,\textdegree C fails the standard.
The full-load target is not comfortably met --- it is met, and the whole margin lives in
the dry cooler's 3\,K approach and the condenser's $U$. This is why those two vendor
confirmations are the first priority in Chapter~\ref{ch:improve}.
\end{quote}

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.80\linewidth]{figures/performance/design_point/cop_tc_sensitivity.pdf}
\caption{Full-load COP at the AHRI 6.7\,\textdegree C LCHW condition versus condensing
temperature, at the design's own 5\,K evaporator approach --- so the curve passes through
the reported 3.53 at the $t_c=45$\,\textdegree C design point rather than describing a
hypothetical machine.}
\label{fig:cop_sens}
\end{figure}

% ── §12.3 ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Integrated Part Load Value (IPLV.IP)}
\label{perf:sec:iplv}

IPLV.IP is the AHRI 550/590-2023~\S5.2 weighted part-load COP,
$0.01A + 0.42B + 0.45C + 0.12D$, evaluated at 100/75/50/25\,\% load with each point at
its own reduced condenser-entering-air temperature (35/26.7/18.3/12.8\,\textdegree C).
Each air temperature is mapped to a condensing temperature via the 10\,K
approach, and the compressor bank is staged (Chapter~\ref{ch:comp}) to deliver the
reduced load, with the VFD modulating capacity below the fixed-speed turndown floor.

\input{tables/performance/iplv.tex}

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.85\linewidth]{figures/performance/design_point/iplv_points.pdf}
\caption{IPLV.IP part-load points. Point~D (25\,\% load) runs on VFD speed modulation
below the fixed-speed turndown floor.}
\end{figure}

\textbf{Result: IPLV.IP $= 5.23$, above the $\geq5.0$ target} --- and with a 4.6\,\%
margin, far more comfortable than the full-load result. Two elements of the design basis
carry it:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{The 10\,K condensing approach} lowers $t_c$ at every point (Point~C
        condenses at just 28.3\,\textdegree C), lifting the two heavily weighted mid-load
        points that dominate the weighting: B reaches COP 6.18 (weight 0.42) and C
        reaches 4.62 (weight 0.45). Together those two points are 87\,\% of the metric.
  \item \textbf{VFD capacity modulation at Point~D.} Rather than running at the
        fixed-speed floor and throwing away the excess as recirculated hot gas --- which
        would collapse this point to COP 2.55 --- the drive slows the compressor so power
        scales down with the delivered duty, holding COP at the floor operating point's
        map value of 4.35. This is deliberately \emph{conservative}: in reality reduced
        speed lets the evaporating temperature float upward (the evaporator is
        4$\times$ oversized at 25\,\% load), which would raise the point further.
\end{itemize}

The drive is also what covers the project's \textbf{30\,kW minimum IT load} without
bypass --- a point a fixed-speed selection of this compressor physically cannot reach,
since its floor sits at $\approx$32\,\% of design duty
(Chapter~\ref{ch:comp}, \S\ref{comp:sec:floor}). The same hardware therefore answers
three separate requirements: the full-load oversize trim, the 25\,\% IPLV point, and the
5:1 turndown.

% ── §12.4 ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Design-Day PUE}
\label{perf:sec:pue}

Facility PUE is (IT load $+$ other facility loads)$/$IT load on a design-day basis, with
the IT load taken as numerically equal to the 150\,kW design cooling duty (the project's
near-constant-load assumption). ``Other facility loads'' are the mechanical-mode
electrical loads at the 35\,\textdegree C / full-load design point.

\input{tables/performance/design_day_pue.tex}

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figures/performance/design_point/pue_breakdown.pdf}
\caption{Design-day facility power breakdown. The compressor bank dominates at
75\,\% of the non-IT load.}
\label{fig:pue_breakdown}
\end{figure}

The design-day PUE is \textbf{1.291}, dominated by the compressor bank at 32.1\,kW of the
43.7\,kW of non-IT load --- \textbf{74\,\% of everything the plant draws beyond the IT
itself}. That single fact frames the whole design: the compressor is the only load worth
attacking, which is precisely why the condensing approach was driven down to 10\,K at the
cost of a very large dry cooler.

The remaining loads are the price of the architecture rather than of the compressor. Fan
power is \textbf{7.9\,kW} --- not the 10.6\,kW rated value: the coil is sized for
196.5\,kW but the plant rejects only $\approx$182\,kW at the design day, so the
variable-speed fans run at 90.7\,\% of design air flow and the cube law gives
$10.6 \times 0.907^3 = 7.9$\,kW (\S\ref{dry:sec:fanpower_open}). This is the same
off-design fan solve the annual simulation uses, so the design-day PUE is consistent with
the monthly and seasonal numbers below. The glycol pump draws 2.57\,kW because the tight
4\,K glycol range demands high flow, and because the selected dry cooler's 8\,psi
(\S\ref{dry:sec:datasheet}) and the condenser's $\approx$35.9\,kPa both sit on its system
curve. The fan power remains the largest single \textbf{uncertainty} here, since the
10.6\,kW rating it scales from is itself an estimate.

This is the \emph{worst-case} PUE: it occurs only at the hottest design ambient. The
seasonal analysis below shows the annual-average is far more favourable, because most of
the year runs at part load or in free cooling.

\noindent \textbf{Glycol pump --- reconciled, one task remaining.} The glycol loop is now
carried consistently at its true 11.64\,kg/s flow throughout: the condenser's glycol-side
pressure drop is $\approx$35.9\,kPa, and Chapter~\ref{ch:pump}'s system curve is rebuilt
on that value plus the selected dry cooler's 8\,psi, giving a mechanical duty of
41.36\,m$^3$/h @ 14.18\,m. That duty falls \emph{outside} the 2AD frame's envelope, so the
pump frame is now the \textbf{2.5AC} (Chapter~\ref{ch:pump}, \S7.4). The pump's electrical
power (2.57\,kW) is the hydraulic duty $\rho g \dot V H/\eta$ and is unchanged by the frame
choice. The one remaining task is to digitize the 2.5AC vendor curves and fix the exact
trim and VFD speed on that frame.

% ── §12.4b ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\subsection{Beyond PUE: TUE and the Losses PUE Cannot See}
\label{perf:sec:tue}

PUE has a well-known blind spot: it stops at the rack. Every watt crossing into the IT
equipment counts as ``useful,'' so the losses \emph{inside} the servers --- power-supply
conversion, on-board voltage regulators, and the servers' own cooling fans --- are
invisible to it. Two facilities with an identical PUE of 1.291 can therefore deliver
materially different compute per watt. Worse, the metric can be gamed in the wrong
direction: moving heat rejection onto the server (bigger on-board fans) \emph{lowers}
PUE while raising total site power.

\textbf{TUE} (Total-power Usage Effectiveness, Patterson et al., 2013) closes the gap by
referencing all facility power to actual \emph{compute} power:

\begin{equation}
\mathrm{TUE} = \mathrm{ITUE} \times \mathrm{PUE},
\qquad
\mathrm{ITUE} = \frac{\text{total IT equipment power}}{\text{compute power}}
\label{eq:tue}
\end{equation}

\noindent where ``compute power'' is what reaches the processors, memory, storage and
network silicon.

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Assumption, stated plainly.} This project models the 150\,kW as a
\emph{black-box IT load} --- there is no server-level power model anywhere in it, and
the project brief specifies none. \textbf{ITUE $= 1.20$ is therefore an assumption, not
a result.} It is a mid-range literature value for modern volume servers ($\approx$94\,\%
PSU efficiency, $\approx$5\,\% on-board fan power, plus VRM and conversion losses). The
band carried throughout is 1.10 (best-in-class 80\,PLUS Titanium supplies, minimal fan
power) to 1.30 (older or fan-heavy hardware).
\end{quote}

\noindent Two consequences follow, and both matter for how much weight the reader should
put on the TUE columns that follow:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{With ITUE held constant, TUE is simply PUE $\times$ 1.20.} It re-ranks
        nothing in this report and introduces no new physics. Every conclusion drawn
        from PUE --- the free-cooling benefit, the seasonal spread, the compressor's
        dominance of the non-IT load --- is unchanged.
  \item \textbf{Its value is one of honesty about scope.} The design-day TUE of
        \textbf{1.55} (band 1.42--1.68) says that for every watt of real compute, the
        site draws about 1.55\,W --- and that the \emph{cooling plant is responsible for
        only part of that overhead}. Of the 0.55 of overhead, roughly 0.29 is this
        project's mechanical plant and roughly 0.26 is IT-internal losses this project
        does not control and has not designed.
\end{itemize}

\noindent A defensible TUE for this facility would require measured server power data
(compute vs.\ PSU vs.\ fan), which sits outside the project scope. The figure is
reported because the metric was requested and because it correctly frames how much of
the total energy problem the chiller design can actually address --- not because 1.55
is a validated result.

\zonedivider{Part B \quad---\quad Seasonal Weather-Driven Performance}

% ── §12.5 ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{Seasonal Performance (Champaign TMY3)}
\label{perf:sec:seasonal}

Before the performance results, Figure~\ref{fig:duration} shows the climate the plant
actually sees: the Champaign TMY3 ambient duration curve against the ASHRAE~90.1
economizer thresholds. It is the origin of every free-cooling hour claimed in this
report, and it independently reproduces the regime hour counts of
Chapter~\ref{ch:dry}, \S\ref{dry:sec:annualregime}.

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figures/performance/annual/ambient_duration_curve.pdf}
\caption{Champaign TMY3 ambient temperature duration curve with the economizer
thresholds and the dry cooler's 35\,\textdegree C design ambient.}
\label{fig:duration}
\end{figure}

\noindent Two things are worth reading off it. First, the 35\,\textdegree C dry-cooler
design ambient is reached for only a handful of hours a year --- the design-day
condition that sizes the whole plant is genuinely a worst case, not a typical one, which
is exactly why the design-day PUE of 1.291 overstates the annual average. Second, the
curve falls below 4\,\textdegree C for 2{,}542\,h and reaches $-27.4$\,\textdegree C at
its extreme, which is what makes the free-cooling architecture pay in this climate.

The full detailed cycle was re-run at every month's and every season's average outdoor
dry-bulb temperature from the real 8{,}760-hour Champaign TMY3 file (WMO~725315),
automatically selecting among all three ASHRAE~90.1 economizer regimes --- mechanical
above 10\,\textdegree C, integrated/partial free cooling between 4 and 10\,\textdegree C,
and full free cooling at or below 4\,\textdegree C (\texttt{main.py}). The total-system COP
below is $Q_{\mathrm{cool}}/P_{\mathrm{total}}$ (all facility power: compressor, both
pumps, and fans), equivalently $1/(\mathrm{PUE}-1)$. Each period carries both PUE and
TUE (Eq.~\ref{eq:tue}); the TUE columns are PUE $\times$ 1.20 throughout, on the assumed
ITUE of \S\ref{perf:sec:tue}, and are reported for scope honesty rather than as an
independent result.

\input{tables/performance/seasonal_perf_summary.tex}

\subsection{Month-by-Month Detail}
\label{perf:sec:monthly}

Table~\ref{tab:monthly_summary} resolves the seasonal averages into the 12 monthly runs
they are grouped from, which is where the regime transitions are actually visible. Three
months (Dec--Feb) run fully free. \textbf{March and November land inside the
integrated/partial band} at 6.0 and 7.1\,\textdegree C monthly averages: the economizer
carries 124\,kW and 111\,kW of the 150\,kW respectively, leaving the chiller to trim only
26\,kW and 39\,kW. Their compressor power collapses to \textbf{1.8\,kW and 2.9\,kW} ---
against the 10.7 and 11.3\,kW those same months cost when the partial band was folded
into full mechanical operation --- and their PUE falls from 1.12 to \textbf{1.03}, within
a rounding error of a fully free month. The remaining seven months run mechanical, their
compressor power tracking ambient almost linearly (13.7\,kW in October to 23.2\,kW in
July).

\input{tables/performance/monthly/monthly_summary.tex}

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.90\linewidth]{figures/performance/monthly/monthly_cop_pue.pdf}
\caption{Monthly-average total-system COP (top) with PUE and TUE (bottom). Aqua bars are
free-cooling months (compressor off), blue are mechanical; solid bars are PUE, hatched
bars TUE. The COP panel's scale is set by the free-cooling months, which is the point:
winter operation is an order of magnitude more efficient than summer.}
\label{fig:monthly_cop_pue}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.85\linewidth]{figures/performance/seasonal/seasonal_cop_pue.pdf}
\caption{Seasonal-average total-system COP, PUE and TUE (same encoding as
Fig.~\ref{fig:monthly_cop_pue}).}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{figures/performance/annual/annual_cop_load.pdf}
\caption{Delivered cooling load and compressor COP across all 8{,}760 hours; aqua
shading marks free-cooling hours.}
\end{figure}

The seasonal picture is strongly favourable and stands in deliberate contrast to the
worst-case design-day metrics of Part~A. Winter runs entirely in free cooling
(compressor off, PUE 1.01, TUE 1.21, total-system COP near 88), while even the hottest
monthly average (July) holds PUE 1.22 / TUE 1.47, against the worst-case design day's
1.291 / 1.55. Spring and Fall now sit at PUE 1.12 --- markedly better than the
1.14/1.15 they showed before the partial band was modelled --- because each contains one
month that runs integrated rather than mechanical. Across the year the economizer
delivers \textbf{526\,MWh of the 1{,}314\,MWh} of cooling the hall needs
(\textbf{40.1\,\%}) with the compressor off or barely loaded, which is the central
benefit of the glycol-loop free-cooling architecture and the reason the
\emph{annual-average} efficiency beats any single design-point number. The annual run is
\textbf{clean across all 8{,}760 hours} --- no hour asks the selected dry cooler
(\S\ref{dry:sec:datasheet}) for more than 100\,\% fan speed.

Read through the TUE column, the same result carries a blunter message. In winter the
plant's own overhead nearly vanishes (PUE 1.01), yet TUE still sits at 1.21 --- because
the IT-internal losses do not go away when the compressor stops. \textbf{In the best
months, essentially all remaining site overhead is inside the servers, not in this
design.} That is the useful thing TUE says here: the mechanical plant's headroom for
further improvement is largest in summer and close to exhausted in winter, and beyond
that point the lever belongs to IT procurement, not to the chiller.

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Method note --- averaged temperature, not averaged hours.} Each monthly and
seasonal row is one cycle run at that period's \emph{average} dry-bulb, not an
integration of its individual hourly runs. Because COP is non-linear in ambient, these
are representative-condition results and will differ modestly from a true hourly
energy integration --- generally flattering, since averaging hides the hot tail. The
8{,}760-hour simulation that would support the exact integration already exists (it
produces Fig.~\ref{fig:duration} and the regime hour counts); converting the tables to
hourly-integrated annual energy is a contained change and is listed as an open item.
\end{quote}

% ── §12.6 ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

All required efficiency metrics are met. The honest caveat is that \textbf{the AHRI
full-load COP margin is thin --- 0.8\,\%} --- and it rests on the 10\,K condensing
approach surviving vendor confirmation of the plate $U$-values and the dry cooler's

healthier 4.6\,\% margin, and its Point-D value is deliberately conservative
(\S\ref{perf:sec:iplv}), so the part-load result is the more robust of the two.

% ── §12.7 ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\section{The Hardware These Targets Demand}
\label{perf:sec:hardware}

The efficiency targets are bought with heat-transfer area and drives. This section makes
that cost explicit, because it is the dominant capital consequence of the whole design.

\subsection{Overall Heat-Transfer Coefficients: the Double-Wall Derate}
\label{perf:sec:uvalues}

The design's approaches require $UA = 19.7$\,kW/K on the evaporator and 20.2\,kW/K on
the condenser. That $UA$ must be bought as \emph{area} (plates) or as \emph{coefficient}
($U$), so the $U$ adopted decides whether the exchangers fit their frame --- which makes
the following correction load-bearing rather than a detail.

\begin{quote}
\textbf{The published $U$ bands do not apply to this hardware.} The bands cited in the
component chapters (\S2.2.1: 2{,}500--4{,}500 for a water/propane BPHE; \S3.2.4:
2{,}000--3{,}500 for refrigerant/glycol) are published for \textbf{generic, single-wall}
plate exchangers. This design uses \textbf{double-wall} plates throughout, adopted for A3
leak safety (\S2.4.1, \S3.4.1) --- and a double wall interposes a second plate plus an
interstitial contact resistance directly in the heat-transfer path. The correct $U$ is
therefore \emph{lower} than the published band, not somewhere within it. It is an easy
error to make in the opposite direction.
\end{quote}

\noindent A resistance-network check anchors the single-wall starting point: for the
evaporator, $1/U = 1/h_{w} + t/k + 1/h_{r}$ with a water film $h_{w}\approx6{,}000$, a
0.4\,mm stainless wall, and a propane boiling film
$h_{r}\approx7{,}000$\,W\,m$^{-2}$K$^{-1}$ returns $U\approx2{,}990$ --- essentially the
3{,}000 the band would suggest. Published double-wall derates fall in the 10--30\,\%
range; a mid-range \textbf{20\,\% derate} is adopted, giving:

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Overall heat-transfer coefficient assumptions, derated for double-wall
construction.}
\label{tab:revision_u}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLLL}
\toprule
\textbf{Exchanger} & \textbf{Published single-wall band} & \textbf{Adopted (DW)} & \textbf{Basis} \\
\midrule
Evaporator (water\,/\,R-290) & 2{,}500--4{,}500 & \textbf{2{,}400} &
Resistance-network single-wall value ($\approx$2{,}990), $\times$0.80 double-wall derate \\
Condenser (R-290\,/\,30\,\% PG) & 2{,}000--3{,}500 & \textbf{2{,}000} &
\S3.2.4's single-wall value, $\times$0.80 double-wall derate \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\noindent Both adopted values sit \emph{below} their published band. The 20\,\% derate is
itself an estimate from a 10--30\,\% literature range and is the first vendor
confirmation to close (\S\ref{imp:sec:vendor}): Kelvion should be asked for the DW derate
\emph{relative to the equivalent single-wall plate}.

\subsubsection*{Vendor plate geometry}

The plate counts rest on confirmed \textbf{Kelvion HP\,DW\,500H} geometry: a
heat-transfer area of \textbf{195\,$\times$\,600\,mm $=$ 0.117\,m$^2$ per plate} in a
frame accepting up to \textbf{120 plates}. This matters more than it might appear --- the
real plate is 76\,\% larger than the $A\times B$ estimate used before vendor data was
available (\S2.6.1, \S3.6.1), and that margin is exactly what lets both exchangers absorb
the tight approaches \emph{and} the double-wall derate inside a standard frame
(\S\ref{perf:sec:hardware}).

\noindent \textbf{These remain assumptions, not vendor results.} $U$ is an
\emph{outcome} of real plate geometry, channel velocity, and the specific
double-wall construction, not a free parameter, and the 20\,\% derate is an
estimate spanning a 10--30\,\% literature range rather than a datasheet figure.
Both coefficients must be confirmed in \textbf{Kelvion Select PHE} before
procurement --- the decisive question being the \emph{double-wall derate relative
to the equivalent single-wall plate}. The same selection run should also report the
resulting channel pressure drops, which feed pump sizing (Chapters~\ref{ch:pump},
\ref{ch:chwpump}). The sensitivity is documented in \S\ref{perf:sec:hardware}: $U$
can fall to $\approx$1{,}700\,W\,m$^{-2}$K$^{-1}$ on either exchanger --- a further
$\approx$30\,\% below the values adopted here --- before the 120-plate frame binds.

\subsection{Component by Component}

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{The hardware the design basis demands. Plate exchangers are sized by LMTD at
the double-wall-corrected $U$ values of Table~\ref{tab:revision_u} against the confirmed
Kelvion HP\,DW\,500H plate (0.117\,m$^2$, 120-plate frame); the dry cooler via the
$\varepsilon$-NTU model of Chapter~\ref{ch:dry}. Plate counts include the report's
standard 20\,\% margin.}
\label{tab:revision_hardware}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLL}
\toprule
\textbf{Component} & \textbf{As designed} & \textbf{Consequence} \\
\midrule
Evaporator BPHE & LMTD 7.6\,K, $UA$ 19.7\,kW/K, $U$ 2{,}400 (DW), 8.21\,m$^2$,
$\approx$\textbf{84 plates} & \textbf{Fits the HP\,DW\,500H frame} with
$\approx$36 plates spare, despite the 5\,K approach \emph{and} the double-wall
derate \\
Condenser BPHE & LMTD 9.0\,K, $UA$ 20.2\,kW/K, $U$ 2{,}000 (DW), 10.12\,m$^2$,
$\approx$\textbf{104 plates} & \textbf{Fits the frame} with $\approx$16 plates
spare \\
Dry cooler & \textbf{Kelvion ULF-PA106K4V-091F095}, 1{,}949\,m$^2$, $UA \approx
73.7$\,kW/K, 60\,dB(A), 10\,\% fan headroom & \textbf{The dominant capital item of the
whole plant.} The 3\,K approach is what makes it this large
(\S\ref{dry:sec:capacity_trap}); the fan headroom is what keeps all 8{,}760 hours off
the fan ceiling \\
Glycol loop & 11.64\,kg/s at a 4\,K range, condenser $\Delta P \approx$35.9\,kPa, dry
cooler 8\,psi & The tight 4\,K range demands high flow, so pump electrical power is
2.57\,kW. Ch.~\ref{ch:pump}'s system curve is rebuilt on these values; the duty falls
outside the 2AD envelope, so the frame is now the \textbf{2.5AC} (exact 2.5AC trim/speed
still to fix) \\
Compressors & 2 $\times$ 4FEP-35Z with VFDs & Standard drive addition; delivers the
5:1 turndown to the 30\,kW minimum load without hot-gas bypass \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\noindent \textbf{Both plate exchangers remain within the selected Kelvion HP\,DW\,500H
frame}, with 36 and 16 plates of headroom respectively, so the capital impact
concentrates almost entirely in the dry cooler and the glycol pump.

That the plate exchangers fit so comfortably is a direct consequence of the confirmed
vendor plate being 76\,\% larger than the $A\times B$ placeholder used before it was
available --- enough to absorb both the tight approaches and the double-wall derate. The
corresponding
\textbf{sensitivity margin is wide}: $U$ would have to fall to
$\approx$1{,}685 (evaporator) or $\approx$1{,}729\,W\,m$^{-2}$K$^{-1}$ (condenser)
--- roughly 30\,\% below the already double-wall-derated values adopted here, and
below the floor of even the single-wall published bands --- before the 120-plate
frame becomes binding. The plate-exchanger sizing is therefore robust to a
substantially worse-than-expected vendor answer, which is the opposite of the
knife-edge the earlier optimistic-$U$ treatment implied.


% ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
\chapter{Cycle Representation: p-h Chart and Temperature Cascade}
\label{ch:cyclerep}
% ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

\section{Pressure-Enthalpy Chart of the Designed Cycle}
\label{cyc:sec:ph}

Figure~\ref{fig:phchart} is the project's p-h chart deliverable (subtask~1h): the
designed cycle plotted on the R-290 saturation dome at the \textbf{project design
point} --- 150\,kW at the 35\,\textdegree C design ambient. Every state point is
evaluated from the same Bitzer AHRI-540 map and CoolProp properties the rest of the
report uses (\texttt{cycle\_figures.py}), so the chart cannot drift from the model.

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{figures/cycle_diagram/ph_chart.pdf}
\caption{R-290 p-h chart of the designed cycle at the 150\,kW / 35\,\textdegree C
design point.}
\label{fig:phchart}
\end{figure}

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Cycle state points at the design point ($t_o = 10$, $t_c = 45$\,\textdegree C,
8\,K superheat, 5\,K subcooling).}
\label{tab:cycle_states}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLLLL}
\toprule
\textbf{State} & \textbf{Description} & \textbf{$T$ [\textdegree C]} & \textbf{$p$ [bar]} & \textbf{$h$ [kJ/kg]} \\
\midrule
1 & Compressor suction (evaporator out, 8\,K superheat) & 18.0 & 6.37 & 600.4 \\
2 & Compressor discharge & 64.8 & 15.34 & 663.2 \\
3 & Condenser out (subcooled liquid, 5\,K) & 40.0 & 15.34 & 307.1 \\
4 & EEV out / evaporator in (isenthalpic, $x=0.227$) & 10.0 & 6.37 & 307.1 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\subsection{Why the Low Condensing Temperature Pays Twice}
\label{cyc:sec:paystwice}

The chart is also the clearest place to see \emph{why} this design spends so much dry
cooler on holding $t_c$ down to 45\,\textdegree C. The COP is the ratio of the horizontal
4\,$\to$\,1 leg (refrigerating effect) to the 1\,$\to$\,2 leg (compressor work), and
raising $t_c$ damages \emph{both}. Re-solving the same cycle --- same compressor, same
$t_o$, same superheat and subcooling --- with the condensing approach relaxed to a
conventional air-cooled 17\,K ($t_c = 52$\,\textdegree C):

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{The same cycle at two condensing temperatures. Only $t_c$ changes; the loss is
compounded, not linear.}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLLL}
\toprule
& \textbf{$t_c=52$\,\textdegree C (17\,K approach)} & \textbf{$t_c=45$\,\textdegree C (this design)} & \textbf{Change} \\
\midrule
Compressor work $h_2-h_1$        & 72.4\,kJ/kg  & 62.8\,kJ/kg  & $-13$\,\% (smaller lift) \\
Refrigerating effect $h_1-h_4$   & 272.8\,kJ/kg & 293.3\,kJ/kg & $+8$\,\% (less flash gas) \\
Flash gas at EEV outlet $x_4$     & 0.284        & 0.227        & $-20$\,\% \\
Pressure ratio                    & 2.81         & 2.41         & $-14$\,\% \\
\midrule
\textbf{COP} $=(h_1-h_4)/(h_2-h_1)$ & \textbf{3.77} & \textbf{4.67} & $\mathbf{+24}$\,\% \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\noindent The work reduction is the expected consequence of a smaller lift. The
\emph{second} gain is less obvious and is visible only on the chart: lowering $t_c$ moves
state~3 left (the liquid leaves the condenser at 40 rather than 47\,\textdegree C), and
because expansion is isenthalpic, state~4 moves left with it. Less refrigerant flashes to
vapour across the valve (22.7\,\% against 28.4\,\%), so more of it arrives in the
evaporator as liquid actually able to absorb heat.

\begin{quote}
A lower condensing temperature therefore pays \textbf{twice} --- once in compressor work,
once in refrigerating effect --- which is why the 24\,\% COP gain is nearly double the
13\,\% work saving alone. This compounding is the entire economic case for the
1{,}949\,m$^2$ dry cooler.
\end{quote}

\noindent State~2 lies well right of the saturated-vapour line, confirming the
discharge is genuinely superheated (64.8\,\textdegree C against a 45\,\textdegree C
saturation temperature) --- the desuperheating zone the condenser's three-zone model
accounts for (\S\ref{cyc:sec:hx}).

\noindent Free cooling is not plotted: the compressor is off in that mode and the
economizer carries the load on the waterside, so no refrigerant cycle exists to draw.

\section{Temperature Cascade}
\label{cyc:sec:cascade}

Figure~\ref{fig:cascade} shows why the condensing temperature --- and therefore the
full-load COP --- is what it is. It is the single most load-bearing diagram in the
report.

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figures/cycle_diagram/temperature_cascade.pdf}
\caption{Temperature cascade from ambient air to chilled water at the design point. The
condensing temperature is set by three approaches stacked in series.}
\label{fig:cascade}
\end{figure}

\noindent The intermediate glycol loop --- the architecture the project brief
prescribes --- places \textbf{three approaches in series} between ambient air and the
condensing refrigerant: the dry cooler's approach, the glycol temperature rise, and
the condenser's own approach. A direct air-cooled condenser would pay only one. This
is the structural reason the full-load COP is harder to reach here than the ASHRAE
90.1 \emph{air-cooled chiller} table (from which the $\geq$3.5 target is drawn)
implicitly assumes, and it is the cost side of the trade that buys the free-cooling
economizer and the winter PUE of 1.01.

Each of the three is therefore driven as tight as the hardware allows: \textbf{3\,K} at
the dry cooler, a \textbf{4\,K} glycol rise, \textbf{3\,K} at the condenser. Their sum is
the 10\,K air-to-refrigerant approach that fixes $t_c = 45$\,\textdegree C. On the cold
side the evaporator approach is held to \textbf{5\,K}, lifting $t_o$ to
10\,\textdegree C. The lift is squeezed from both ends, and \S\ref{cyc:sec:paystwice}
shows what each kelvin is worth.

\begin{quote}
None of these three is generous, and that is the point: at 0.09\,COP/K
(\S\ref{perf:sec:sensitivity}) and only $\approx$0.3\,K of margin to the target,
\textbf{there is no slack anywhere in this cascade}. Any one of them slipping by half a
kelvin --- a fouled coil, an optimistic plate $U$, a warmer glycol loop --- puts the
full-load COP below 3.5.
\end{quote}

\section{Heat-Exchanger Temperature Profiles}
\label{cyc:sec:hx}

Figure~\ref{fig:hxprofiles} plots temperature against duty through both refrigerant-side
exchangers at the design point --- the picture behind the LMTD sizing of
Chapters~\ref{ch:evap} and~\ref{ch:cond}, and where each exchanger actually pinches.

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figures/cycle_diagram/hx_profiles.pdf}
\caption{Counter-flow temperature profiles at the design point: condenser
(three-zone) and evaporator (two-zone).}
\label{fig:hxprofiles}
\end{figure}

\noindent The \textbf{condenser} shows the three zones its model decomposes: a steep
desuperheating leg (64.8\,$\to$\,45\,\textdegree C) over the first $\approx$13\,\% of
duty, the dominant isothermal condensing plateau at 45\,\textdegree C, and a short
subcooling tail to 40\,\textdegree C. Its tightest point is the \textbf{cold end}, where
the 40\,\textdegree C subcooled liquid leaves against 38\,\textdegree C entering glycol
--- a 2\,K pinch. This is precisely the constraint that forces the dry cooler's 3\,K
approach (\S\ref{dry:sec:capacity_trap}): warmer glycol would close this gap to zero and
the exchanger would stop working, regardless of how much area it had.

The \textbf{evaporator} boils isothermally at 10\,\textdegree C over $\approx$95\,\% of
its duty, then superheats to 18\,\textdegree C in a short tail. Its approach is set at
the cold end, where the 15\,\textdegree C leaving chilled water meets the
10\,\textdegree C evaporating refrigerant --- the 5\,K approach, bought with the plate
count of \S\ref{perf:sec:hardware}.


% ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
\chapter{Refrigerant Charge Analysis and Safety Measures}
\label{ch:safety}
% ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

\zonedivider{Part A \quad---\quad Charge Inventory}

\section{Why This Chapter Is a Condition, Not an Addendum}
\label{safe:sec:why}

The project brief admits a refrigerant of safety class A3 \emph{only}
``\textbf{with charge analysis}'' (subtask~1a). R-290 is A3, so this chapter is a
\textbf{condition on the working-fluid selection itself}
(Chapter~\ref{ch:refrigerant}) rather than an optional extra: without it, the R-290
choice is not substantiated. It also carries subtask~1i (safety measures), which
the per-component chapters have until now addressed only piecemeal.

\section{Charge Inventory}
\label{safe:sec:inventory}

The inventory below is built component by component from the same geometry and
CoolProp properties the rest of the model uses (\texttt{charge\_analysis.py}), at
the design point. It is reported as a \textbf{range}, not a point value:
refrigerant redistributes with operating mode, and the void fractions in the two
two-phase exchangers are genuinely uncertain. Safety systems are sized on the
\emph{upper} bound.

\input{tables/safety/charge_inventory.tex}

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figures/safety/charge_inventory.pdf}
\caption{R-290 charge inventory at the design point (4\,m liquid line, no receiver).
Whiskers show the bounds from two-phase void fraction and oil-dissolved refrigerant.}
\label{fig:charge_inv}
\end{figure}

\subsection{What Dominates the Inventory}
\label{safe:sec:higher}

The total comes to \textbf{5.5--8.7\,kg}, and it is worth naming which items carry it,
because the answer has been \emph{engineered} rather than merely observed. In an early
layout the liquid line dominated the whole inventory --- it is the only line holding dense
subcooled liquid, and at a loose 15\,m it alone would be 6.3\,kg, half the system. So the
plant is deliberately laid out to make that line short: co-locating the condenser and
evaporator holds the liquid line to \textbf{4\,m and 1.69\,kg} (\S\ref{safe:sec:linelength}).

With that done, no single item dominates any more. The largest is now the
\textbf{condenser two-phase holdup (1.7--2.9\,kg)}, followed by the liquid line
(1.69\,kg) and the \emph{oil separator}'s 40\,L of discharge vapour (1.37\,kg). The
suction and discharge lines, being low-density vapour, together hold only
\textbf{0.3\,kg} despite their length --- which is exactly why the layout effort is spent
on the liquid line and not on them.

\noindent Had a \textbf{liquid receiver} been fitted, the inventory would read
\textbf{8.4--14.8\,kg} --- and because the rest of the plant is now so lean, that receiver
would be a \emph{third to a half} of the whole charge
(\S\ref{receiver:sec:charge_cost}), making every EN~378 obligation in Part~B
correspondingly heavier. Omitting it is the single largest charge-reduction decision in
the design; minimizing the liquid line is the second.

\subsection{The Liquid Line: the Lever, and Where It Bottoms Out}
\label{safe:sec:linelength}

The liquid line is the one line whose length matters to charge, at
\textbf{0.42\,kg/m} of dense subcooled liquid --- roughly 25 times the suction line's
contribution per metre. The project brief scopes piping to diameters only
(\S\ref{piping:sec:scope}), so its length is a layout choice, and this design spends that
choice: the condenser and evaporator are placed adjacent so the run is \textbf{4\,m},
which is about the practical floor once isolation valves, bend radii and service
clearance are allowed for. The charge scales almost linearly with it:

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{System charge vs.\ liquid-line length (suction/discharge held at the adopted
4\,/\,5\,m). The 4\,m row is the design; longer rows show what a looser layout would cost.}
\label{tab:charge_sensitivity}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLL}
\toprule
\textbf{Liquid-line length} & \textbf{Total charge} & \textbf{vs.\ the 4\,m design} \\
\midrule
3\,m  & 5.0--8.2\,kg   & $-8$\,\% \\
\textbf{4\,m (adopted)} & \textbf{5.5--8.7\,kg} & baseline \\
5\,m  & 5.9--9.1\,kg   & $+8$\,\% \\
10\,m & 8.0--11.2\,kg  & $+46$\,\% \\
15\,m & 10.1--13.3\,kg & $+84$\,\% \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\noindent The message is the same one, now acted on: for an A3 refrigerant, \textbf{every
metre of liquid line is charge}, and charge is what drives the EN~378 obligations of
Part~B. A careless 15\,m run would have put this plant at 13\,kg; the 4\,m layout keeps it
under 9\,kg. The plant-room drawing must therefore \textbf{hold the condenser, evaporator
and compressor pack tightly together}, and any layout change that lengthens the liquid
line has to be treated as a safety regression, not a convenience.

\zonedivider{Part B \quad---\quad EN 378 Safety Assessment}

\section{Flammability Assessment}
\label{safe:sec:en378}

\input{tables/safety/en378_assessment.tex}

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figures/safety/charge_concentration.pdf}
\caption{Room concentration following a full release of the upper-bound charge, vs.\
room volume, against the EN~378 thresholds.}
\label{fig:charge_conc}
\end{figure}

The assessment is decisive and is worth stating plainly. Diluting a full
8.7\,kg release below R-290's practical limit by room volume alone would require a
\textbf{1{,}084\,m$^3$} plant room; even reaching the bare LFL needs
\textbf{229\,m$^3$}. A realistically sized machinery room for a 150\,kW chiller
($\approx$90\,m$^3$) would reach $\approx$97\,g/m$^3$ --- \textbf{2.5$\times$ the
LFL}. \textbf{Room volume is therefore not a viable safety strategy}, and the
installation cannot rely on dilution.

This conclusion is robust to every charge decision taken in the report: even the
charge-minimized 5.5\,kg lower bound would need a 145\,m$^3$ room to reach the bare LFL,
and a 688\,m$^3$ room to reach the practical limit. \textbf{No plausible plant room
dilutes this charge to safety.} The charge-reduction measures --- omitting the receiver
(Chapter~\ref{ch:receiver}) and the 4\,m liquid line (\S\ref{safe:sec:linelength}) --- cut
every number in Part~B, ventilation from 303 to \textbf{213\,m$^3$/h}, but they do not,
and could not, change the conclusion.

Compliance instead rests on treating the plant room as a \textbf{dedicated
refrigerating machinery room} under EN~378, whose provisions decouple the permitted
charge from room volume:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Refrigerant detection} at or below 20\,\% LFL
        ($\mathbf{7.6\,g/m^3}$), which alarms and starts emergency ventilation
        long before a flammable mixture can form.
  \item \textbf{Emergency mechanical ventilation} of
        $\dot V = 0.014\,m^{2/3} = \mathbf{213\,m^3/h}$ (0.059\,m$^3$/s) per
        EN~378-3 --- a modest fan, and a reminder that \emph{detection plus
        ventilation}, not volume, is what makes A3 installations safe.
  \item \textbf{Ignition-source control} in the machinery room: no open flames, and
        electrical equipment either outside the room or rated for the zone.
\end{itemize}

\section{Charge-Minimisation and A3 Measures Already in the Design}
\label{safe:sec:minimisation}

Several decisions made earlier in this report are, in effect, A3 safety measures.
They are consolidated here (subtask~1i) rather than left scattered:

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{A3 safety measures already embedded in the design.}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLL}
\toprule
\textbf{Measure} & \textbf{What it does} & \textbf{Where} \\
\midrule
\textbf{Double-wall BPHEs} & Prevents an R-290 leak reaching the chilled water and
hence the occupied data hall --- the single most important barrier in the design,
and the reason the plant room is the \emph{only} space with a refrigerant
inventory & \S\ref{ch:evap}, \S\ref{ch:cond} \\
Glycol loop (not direct DX) & The refrigerant never leaves the plant room; the data
hall sees only water & Ch.~\ref{ch:overview} \\
Semi-hermetic compressor & No external shaft seal --- removes a leak path an
open-drive machine would have & \S9.2 \\
Float-valve oil return (not solenoid) & Avoids an electrical ignition source in the
refrigerant-wetted oil path & \S\ref{oilsep:sec:oilreturn} \\
Relief discharge routed outdoors & A relieving vessel vents flammable gas to a safe
outdoor location, never into the plant room & \S\ref{ch:oilsep} \\
Pressure vessels rated 28--45\,bar & All clear the 19.07\,bar 55\,\textdegree C
standstill pressure with large margin & \S\ref{ch:evap}, \S\ref{ch:oilsep} \\
\midrule
\textbf{No liquid receiver} & Removes 2.97--6.16\,kg (a third to a half of the charge)
and two more joints --- the largest single charge reduction, and it \emph{improves} the
COP rather than costing it & Ch.~\ref{ch:receiver} \\
\textbf{Short (4\,m) liquid line} & Condenser and evaporator co-located so the only
liquid-holding line is 4\,m, not a loose 15\,m --- keeps the system under 9\,kg instead
of over 13\,kg & \S\ref{safe:sec:linelength} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\section{Limitations and Open Items}
\label{safe:sec:limitations}

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{The inventory is an engineering estimate, not a commissioning
        charge.} Void fractions in the two-phase exchangers are bounded rather than
        modelled, and the 6.4\,kg spread between low and high bounds reflects that
        honestly. The real charge is established by weighing at commissioning ---
        which is itself still an outstanding deliverable (subtask~2).
  \item \textbf{The 15\,m line lengths are assumed}, and the charge scales nearly
        linearly with the liquid line (\S\ref{safe:sec:linelength}). This is the
        dominant uncertainty in the whole chapter and should be closed with a real
        layout before the receiver is procured.
  \item \textbf{The 90\,m$^3$ machinery room is assumed}, not specified: the brief
        gives the data hall footprint (8\,$\times$\,12\,m) but not the plant room.
        The EN~378 conclusion is insensitive to it --- no plausible plant room
        dilutes this charge below the LFL --- but the ventilation design needs the
        real geometry.
  \item \textbf{Ventilation is sized by the EN~378-3 emergency formula only.} A
        full design must also check normal ventilation, the detector count and
        placement (R-290 is heavier than air and pools low), and the airflow path.
  \item \textbf{No formal risk assessment or zone classification} (e.g.\ ATEX) has
        been carried out; the ignition-source control above states intent, not a
        classified drawing.
\end{enumerate}

\section{References}

\begin{enumerate}
  \item EN 378-1, \textit{Refrigerating systems and heat pumps --- Safety and
        environmental requirements. Part 1: Basic requirements, definitions,
        classification and selection criteria} --- LFL, practical limits, charge
        limits by occupancy.
  \item EN 378-3, \textit{Part 3: Installation site and personal protection} ---
        machinery-room requirements, emergency ventilation
        ($\dot V = 0.014\,m^{2/3}$), detection.
  \item IEC 60335-2-40, \textit{Particular requirements for electrical heat pumps,
        air-conditioners and dehumidifiers} --- appliance-level A3 charge limits.
  \item ASHRAE 15, \textit{Safety Standard for Refrigeration Systems} ---
        machinery-room and standstill-pressure basis.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Consolidated Equipment List}
\label{bom:sec:list}

Table~\ref{tab:bom} consolidates every major component and accessory selected across the
preceding chapters into a single parts list (project subtask~1g). Each line carries the
selected vendor model (or, where no vendor selection was made, the specification and its
placeholder status), the quantity implied by the dual 75\,kW parallel-circuit

selection is developed and justified.

\input{tables/bom/bom.tex}

\noindent \textbf{Not yet vendor-selected} (carried as placeholders, flagged in their own
chapters): the CRAH/in-row units (item~15, Chapter~\ref{ch:chwpump}) and the expansion
