% ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
\chapter{Automatic Control of the Compressor--Expansion Device Interaction}
\label{ch:controls}
% ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

\section{Scope: Why This Is a Discussion, Not a Simulation}
\label{ctrl:sec:scope}

The brief asks for the ``\textbf{consideration of} automatic control of compressor
--- expansion device interaction'' (subtask~3b). The verb is deliberate: section~3
asks for consideration, where section~1 asks for ``calculation and/or selection''.

That framing matches the physics. Control interaction is inherently
\textbf{transient} --- it lives in the mass and energy storage of the evaporator, the
valve's actuator dynamics, and the tuning of two feedback loops. Every model in this
report is \textbf{steady-state}. A credible dynamic study would need a different
model entirely, and inventing one would produce numbers with no more authority than
the discussion below. It is therefore not attempted, and that boundary is stated
rather than blurred.

What the steady-state model \emph{can} settle, it does: the valve's control
authority across the real operating envelope (\S\ref{ctrl:sec:authority}), which is a
genuine sizing question with a genuine finding.

\section{Control Architecture}
\label{ctrl:sec:architecture}

Two loops act on the same evaporator, and the entire subject of this chapter is that
they are not independent.

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{The two interacting refrigerant-side control loops.}
\label{tab:loops}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLLL}
\toprule
& \textbf{Compressor (VFD $+$ staging)} & \textbf{EEV} \\
\midrule
\textbf{Role} & \textbf{Master} --- sets capacity & \textbf{Slave} --- sets superheat \\
Controlled variable & Leaving chilled-water temperature (15\,\textdegree C setpoint) & Evaporator outlet superheat (8\,K setpoint) \\
Manipulated variable & Shaft speed; number of units running & Valve orifice opening \\
Physical effect & Refrigerant mass flow, hence duty & Split of the evaporator between boiling and superheat \\
Timescale & Slow (thermal mass of the CHW loop) & Fast (evaporator refrigerant-side dynamics) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\noindent The plant-level supervisory logic sits above both: it selects
\textbf{mechanical} or \textbf{free-cooling} mode on outdoor air temperature
(Chapter~\ref{ch:dry}, \S\ref{dry:sec:annualregime}), and in free cooling the
compressors and the EEV are simply off --- the refrigerant loop is idle and this
chapter's subject does not arise for $\approx$29\,\% of the year.

\section{The Interaction}
\label{ctrl:sec:interaction}

The loops are coupled because \textbf{both act on the evaporator's refrigerant
inventory}, from opposite ends. The compressor withdraws vapour from it; the EEV
admits liquid into it. Superheat is the observable that reports the balance between
the two.

A capacity change propagates as follows:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item Cooling demand rises; the master loop raises compressor speed.
  \item Higher swept volume withdraws vapour faster than the EEV is feeding liquid,
        so \textbf{suction pressure falls} and the evaporator's liquid inventory
        starts to deplete.
  \item The boiling zone shortens, the superheat zone lengthens, and the measured
        \textbf{superheat rises}.
  \item The slave loop sees the superheat error and \textbf{opens the EEV}, restoring
        liquid feed until superheat returns to setpoint.
\end{enumerate}

\noindent The compressor therefore acts as a \textbf{disturbance} to the superheat
loop, and the EEV as a disturbance to the evaporating pressure the compressor is
working against. Neither loop can be tuned in isolation.

\subsection{The Two Failure Modes}

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Hunting.} If the EEV loop is tuned too aggressively --- or too close
        in bandwidth to the compressor loop --- the two oscillate against each other:
        the valve overshoots, superheat undershoots, the valve closes, superheat
        spikes, and the cycle repeats. Symptoms are oscillating suction pressure and
        capacity, and it is the more common of the two faults.
  \item \textbf{Floodback.} If the EEV loop is too slow, or the superheat setpoint is
        too low, a fast capacity reduction leaves the evaporator over-fed and
        \textbf{liquid refrigerant reaches the compressor suction}. For a
        semi-hermetic reciprocating machine this is the destructive failure ---
        liquid is incompressible, and slugging damages valves and bearings.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Why This Design Is More Exposed Than Most}
\label{ctrl:sec:exposure}

Two decisions taken elsewhere in this report make the interaction harder here, and
both are consequences worth owning explicitly:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{The VFD makes the disturbance continuous.} A fixed-speed bank
        disturbs the superheat loop only at discrete stage changes, which are
        infrequent and predictable. The VFD adopted for efficiency compliance
        (\S\ref{perf:sec:revision}) modulates continuously, so the superheat loop is
        \emph{always} tracking a moving target. The efficiency gain is real, but it
        buys a continuously-coupled control problem.
  \item \textbf{There is no liquid receiver to absorb an excursion.} The receiver was
        omitted (Chapter~\ref{ch:receiver}) because it would have broken AHRI
        compliance and added $\approx$25\,\% charge --- decisions this report stands
        behind. But a receiver is also a buffer: it damps inventory swings between
        the condenser and evaporator. Without one the system is \textbf{critically
        charged}, and a superheat excursion has nothing to absorb it.
        \textbf{Floodback protection therefore rests entirely on the control system},
        not on hardware forgiveness.
\end{enumerate}

\section{EEV Control Authority Across the Envelope}
\label{ctrl:sec:authority}

This is the part the steady-state model settles. The valve must modulate across the
project's full \textbf{5:1 turndown} (150\,kW design to the 30\,kW minimum IT load),
and at each load the condenser-entering-air temperature is lower, which lowers $t_c$
and therefore the pressure drop across the valve. Both effects shrink the required
opening, and they compound.

\input{tables/controls/eev_authority.tex}

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figures/controls/eev_authority.pdf}
\caption{EEV opening required across the 5:1 load turndown, for both candidate
circuit architectures.}
\label{fig:eev_authority}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Result: the Single-Circuit Architecture Is Confirmed}
\label{ctrl:sec:architecture_resolved}

This analysis settled an architecture question the report had previously left
inconsistent. Chapter~\ref{ch:comp} once described ``two parallel refrigerant
\emph{circuits}, each with its own \dots\ EEV'', while \texttt{main.py} had always
modelled a \textbf{single shared circuit} --- one condenser, one evaporator, one
expansion valve, fed by two compressors on a common manifold.

\textbf{The single-circuit architecture is adopted}, and the valve authority is one of
the reasons why. Table~\ref{tab:eev_authority} evaluates both readings:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{One EEV on the full flow (adopted)}: \textbf{82.6\,\% open at design,
        falling to 24.3\,\%} at the 30\,kW minimum load. Comfortable authority across
        the whole 5:1 turndown, and the E3V65 is well matched to the duty.
  \item \textbf{Two EEVs on split flow (rejected)}: only 41.3\,\% open at design ---
        the E3V65 would be \textbf{oversized} for half the flow --- falling to
        \textbf{12.2\,\%} at minimum load, \emph{below} the $\approx$15\,\% floor at
        which an EEV's characteristic goes non-linear and stepper resolution and
        stiction begin to dominate. Superheat control would degrade exactly where
        floodback protection matters most, and where there is no receiver to forgive
        it (\S\ref{ctrl:sec:exposure}).
\end{itemize}

\noindent The rejected column is retained deliberately: it shows that the
single-circuit choice is not merely a modelling convenience but the arrangement that
keeps the expansion valve inside its controllable range at minimum load. Had the
two-circuit reading been adopted, the E3V65 would have had to be replaced with a
smaller (E3V45-class) valve to move half-flow nearer the middle of its range.

\textbf{Consequences of the confirmed architecture}, now applied throughout the
report:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{One EEV}, not two --- the BOM quantity is corrected accordingly.
  \item \textbf{Compressor redundancy only.} Two parallel compressors protect against
        a compressor failure, but a fault in the single condenser, evaporator or
        expansion valve stops the plant. The redundancy claim is restated on that
        narrower basis (\S\ref{pc:sec:cons}).
  \item \textbf{Fewer components and less charge} than two independent circuits ---
        consistent with the A3 charge-minimisation objective
        (\S\ref{safe:sec:minimisation}).
\end{itemize}

\section{The Superheat Setpoint --- and a Trap}
\label{ctrl:sec:superheat}

It is tempting to select the superheat setpoint from the compressor map. That is a
mistake, and the mistake is instructive.

\input{tables/controls/superheat_sensitivity.tex}

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.92\linewidth]{figures/controls/superheat_sensitivity.pdf}
\caption{What the compressor map says about the superheat setpoint at fixed $t_o$ ---
and why the trend is an artifact rather than a design lever.}
\label{fig:superheat_sens}
\end{figure}

Read naively, the map says \textbf{more superheat is better}: COP rises from 4.55 at
4\,K to 4.88 at 15\,K, a 6.8\,\% span that is far from negligible. \textbf{This is an
artifact.} The AHRI-540/EN12900 convention credits \emph{all} superheat as
\emph{useful} --- it assumes the superheat is picked up inside the evaporator, so it
adds to the refrigerating effect $h_1 - h_3$. Holding $t_o$ fixed and raising the
setpoint therefore raises $h_1$, and COP follows.

Physically the evaporator's area is finite and the trend reverses. From the
evaporator's T--Q profile (Figure~\ref{fig:hxprofiles}), boiling occupies
$\approx$95\,\% of the duty and the superheat tail only $\approx$5\,\% --- but the
superheat zone transfers heat to \emph{gas}, with a far poorer film coefficient, so
it consumes area disproportionately. Demanding more superheat at fixed area
\textbf{steals area from boiling}, which forces $t_o$ down, and a lower $t_o$ costs
more COP than the higher $h_1$ gains. Capturing this properly requires an
area-constrained evaporator solve, which this report does not perform
(\S\ref{ctrl:sec:openitems}).

\noindent \textbf{The correct conclusion is the opposite of the chart:} superheat
should be set \emph{as low as is safely controllable}, not high. It is a
\textbf{compressor-protection setpoint}, not an efficiency knob. The 8\,K design
value (Chapter~\ref{ch:comp}) is retained: it matches the compressor vendor's own
selection basis, and it leaves margin above the floodback boundary for a system with
no receiver to forgive an excursion.

\section{Recommended Control Measures}
\label{ctrl:sec:measures}

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Recommended measures for stable compressor--EEV interaction.}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLL}
\toprule
\textbf{Measure} & \textbf{Purpose} & \textbf{Why here} \\
\midrule
\textbf{Timescale separation} --- EEV loop tuned 5--10$\times$ faster than the
compressor capacity loop & Lets the slave reject the master's disturbance before it
propagates & The standard defence against hunting; the VFD makes it essential
(\S\ref{ctrl:sec:exposure}) \\
\textbf{Compressor ramp-rate limit} & Bounds how fast the disturbance can arrive &
Keeps the superheat loop inside its tracking capability \\
\textbf{Minimum-superheat clamp} & Hard floor below which the EEV closes regardless
of the loop & Last-line floodback protection --- load-bearing here, as there is no
receiver \\
\textbf{MOP (maximum operating pressure) limit} & Caps evaporating pressure during
pull-down & Protects the compressor motor from overload on a warm start \\
\textbf{Suction superheat sensing near the evaporator outlet} & Minimises transport
lag in the feedback path & Lag is what turns an aggressive loop into an oscillating one \\
\textbf{Staging hysteresis / minimum run time} & Prevents short-cycling at stage
boundaries & The bank stages at $\approx$50\,\% load (\S\ref{comp:sec:staging}) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\section{Open Items}
\label{ctrl:sec:openitems}

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{EEV vendor confirmation.} With the single-circuit architecture
        confirmed (\S\ref{ctrl:sec:architecture_resolved}), the E3V65 sits at
        82.6\,\% open at design and 24.3\,\% at minimum load --- comfortable, but
        computed from the incompressible-orifice $K_v$ estimate of
        Chapter~\ref{ch:eev}, which is explicitly a shortlisting figure rather than a
        vendor two-phase capacity rating (\S\ref{eev:sec:whyfirstcut}). Confirm the
        opening range against the manufacturer's own capacity table before ordering.
  \item \textbf{No dynamic model, therefore no tuning constants.} This chapter gives
        the architecture, the failure modes and the measures, but not PID gains or a
        stability proof. Those need a transient model and, ultimately, commissioning
        on the real plant (subtask~2).
  \item \textbf{The superheat/evaporator-area coupling is argued, not computed}
        (\S\ref{ctrl:sec:superheat}). The claim that the real trend opposes the map's
        rests on the T--Q profile and the poor gas-side coefficient, which is sound
        but qualitative. An area-constrained evaporator solve would settle it.
  \item \textbf{Critical-charge sensitivity is unquantified}
        (\S\ref{receiver:sec:limitations}), and it interacts directly with this
        chapter: with no receiver, a charge error shows up as a superheat control
        problem.
  \item \textbf{Free-cooling transitions are not analysed.} Mode switches at the
        4\,\textdegree C threshold start and stop the compressors; the associated
        refrigerant migration and restart superheat behaviour are not modelled.
\end{enumerate}


% ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
\chapter{System Assessment: Pros and Cons}
\label{ch:proscons}
% ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

\section{What Was Chosen, and What Was Prescribed}
\label{pc:sec:framing}

An honest assessment has to separate the decisions this design \emph{made} from the
ones the brief \emph{imposed}. The brief prescribes water to the CRAH units, and
\textbf{heat rejection via a dry cooler with an intermediate water/propylene-glycol
loop}. That single prescription determines most of what follows --- including the
design's central weakness --- so it is not fair to score it as a free choice. What
was genuinely chosen: the refrigerant, the compressor arrangement and staging, the
approach temperatures, the VFD, and the omission of the liquid receiver.

\section{Pros}
\label{pc:sec:pros}

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Strengths of the selected system.}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLL}
\toprule
\textbf{Strength} & \textbf{Evidence} & \textbf{Why it matters} \\
\midrule
\textbf{Free cooling dominates the annual picture} & 2{,}542\,h (29\,\%) full free
cooling, 1{,}358\,h (15.5\,\%) partial --- \textbf{44.5\,\% of the year}
(Fig.~\ref{fig:duration}). Winter PUE $\approx$1.01, total-system COP in the high
tens & The strongest feature of the design, and the payoff that justifies the glycol
loop's efficiency cost. In Champaign's climate zone 5A this is worth far more than
the design-day penalty \\
\textbf{Very low GWP with a mature ecosystem} & R-290, GWP\,3 against a $<$150 limit
--- a $50\times$ margin (Ch.~\ref{ch:refrigerant}) & Not merely compliant but
future-proof against tightening regulation, and every pressure vessel and the
compressor were available A3-rated from a single vendor \\
\textbf{Warm chilled water exploited} & 15\,\textdegree C LCHW (ASHRAE W17) gives
design COP 4.67 against 3.53 at the AHRI 6.7\,\textdegree C rating point
(\S\ref{perf:sec:fullload}) & The design is tuned for the condition it actually runs
at, not the rating condition. Data-center CHW envelopes reward this \\
\textbf{Compressor redundancy} & Two 75\,kW compressors on a common manifold; losing
one leaves 50\,\% capacity (\S9.1). \textbf{Compressors only} --- the shared circuit's
exchangers and EEV are unduplicated & For 24/7/365 IT load, partial failure beats total
failure, and the compressor is the likeliest thing to fail. But this is not full
N$+$1: see \S\ref{imp:sec:architecture} \\
\textbf{Genuine 5:1 turndown} & VFD $+$ staging covers 150\,kW to the 30\,kW minimum
without hot-gas bypass (\S\ref{perf:sec:iplv}) & Resolves the turndown floor the
fixed-speed design could not, and lifts IPLV.IP from 4.43 to 5.23 \\
\textbf{Low refrigerant charge} & 5.5--8.7\,kg, no receiver
(Ch.~\ref{ch:receiver}) & For an A3 fluid, charge is the safety currency. Omitting the
receiver \emph{and} co-locating the exchangers for a 4\,m liquid line together nearly
halve the charge, receiver removal also improving the COP \\
\textbf{Refrigerant never leaves the plant room} & Double-wall BPHEs plus the glycol
loop (\S\ref{safe:sec:minimisation}) & The occupied data hall sees only water. This is
the design's most important safety property, and it is a \emph{consequence} of the
prescribed architecture \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\section{Cons}
\label{pc:sec:cons}

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Weaknesses of the selected system.}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLL}
\toprule
\textbf{Weakness} & \textbf{Evidence} & \textbf{Assessment} \\
\midrule
\textbf{The glycol loop costs three stacked approaches} & Dry cooler $+$ glycol rise
$+$ condenser $=$ 10\,K air-to-refrigerant even with all three driven as tight as the
hardware allows, against $\approx$1 approach for a direct air-cooled condenser
(Fig.~\ref{fig:cascade}) &
\textbf{The design's central weakness --- and it is inherited, not chosen.} It is why
the COP target (drawn from an \emph{air-cooled chiller} table) is structurally harder to
hit here, and why compliance costs a 1{,}949\,m$^2$ dry cooler \\
\textbf{Thin AHRI compliance margin} & 3.53 against $\geq$3.5 --- $\approx$1\,\%
(\S\ref{perf:sec:openitems}) & Contingent on the design approaches surviving vendor
re-selection, and on assumed $U$ values (\S\ref{perf:sec:uvalues}). \textbf{The most
fragile claim in the report} \\
\textbf{A3 flammability drives real obligations} & No plausible plant room dilutes the
charge below LFL; compliance rests on detection at 7.6\,g/m$^3$ $+$ 213\,m$^3$/h
ventilation (\S\ref{safe:sec:en378}) & A genuine cost of R-290 --- the price paid for
GWP\,3. Manageable and well-precedented, but not free \\
\textbf{Critical charge, no buffer} & No receiver, so charge must be correct rather
than sufficient (\S\ref{receiver:sec:consequences}) & Accepted deliberately: the
receiver's cost (COP and charge) exceeded its benefit. But it removes hardware
forgiveness and pushes floodback protection entirely onto controls
(\S\ref{ctrl:sec:exposure}) \\
\textbf{Continuously-coupled control problem} & VFD modulates continuously, so the
superheat loop always tracks a moving target (\S\ref{ctrl:sec:interaction}) & The
price of the efficiency revision. Standard measures exist, but tuning is now on the
critical path \\
\textbf{Dry cooler is the capital burden} & A 1{,}949\,m$^2$ coil, $UA \approx
73.7$\,kW/K, forced by the 3\,K approach the COP target needs
(\S\ref{dry:sec:capacity_trap}) & The design's dominant cost, concentrated in one
component. Large footprint, six fans \\
\textbf{Two exchangers where one would do} & Every kW crosses refrigerant$\to$glycol
$\to$air, each with its own $\Delta T$, pumping power and cost & Inherent to the
prescribed architecture. The glycol pump alone is 2.57\,kW of parasitic load \\
\textbf{Higher parasitic pumping} & Two pumped loops (glycol $+$ CHW) $=$ 3.68\,kW,
8\,\% of the non-IT load (Fig.~\ref{fig:pue_breakdown}) & Modest against the
compressor's 69\,\%, but a direct-DX architecture would not have the glycol loop at all \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\section{The Central Trade, Stated Plainly}
\label{pc:sec:verdict}

The glycol loop is simultaneously the design's greatest weakness and the enabler of
its greatest strength, and the two cannot be separated:

\begin{itemize}
  \item It \textbf{costs} $\approx$7\,K of extra condensing approach against a direct
        air-cooled condenser --- worth roughly 20\,\% of the full-load COP, and the
        entire reason the efficiency revision (larger dry cooler, more plates, VFDs)
        was necessary at all.
  \item It \textbf{buys} the waterside economizer, and with it \textbf{40.1\,\% of the
        year's cooling energy delivered without meaningful compressor work} --- 29\,\% of
        hours fully free at PUE\,1.01 and a further 15.5\,\% integrated at PUE\,$\approx$1.03
        (\S\ref{perf:sec:seasonal}) --- plus the ability to keep an A3 refrigerant entirely
        out of the data hall.
\end{itemize}

\noindent \textbf{In Champaign's climate this trade is clearly worth making.} The
design-day penalty is paid on a handful of hours a year (Fig.~\ref{fig:duration});
the free-cooling benefit is collected on thousands. A direct air-cooled DX chiller
would post a better AHRI number and a worse annual one --- which is a fair summary of
why standardized full-load ratings flatter architectures that this application does
not want.

The honest caveat: \textbf{the compliance metrics are met, but not comfortably.} The
1\,\% AHRI margin rests on vendor confirmations that have not yet come back
(\S\ref{perf:sec:uvalues}, \S\ref{dry:sec:fanpower_open}). The design is sound; the
numbers are not yet safe.


% ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
\chapter{Suggestions for Improvement}
\label{ch:improve}
% ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

\section{Priority 1 --- Close the Open Vendor Confirmations}
\label{imp:sec:vendor}

These are not improvements so much as \textbf{debts}: the compliance case currently
rests on them, and none is expensive to close.

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Outstanding vendor confirmations, in order of how much rests on them.}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLL}
\toprule
\textbf{Item} & \textbf{What is assumed} & \textbf{What rests on it} \\
\midrule
\textbf{BPHE double-wall $U$ derate} & $U = 2{,}400$ (evap) / 2{,}000 (cond), a 20\,\%
DW derate estimated from a 10--30\,\% literature range
(\S\ref{perf:sec:uvalues}) & Plate counts, and hence whether both exchangers stay in
the 120-plate frame. Ask Kelvion Select PHE for the DW derate \emph{relative to the
equivalent single-wall plate} \\
\textbf{Dry cooler fan power} & 10.6\,kW, fan-law scaled --- Select RT does not report
it (\S\ref{dry:sec:fanpower_open}) & The design-day PUE. \textbf{The single largest
uncertainty in that figure} \\
\textbf{Glycol pump duty point} & Ch.~\ref{ch:pump}'s system curve predates the
revised flow and the re-selected dry cooler's 8\,psi
(\S\ref{perf:sec:pue}) & Pump frame selection; 2AD was already near its envelope top,
2.5AC now likely \\
\textbf{CRAH coil $\Delta P$} & 50\,kPa placeholder --- no unit ever selected
(\S\ref{chwpump:sec:crah}) & CHW pump selection. The only major component with no
vendor basis at all \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\section{Priority 2 --- Consider Circuit Redundancy Against the Single Point of Failure}
\label{imp:sec:architecture}

The circuit architecture is now \textbf{settled: one shared refrigerant circuit} fed
by two parallel compressors (\S\ref{ctrl:sec:architecture_resolved}). That choice is
right on the merits --- it keeps the EEV inside its controllable range at minimum load
(24.3\,\% open, against 12.2\,\% and unworkable on a split-flow arrangement), and it
means fewer components and less charge, which an A3 system wants.

It does, however, leave an honest weakness worth naming: \textbf{the shared circuit is
a single point of failure}. Two parallel compressors protect against a compressor
fault, but the condenser, the evaporator and the expansion valve are each unduplicated
--- a fault in any of them stops all 150\,kW of cooling. For a facility whose IT load
runs 24/7/365, that is a real exposure, and the redundancy the design claims
(\S\ref{pc:sec:pros}) is narrower than a casual reading suggests.

Options, none free:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Accept it} and manage the risk operationally (spares held, service
        contract). Reasonable for an edge facility, and the status quo.
  \item \textbf{Two fully independent circuits} --- restores graceful degradation to
        50\,\% on any single fault, at the cost of duplicated exchangers, more charge,
        higher capital, and a smaller EEV to keep authority at part load.
  \item \textbf{Duplicate only the cheapest single points} (e.g.\ a parallel EEV with
        isolation valves), which buys partial coverage for modest cost.
\end{itemize}

\noindent Recommendation: \textbf{accept the single circuit}, but state the exposure
explicitly to the client. The strengths table (\S\ref{pc:sec:pros}) is worded to that
standard --- ``compressor redundancy'', not ``N$+$1'' --- and any client-facing summary
should keep it that way.

\section{Priority 3 --- Protect the Short Liquid Line at Layout Stage}
\label{imp:sec:liquidline}

The design already takes the liquid line to \textbf{4\,m} by co-locating the condenser
and evaporator, which is most of why the charge is 5.5--8.7\,kg rather than over 13\,kg
(\S\ref{safe:sec:linelength}). \textbf{The risk is that this is undone on the drawing
board}: a real plant-room layout, routed for pipefitter convenience or to clear other
equipment, can silently stretch that run and add charge back. For an A3 refrigerant that
is a safety regression, not a detailing choice. The recommendation is therefore to carry
the 4\,m liquid line as a \textbf{fixed constraint into the P\&ID and the plant-room
general arrangement}, and to re-run the charge and EN~378 numbers if it cannot be held.

\section{Priority 4 --- Float the Economizer Changeover}
\label{imp:sec:floating}

\textbf{This is the best return per dollar in the whole report: it is a control-logic
change with no hardware attached.}

The design follows the project's stated rule --- activate the economizer at
OAT $\leq 10$\,\textdegree C --- and Figure~\ref{fig:econ_band} shows what that rule
costs. At 10\,\textdegree C the economizer is delivering \textbf{75\,kW, half the design
load}, and then the changeover switches it off. Compressor power jumps from 6.4 to
13.0\,kW across a threshold the ambient does not notice. That step is not physics; it is
an artefact of a fixed changeover temperature.

The economizer stops being useful only when the glycol it is fed is no longer colder
than the chilled water returning from the hall. With glycol at OAT $+\,5$\,K and a
21\,\textdegree C return, that crossover is at \textbf{OAT $= 16$\,\textdegree C}, not
10\,\textdegree C:

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Economizer duty above the fixed 10\,\textdegree C changeover, rated with the same
$\varepsilon$--NTU model (\S\ref{econ:sec:partialmodel}). This capacity exists and is
currently discarded.}
\label{tab:econ_above10}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLL}
\toprule
\textbf{OAT [\textdegree C]} & \textbf{Glycol supply [\textdegree C]} & \textbf{Economizer duty} \\
\midrule
10 & 15 & 75.0\,kW (50\,\% of load) --- \emph{switched off here} \\
11 & 16 & 62.5\,kW (41.7\,\%) \\
12 & 17 & 50.0\,kW (33.3\,\%) \\
13 & 18 & 37.5\,kW (25.0\,\%) \\
14 & 19 & 25.0\,kW (16.7\,\%) \\
15 & 20 & 12.5\,kW (8.3\,\%) \\
16 & 21 & 0\,kW --- true crossover \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\noindent Champaign spends \textbf{1{,}568\,h/yr (17.9\,\% of the year)} between 10 and
16\,\textdegree C. Every one of those hours currently runs the chiller at full 150\,kW
duty while an already-purchased exchanger sits idle with 0--50\,kW of free cooling
available.

\textbf{Recommendation:} replace the fixed 10\,\textdegree C rule with a floating
changeover --- enable the economizer whenever the available glycol supply is below the
CHW return by some useful margin (say 2\,K), and let the chiller trim whatever is left.
This \emph{exceeds} the ASHRAE~90.1~\S6.5.1 requirement rather than conflicting with it:
90.1 mandates activation \emph{by} 10\,\textdegree C, and a floating changeover activates
earlier. The economizer, the valves and the piping are all already in the BOM; only the
control sequence changes. The one real cost is more hours of low-load chiller operation,
where the machine cycles rather than modulates (\S\ref{econ:sec:partialmodel}) --- so the
sequence should carry a minimum-runtime guard.

\section{Priority 5 --- Efficiency Headroom}
\label{imp:sec:efficiency}

The targets are met, but not comfortably --- the full-load COP clears by 0.8\,\%, worth
about 0.3\,K of condensing temperature (\S\ref{perf:sec:sensitivity}). If more margin is
wanted, these are the levers, in descending order of effect:

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Further efficiency measures, with their costs.}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lLL}
\toprule
\textbf{Measure} & \textbf{Effect} & \textbf{Cost} \\
\midrule
Reduce the condensing approach further (10\,K $\to$ 7\,K) & AHRI full-load COP
$\approx$3.48 $\to$ 3.7$+$; lifts every IPLV point
(\S\ref{perf:sec:sensitivity}) & A larger dry cooler again --- already the design's
dominant capital item \\
\textbf{Float the economizer changeover} instead of fixing it at
10\,\textdegree C (\S\ref{imp:sec:floating}) & A further 1{,}568\,h/yr
(17.9\,\% of the year) in which the economizer can still carry 50\,kW down to
0\,kW of the load but is switched off & \textbf{Controls only --- no hardware.}
The cheapest improvement now available \\
Increase subcooling beyond 5\,K & $+$6\,\% COP at 10\,K, $+$11\,\% at 15\,K & More
condenser area; diminishing returns; interacts with the charge inventory \\
Economised / vapour-injection cycle & Improves COP at high lift & Rejected: adds
complexity and charge for a benefit that the modest 2.41 pressure ratio does not
justify. Noted here for completeness, as subtask~1d asks \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\section{Priority 6 --- Modelling Fidelity}
\label{imp:sec:modelling}

Ranked by how much the current simplification could mislead:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Glycol-loop hydraulics in the partial band}, where the economizer and the
        condenser are both served by one loop and one dry cooler. The loop's hot return is
        closed on an energy balance at the free-cooling flow rather than resolved as two
        branches; and cycling losses below the drive's minimum speed (679\,h/yr) are not
        modelled (\S\ref{econ:sec:partialmodel}). Both are bounded by the few kW of
        compressor duty in that band.
  \item \textbf{Fixed approach temperatures across all ambients.} $t_c = T_{air} + 10$
        and the glycol approaches are held constant rather than re-solving the dry
        cooler's $\varepsilon$-NTU model at each hour. Cheap and defensible, but it
        does not capture the fan-speed/approach trade at part load.
  \item \textbf{Area-constrained evaporator solve}, which would settle the superheat
        argument of \S\ref{ctrl:sec:superheat} rather than leaving it qualitative.
  \item \textbf{Han correlation applied to R-290.} Regressed on R-410A and R-22;
        transfer to propane is accepted practice but unvalidated
        (\S2.9), and it underpins every refrigerant-side pressure drop.
  \item \textbf{Dynamic model} for the control interaction (\S\ref{ctrl:sec:openitems}).
\end{enumerate}

\section{Priority 7 --- Deliverables Still Outstanding}
\label{imp:sec:deliverables}

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Piping \& instrumentation diagram} (subtask~1h) --- the last unresolved
        cross-reference in this report.
  \item \textbf{Commissioning procedure} (subtask~2) --- now carrying real weight:
        with a critically-charged, receiver-less system, \textbf{charge weighing and
        the 5\,K subcooling check are acceptance criteria}, not routine steps
        (\S\ref{receiver:sec:consequences}).
  \item \textbf{Specification sheet extracts}, which the brief requires be attached.
\end{itemize}
